Counting Homomorphisms from Hypergraphs of Bounded Generalised Hypertree Width: A Logical Characterisation This is the extended version of the conference contribution [ 23 ] .
Benjamin Scheidt, Nicole Schweikardt

Abstract
We introduce the 2-sorted counting logic GC k \textsf{{GC}}^{{k}} that expresses properties of hypergraphs. This logic has available k k variables to address hyperedges, an unbounded number of variables to address vertices of a hypergraph, and atomic formulas E ⁡ ( e , v ) E(e,v) to express that vertex v v is contained in hyperedge e e . We show that two hypergraphs H , H ′ H,H^{\prime} satisfy the same sentences of the logic GC k \textsf{{GC}}^{{k}} if, and only if, they are homomorphism indistinguishable over the class of hypergraphs of generalised hypertree width at most k k . Here, H , H ′ H,H^{\prime} are called homomorphism indistinguishable over a class 𝒞 \mathcal{C} if for every hypergraph G ∈ 𝒞 G\in\mathcal{C} the number of homomorphisms from G G to H H equals the number of homomorphisms from G G to H ′ H^{\prime} . This result can be viewed as a lifting (from graphs to hypergraphs) of a result by Dvořák (2010) stating that any two (undirected, simple, finite) graphs H , H ′ H,H^{\prime} are indistinguishable by the k + 1 k{+}1 -variable counting logic C k + 1 C^{k+1} if, and only if, they are homomorphism indistinguishable over the class of graphs of tree-width at most k k .



1
Introduction

Counting homomorphisms from a given class
\mathcal{C}
of graphs induces a similarity measure between graphs:
Consider an arbitrary graph
H
.
The results of the homomorphism counts for all
G\in\mathcal{C}
in
H
can be represented by
a mapping (or, “vector”)
\textsf{{HOM}}_{\mathcal{C}}(H)
that associates
with every
G\in\mathcal{C}
the number
\operatorname{hom}(G,H)
of homomorphisms from

G
to
H
.
A similarity measure for the mappings
\textsf{{HOM}}_{\mathcal{C}}(H)
and
\textsf{{HOM}}_{\mathcal{C}}(H^{\prime})
can then
be viewed as a similarity measure of two given graphs
H
and
H^{\prime}
.
An overview of this approach, its relations to
graph neural
networks
, and its usability as a similarity measure of graphs can
be found in Grohe’s survey
[
18
]
.

Two graphs
H,H^{\prime}
are viewed as “equivalent” (or,

indistinguishable
)
over
\mathcal{C}
if

\textsf{{HOM}}_{\mathcal{C}}(H)=\textsf{{HOM}}_{\mathcal{C}}(H^{\prime})
, i.e.,
for every graph
G
in
\mathcal{C}
the number of homomorphisms from
G
to
H
equals the number of homomorphisms from
G
to
H^{\prime}
.

A classical result by Lovász
[
20
]
shows that two graphs

H
and
H^{\prime}
are indistinguishable over the class of
all
graphs
if, and only if, they are isomorphic.
This inspired a lot of research in recent years, examining the notion
of
homomorphism indistinguishability over a class
\mathcal{C}
for
various classes
\mathcal{C}

[
12
,
11
,
6
,
5
,
7
,
17
,
22
]
.

In particular, Grohe
[
17
]
proved that two graphs are
homomorphism indistinguishable over the class of graphs of

tree-depth at most
k
if, and only if, they are
indistinguishable by sentences of first-order counting logic
C
of
quantifier-rank at most
k
(
C
is the extension of first-order logic
with counting quantifiers of the form
\exists^{\geqslant n}x
meaning
“there exist at least
n
elements
x
”).

A decade earlier,
Dvořák
[
12
]
proved that two graphs are homomorphism
indistinguishable over the class of graphs of
tree-width at most

k
if, and only if, they are indistinguishable by sentences of the

k{+}1
-variable fragment
C^{k+1}
of
C
.
From Cai, Fürer and Immerman
[
8
]
we know that this precisely coincides with
indistinguishability by the
k
-dimensional Weisfeiler-Leman algorithm.

An obvious question is if and how these kinds of results can be lifted from graphs to
hypergraphs.

A first answer to this question was given by Böker in
[
7
]
: He introduces a
new version of a
color refinement
algorithm on hypergraphs and proves that two
hypergraphs
H
and
H^{\prime}
cannot be distinguished by this algorithm if, and only if, they are
homomorphism indistinguishable over the class
of
Berge-acyclic
hypergraphs.
This can be viewed as a lifting — from graphs to hypergraphs — of the result of

[
12
,
8
]
for the case
k=1
(i.e., trees) to
“tree-like” hypergraphs. Note that there are different
concepts of “tree-likeness” for hypergraphs. Berge-acyclicity is a
rather restricted one; it is subsumed by the more general concept of

\alpha
-acyclic hypergraphs, which coincides with the hypergraphs of

generalised hypertree width
1
(cf.,

[
14
,
15
,
13
]
).

This paper gives a further answer to the above question: For arbitrary

k\geqslant 1
we consider the class
\textsl{GHW}_{k}
of hypergraphs of generalised
hypertree width at most
k
.
Our main result provides a logical characterisation of
homomorphism indistinguishability over the class
\textsl{GHW}_{k}
.
We introduce a new logic called
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
and show that two hypergraphs
are homomorphism indistinguishable over
\textsl{GHW}_{k}
if, and only if, they
are indistinguishable by sentences of the logic
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
.

\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
is a 2-sorted counting logic for expressing properties of hypergraphs. It
has available
k
“blue” variables to address edges, and an unbounded number of
“red” variables to address vertices of a hypergraph, and atomic formulas

E(e,v)
to express that vertex
v
is contained in edge
e
, as well
as atomic formulas
e=e^{\prime}
and
v=v^{\prime}
for expressing equality of edge
or vertex variables.
Counting quantifiers are of the form
\exists^{\geqslant n}\overline{z}
where

\overline{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{\ell})
is either a tuple of edge variables or
a tuple of vertex variables; and their meaning is “there exist at
least
n
tuples
\overline{z}
”.
In the logic
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
, each vertex variable
v
has to be
guarded

by an edge variable
e
and an atomic statement
E(e,v)
(meaning that
vertex
v
is included in edge
e
);
the use of quantifiers is restricted in a way to ensure that guards are always present.
Our design of the logic
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
is heavily inspired by the
guarded fragment of
first-order logic
(cf.,
[
4
,
16
,
15
]
).

Our main result can be viewed as a lifting — from graphs to
hypergraphs — of Dvořák’s
[
12
]
result: Dvořák proves
that two graphs are homomorphism indistinguishable over the class

\textsl{TW}_{k}
of
graphs of tree-width
\leqslant k
iff they are indistinguishable by the logic
C^{k+1}
.
We prove that two hypergraphs are homomorphism indistinguishable over the class
\textsl{GHW}_{k}
of
hypergraphs of generalised hypertree width
\leqslant k
iff they are indistinguishable by
the logic
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
.

This is analogous (although not tightly related) to the following classical results:
Kolaitis and Vardi
[
19
]
proved that the conjunctive queries of tree-width
\leqslant k
are precisely the queries expressible in
the
k{+}1
-variable fragment of a certain subclass
L
of first-order
logic. Gottlob et al.
[
15
]
proved that the conjunctive
queries of hypertree width
\leqslant k
are precisely the ones expressible
in the
k
-guarded fragment of
L
.
This is somehow parallel to our result generalising Dvořák’s
characterisation; it is what initially gave us the confidence to work
on our hypothesis.

The proof of our theorem is at its core very similar to Dvořák’s proof — but it is far from straightforward.
Before being able to follow along the lines of Dvořák’s proof, we
first have to perform a number of reduction steps and build the
necessary machinery.

The first step is to move over from
homomorphisms on hypergraphs to homomorphisms on incidence graphs.
Fortunately, Böker
[
7
]
already implicitly achieved what
is needed in our setting. The result is: Two hypergraphs
H,H^{\prime}
are
homomorphism indistinguishable over the class
\textsl{GHW}_{k}
iff their
incidence graphs
I,I^{\prime}
are homomorphism indistinguishable over the
class
\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
of incidence graphs of generalised hypertree width

\leqslant k
;
see Section
3
.

Next, for an inductive proof in the spirit of Dvořák, we would need an inductive characterisation of the class
\textsl{IGHW}_{k}

in the spirit of
[
9
]
. Unfortunately, generalised
hypertree decompositions seem to be unsuitable for such a
characterisation. That is why we work with severely restricted
decompositions that we call
entangled hypertree decompositions

(ehds, for short).
In Section
4
we prove that homomorphism
indistinguishability over the class
\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
coincides with
homomorphism indistinguishability over the class
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
of incidence
graphs of
entangled hypertree width

\leqslant k
.
In our opinion this is interesting on its own, since the requirements
of ehds are quite harsh and
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}\varsubsetneq\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
for arbitrarily large
k
.

In Section
6
we introduce the logic
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
and provide some
example formulas.
In Section
7
we establish a
normal form

for
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
called
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
that
imposes certain restrictions on the way guards of red variables can
change between quantifications in a formula. These restrictions are
crucial for the proof of our main theorem.
The inductive characterisation of
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
follows
in Section
8
, where we also provide the
machinery of
quantum
incidence graphs
as an analogue of the quantum
graphs used in Dvořák’s proof, tailored towards our setting.
In Section
9
we prove that
two incidence graphs
I,I^{\prime}
are indistinguishable by the logic
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}

(or, equivalently,
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
)
if, and only if, they are homomorphism indistinguishable over the
class
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
. This is achieved by two inductive proofs: We use our
inductive characterisation of
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
to show that for every
incidence graph
J
in
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
and every
m\geqslant 0

there exists an
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
-sentence that is satisfied by an incidence graph
I
iff there are precisely
m
homomorphisms from
J
to
I
. For the opposite direction, we proceed by induction on the definition
of
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
and construct for every sentence
\chi
in
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
and
certain size parameters
m,d\geqslant 0

a quantum incidence graph
Q
in
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
satisfying the following:
for all incidence graphs
I
that match the size parameters
m,d
,
the number
\operatorname{hom}(Q,I)
of homomorphisms from
Q
to
I
is either 0
or 1, and it is 1 if and only if
I
satisfies the sentence

\chi
.
Both proofs are quite intricate, and the details of the syntax
definition of
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
had to be tweaked right in order to enable
proving
both
directions.

We wrap up in Section
10
: Plugging together the
results achieved in the previous sections yields our main theorem: Two
hypergraphs are homomorphism indistinguishable over the class
\textsl{GHW}_{k}

of
hypergraphs of generalised hypertree width
\leqslant k
iff they are indistinguishable by
the logic
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
.

While the main body of this paper contains concise proof sketches, the
formal proof details can be found in a comprehensive appendix.

Acknowledgement:

We thank Isolde Adler for pointing us to
the results in
[
2
,
1
,
3
]
, which led to Theorem
2.7
.

2
Preliminaries

This section provides basic notions concerning hypergraphs, incidence graphs, hypertree decompositions, and homomorphisms.
We write
\mathbb{R}
for the set of
reals,
\mathbb{N}
for the set of
non-negative integers, and we let
\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}\coloneqq\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}

and
[n]\coloneqq\{1,\ldots,n\}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
.

2.1
Hypergraphs

The hypergraphs considered in this paper are generalisations of
ordinary undirected graphs, where each edge can consist of an
arbitrary number of vertices. For our proofs it will be necessary to
deal with hypergraphs in which the same edge can have multiple
occurrences. Furthermore, it will be convenient to assume that every
vertex belongs to at least one edge. This is provided by the following
definition that is basically taken from
[
7
]
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
hypergraph

H\coloneqq(V(H),\allowbreak E(H),f_{H})

consists of disjoint finite sets
V(H)
of
vertices
and

E(H)
of edges, and an
incidence function

f_{H}
associating
with every
e\in E(H)
the set
f_{H}(e)\subseteq V(H)
of vertices
incident with edge
e
, such that
V(H)=\bigcup_{e\in E(H)}f_{H}(e)
.

A
simple hypergraph
is a hypergraph
H
where the function
f_{H}
is injective.

We can identify the edges of a hypergraph
H
with the multiset
M_{H}\coloneqq\{\!\!\{f_{H}(e):e\in E(H)\}\!\!\}
; the
number of occurrences
of a set
s\subseteq V(H)
in this multiset then is the number of
occurrences of “edge
s
” in
H
. The
simple
hypergraphs are
the hypergraphs in which every “edge
s
” has only one occurrence.

Every hypergraph
H=(V(H),E(H),f_{H})
can be represented by an ordinary, bipartite graph
{I}_{H}
in the following way:
The vertices
v\in V(H)
occur as
red
nodes of
{I}_{H}
, i.e.,
R({I}_{H})\coloneqq V(H)
.
The edges
e\in E(H)
occur as
blue
nodes of
{I}_{H}
, i.e.,

B({I}_{H})\coloneqq E(H)
.
And there is an edge from each blue node
e
to all red nodes
v\in f_{H}(e)
. I.e.,

E({I}_{H})\coloneqq\{(e,v)\in B({I}_{H})\times R({I}_{H}):v\in f_{H}(e)\}
.
The condition
V(H)=\bigcup_{e\in E(H)}f_{H}(e)
implies that every red node is adjacent to at least one blue node.
It is straighforward to see that the mapping
H\mapsto{I}_{H}

provides a bijection between the class of all hypergraphs and the
class of all
incidence graphs
, where the notion of incidence
graphs is as follows.

Definition 2.2
.

An
incidence graph

I=(R(I),\allowbreak B(I),\allowbreak E(I))
consists of disjoint finite sets
R(I)
and
B(I)
of

red
nodes and
blue
nodes, respectively, and a set of
edges
E(I)\subseteq B(I)\times R(I)
, such that each red node is
adjacent to at least one blue node.

As usual for graphs, the
neighbourhood

of a node
v
is the set
N_{I}(v)
of all nodes adjacent to
v
.
Thus, if
I
is the incidence graph
{I}_{H}
of a hypergraph
H
, then the neighbourhood
of every blue node
e
is
N_{I}(e)=f_{H}(e)
, i.e., the set of all vertices of
H
that are incident with edge
e
.
The neighbourhood
of every red node
v
is
N_{I}(v)=\{e\in E(H):v\in f_{H}(e)\}
, i.e., the set of all edges of
H
that are incident with
vertex
v
.

Two incidence graphs
I,I^{\prime}
are
isomorphic
(
I\cong I^{\prime}
, for short) if there exists
an
isomorphism
\pi=(\pi_{R},\pi_{B})
from
I
to
I^{\prime}
, i.e, bijections
\pi_{R}:R(I)\to R(I^{\prime})
and
\pi_{B}:B(I)\to B(I^{\prime})
such that
for all
(e,v)\in B(I)\times R(I)
we have:
(e,v)\in E(I)

\iff

(\pi_{B}(e),\pi_{R}(v))\in E(I^{\prime})
.
We sometimes drop the subscript and write
\pi(e)
and
\pi(v)
instead of
\pi_{B}(e)
and
\pi_{R}(v)
.

2.2
Generalised Hypertree Decompositions

We use the same notation as
[
13
]
for decompositions of
hypergraphs, but we write
\textit{bag}(t)
and
\textit{cover}(t)
instead of

\chi(t)
and
\lambda(t)
, respectively, and we formalise them with
respect to incidence graphs rather than hypergraphs.

Definition 2.3
.

A
complete generalised hypertree decomposition
(
ghd
, for
short) of an incidence graph
I
is a tuple
D\coloneqq(T,\textit{bag},\textit{cover})
, where
T\coloneqq(V(T),E(T))
is a
finite undirected tree,
and

bag
and
cover
are mappings that associate with every tree-node

t\in V(T)
a set
\textit{bag}(t)\subseteq R(I)
of red nodes of
I
and a
set
\textit{cover}(t)\subseteq B(I)
of blue nodes of
I
, having the
following properties:

1.

Completeness
: For every
e\in B({I})
there is a
tree-node
t\in V(T)
with
N_{I}(e)\subseteq\textit{bag}(t)
and

e\in\textit{cover}(t)
.

2.

Connectedness for red nodes
: For every
v\in R({I})

the subgraph
T_{v}
of
T
induced on
V_{v}\coloneqq\{t\in V(T):v\in\textit{bag}(t)\}
is a tree.

3.

Covering of Bags
: For every
t\in V(T)
we have
\textit{bag}(t)\subseteq\bigcup_{e\in\textit{cover}(t)}N_{{I}}(e)
.

The
width
\operatorname{w}(D)
of a ghd

D
is defined as the maximum number
of blue nodes in the cover of a tree-node, i.e.,
\operatorname{w}(D)\coloneqq\max\{|\textit{cover}(t)|:t\in V(T)\}
.
We write
\textit{ghds}({I})
to denote the class of
all ghds of an incidence graph
I
.

It is straightforward to see that this notion of a ghd of an incidence
graph
I
coincides the classical notion (cf.,

[
14
,
13
]
) of a complete generalised hypertree
decomposition of a hypergraph
H
where
{I}_{H}=I
.

Definition 2.4
.

The
generalised hypertree width
of an incidence graph
I
is

\operatorname{ghw}(I)\coloneqq\min\{\operatorname{w}(D):D\in\textit{ghds}(I)\}
.
By
\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
we denote the class of all incidence graphs of generalised hypertree width
\leqslant k
.

It is straightforward to see that

\operatorname{ghw}({I}_{H})
coincides with the classical notion (cf.,
[
13
]
) of generalised hypertree width of
a hypergraph
H
, and
\textsl{IGHW}_{k}{}
is the class of incidence graphs
{I}_{H}
of all hypergraphs
H
of generalised hypertree width
\leqslant k
.

For our proofs we need ghds with specific further properties, defined as follows;
we are not aware of any related work that studies this particular kind
of decompositions.
1
1

1

Hypertree decompositions satisfying
condition
4
(but
not necessarily
condition
5
)
of Definition
2.5
are known
as
strong
decompositions
[
15
]
.

Definition 2.5
.

An
entangled hypertree decomposition
(
ehd
, for short) of
an incidence graph
I
is a ghd
D
of
I
that additionally
satisfies the following requirements:

4.

Precise coverage of bags
: For all tree-nodes
t\in V(T)
we
have

\bigcup_{e\in\textit{cover}(t)}N_{I}(e)=\textit{bag}(t).

5.

Connectedness for blue nodes
: For every
e\in B(I)
the
subgraph
T_{e}
of
T
induced on
V_{e}\coloneqq\{t\in V(T):e\in\textit{cover}(t)\}
is a tree.

We write
\textit{ehds}(I)
to denote the class of all ehds of an incidence graph
I
.

Definition 2.6
.

The
entangled hypertree width
of an incidence graph
I
is

\operatorname{ehw}(I)\coloneqq\min\{\operatorname{w}(D):D\in\textit{ehds}(I)\}
.
For a hypergraph
H
we let
\operatorname{ehw}(H)\coloneqq\operatorname{ehw}(I_{H})
.

By
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
we denote the class of all incidence graphs of entangled hypertree width
\leqslant k
.

Clearly,
\operatorname{ghw}(I)\leqslant\operatorname{ehw}(I)
for every incidence graph
I
. Hence,
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}\subseteq\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
.
Applying results from
[
2
,
1
,
3
]
shows that
there exist arbitrarily large
k
such that

\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
is a strict subclass of
\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
.
More precisely:

Theorem 2.7
.

\textsl{IEHW}_{k}\subseteq\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
.
Furthermore,

\textsl{IEHW}_{1}=\textsl{IGHW}_{1}
, but
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}\varsubsetneq\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
for each

k\in\{2,3\}
.
Moreover, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
such
that
\textsl{IGHW}_{k}\not\subseteq\textsl{IEHW}_{k+n}
(and hence,
\textsl{IEHW}_{k+n}\varsubsetneq\textsl{IGHW}_{k+n}
).

Proof.

\textsl{IEHW}_{k}\subseteq\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
holds because every ehd also is a ghd.

\textsl{IEHW}_{1}=\textsl{IGHW}_{1}
holds because ghds of width 1 are known to be
equivalent to so-called
join trees
, and these can easily be
translated into ehds
of width 1.
For the remaining statements, we use elaborate results from

[
2
,
1
,
3
]
that relate the

hypertree width

\operatorname{hw}(H)
(cf.,

[
14
,
15
]
) of a hypergraph to its

generalised hypertree width

\operatorname{ghw}(H)
:

From
[
2
, Proposition 3.3.2]
(cf. also

[
3
, Example 3]
) and

[
1
, Claim 6.1]
we obtain for each
k\in\{2,3\}
a
simple hypergraph
H_{k}
such that
\operatorname{ghw}(H_{k})=k
and
\operatorname{hw}(H_{k})=k{+}1
.
Furthermore,

[
2
, Fact 3.3.1]

and
[
1
, Theorem 4.1]

provide for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
a simple
hypergraph
H^{n}
such that
\operatorname{hw}(H^{n})=\operatorname{ghw}(H^{n}){+}n
.
2
2

2

Note that the notions
c_{H}{\text{-}}\operatorname{hw}(H)
and
c_{H}{\text{-}}\operatorname{ghw}(H)
in
[
2
]
correspond to
\operatorname{hw}(H)
and
\operatorname{ghw}(H)
for all hypergraphs
H
according to
[
2
, Example 2.1.10]
.

It is straightforward to verify that every ehd also is a
complete
hypertree decomposition
in the sense of

[
14
,
15
]
. Consequently, for every hypergraph
H

we have
\operatorname{hw}(H)\leqslant\operatorname{ehw}(H)
.
Therefore, for each
k\in\{2,3\}
, the incidence graph of
H_{k}

witnesses that
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}\varsubsetneq\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
.

To address the theorem’s next statement, consider an arbitrary

n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
H\coloneqq H^{n+1}
and let
k\coloneqq\operatorname{ghw}(H)
. Then,

\operatorname{ehw}(H)\geqslant\operatorname{hw}(H)=k{+}n{+}1
. Thus, the incidence graph
of
H
belongs to
\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
but not to
\textsl{IEHW}_{k+n}
.
∎

2.3
Homomorphisms

We use the classical notions for hypergraphs and incidence graphs:

Definition 2.8
.

Let
F
and
H
be hypergraphs. A
homomorphism
from
F
to
H
is a
pair
(h_{V},h_{E})
of mappings
h_{V}:V(F)\to V(H)
and
h_{E}:E(F)\to E(H)

such that for every
e\in E(F)
we have

f_{H}(h_{E}(e))=\{h_{V}(v):v\in f_{F}(e)\}
.

By
\operatorname{Hom}(F,H)
we denote the set of all homomorphisms from
F
to

H
, and we let
\operatorname{hom}(F,H)\coloneqq|\operatorname{Hom}(F,H)|
be the number of
homomorphisms from
F
to
H
.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
J
and
I
be incidence graphs. A
homomorphism
from
J
to
I
is a pair

h=(h_{R},h_{B})
of mappings

h_{R}:R(J)\to R(I)
and
h_{B}:B(J)\to B(I)
such that for all
(e,v)\in E(J)
we have

(h_{B}(e),h_{R}(v))\in E(I)
.

We sometimes drop the subscript and write
h(e)
and
h(v)
instead of
h_{B}(e)
and
h_{R}(v)
.

By
\operatorname{Hom}(J,I)
we denote the set of all homomorphisms from
J
to

I
, and we let
\operatorname{hom}(J,I)\coloneqq|\operatorname{Hom}(J,I)|
be the number
of homomorphisms from
J
to
I
.

As pointed out in
[
7
]
, every homomorphism from a
hypergraph
F
to a hypergraph
H
also is a homomorphism from the
incidence graph
{I}_{F}
to the incidence graph
{I}_{H}
;
but there exist homomorphisms from
{I}_{F}
to
{I}_{H}

that do not correspond to any homomorphism from
F
to
H
.
In fact, every homomorphism
(h_{R},h_{B})
from
{I}_{F}
to

{I}_{H}
is a pair of mappings
(h_{V},h_{E})\coloneqq(h_{R},h_{B})
with

h_{V}:V(F)\to V(H)
and
h_{E}:E(F)\to E(H)
such that for every
e\in E(F)
we have

f_{H}(h_{E}(e))\supseteq\{h_{V}(v):v\in f_{F}(e)\}
— i.e., the
condition “
=
” of Definition
2.8
is relaxed
into the condition “
\supseteq
”.

3
Homomorphism Indistinguishability

Let
(B,B^{\prime},\mathcal{C})
be either two incidence graphs and a class of incidence graphs or two hypergraphs and a class of hypergraphs.
By
\textsf{{HOM}}_{\mathcal{C}}(B)
we denote the function
\alpha:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{N}
that associates with every
A\in\mathcal{C}
the number
\operatorname{hom}(A,B)
of homomorphisms from
A
to
B
.
We say that
B
and
B^{\prime}
are
homomorphism indistinguishable over
\mathcal{C}
if
\textsf{{HOM}}_{\mathcal{C}}(B)=\textsf{{HOM}}_{\mathcal{C}}(B^{\prime})
.
Note that
\textsf{{HOM}}_{\mathcal{C}}(B)\neq\textsf{{HOM}}_{\mathcal{C}}(B^{\prime})
means that there
exists an
A\in\mathcal{C}
that
distinguishes
between
B
and
B^{\prime}

in the sense that
\operatorname{hom}(A,B)\neq\operatorname{hom}(A,B^{\prime})
, i.e., the number of
homomorphisms from
A
to
B
is different from the number of
homomorphisms from
A
to
B^{\prime}
.

Recall from Section
2
that
\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
is the class of all incidence graphs of generalised hypertree width
\leqslant k
.
We write
\textsl{GHW}_{k}
for the class of all hypergraphs of generalised
hypertree width
\leqslant k
(i.e., all hypergraphs
H
for which

{I}_{H}\in\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
), and
\textsl{sGHW}_{k}
for the subclass consisting
of all
simple
hypergraphs (i.e., hypergraphs where each edge
has multiplicity 1) in

\textsl{GHW}_{k}
.
Proof details for the following theorem can be found in Appendix
A
.

Theorem 3.1

(implicit in
[
7
]
)
.

Let
H,H^{\prime}
be hypergraphs.

1.

If
H
and
H^{\prime}
are simple hypergraphs, then

\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{GHW}_{k}}(H)=\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{GHW}_{k}}(H^{\prime})

\iff

\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{sGHW}_{k}}(H)=\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{sGHW}_{k}}(H^{\prime})
.

2.

\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{GHW}_{k}}(H)=\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{GHW}_{k}}(H^{\prime})

\iff

\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IGHW}_{k}}({I}_{H})=\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IGHW}_{k}}({I}_{H^{\prime}})
.

Böker
[
7
]
proved the analogous statement for

\textsl{BA},\textsl{IBA}
instead of
\textsl{GHW}_{k},\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
, where
BA
is the
class of all Berge-acyclic hypergraphs and
IBA
is the class of
all incidence graphs of hypergraphs in
BA
.
Böker’s proof, however, works for all classes
C
of
hypergraphs and the associated class
IC
of all incidence
graphs of hypergraphs in
C
, provided that
C
is
closed under
local merging
and
pumping
(Böker calls this
“leaf adding”).
A class
C
is closed under
local merging
, if every

F\in\textsl{C}
remains in
C
when merging two vertices

u_{1},u_{2}\in V(F)
that are adjacent via a common edge
e\in E(F)

(i.e.,
u_{1},u_{2}\in f_{F}(e)
), into a single new vertex
u
.
A class
C
is closed under
pumping
, if every

F\in\textsl{C}
remains in
C
when inserting a newly created
vertex into an edge
e\in E(F)
(but note that we must not insert
vertices in the intersection of multiple edges).
It is easy to see that the class
\textsl{GHW}_{k}
is closed under local merging and pumping.

4
Relating
\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
to
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}

Recall from Section
2
that

\textsl{IEHW}_{k}\subseteq\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
, for the class
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
of
incidence graphs of entangled hypertree width
\leqslant k
and the class
\textsl{IGHW}_{k}

of incidence graphs of generalised hypertree width
\leqslant k
.
By Theorem
2.7
there exist arbitrarily large
k
such that
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
is a strict
subclass of
\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
.
This section’s main result is that, nevertheless:

Theorem 4.1
.

For all incidence graphs
I
and
I^{\prime}
we have

\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IGHW}_{k}}(I)=\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IGHW}_{k}}(I^{\prime})

\iff

\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IEHW}_{k}}(I)=\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IEHW}_{k}}(I^{\prime})
.

The proof heavily relies on the following technical main lemma, which
uses the following notation:
For an arbitrary incidence graph
J
,
for
s\subseteq R(J)
, and for
n\in\mathbb{N}
we write
J+n{\cdot}s
to
denote the incidence graph
J^{\prime}
obtained from
J
by inserting
n

new blue nodes
\hat{e}_{1},\ldots,\hat{e}_{n}
and edges
(\hat{e}_{i},v)
for all

i\in[n]
and all
v\in s
— i.e.,
N_{J^{\prime}}(\hat{e}_{i})=s
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
J,I,I^{\prime}
be incidence graphs with

\operatorname{hom}(J,I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J,I^{\prime})
, let
e\in B(J)
, and let
s\subseteq N_{J}(e)
.
For every
m\in\mathbb{N}
there exists an
n\in\mathbb{N}
with

n\geqslant m
such that
J_{n}\coloneqq J+n{\cdot}s
satisfies

\operatorname{hom}(J_{n},I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J_{n},I^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Let
J,I,I^{\prime},e,s,m
be given as in the lemma’s assumption.
Let
[e]\coloneqq\{e^{\prime}\in B(J):N_{J}(e^{\prime})=N_{J}(e)\}
.
Let
z\coloneqq|[e]|
.

We partition the set
\operatorname{Hom}(J,I)
of all homomorphisms from

J
to
I
as follows.
Let
\mathcal{R}
be the set of all mappings
r:R(J)\to R(I)
, let

\mathcal{B}
be the set of all mappings
b:B(J)\setminus[e]\to B(I)
.
For all
(r,b)\in\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{B}
let

\textup{H}_{r,b}\coloneqq\{(h_{R},h_{B})\in\operatorname{Hom}(J,I):h_{R}=r\text{ and
}h_{B}(e^{\prime})=b(e^{\prime})\text{ for all }e^{\prime}\in B(J)\setminus[e]\}
.
Let

\mathcal{RB}=\{(r,b)\in\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{B}:H_{r,b}\neq\varnothing\}
.
Obviously,
the sets
\textup{H}_{r,b}
are pairwise disjoint, and

\operatorname{Hom}(J,I)=\bigcup_{(r,b)\in\mathcal{RB}}\textup{H}_{r,b}
.

Let
X_{r,b}
be the set of all
\tilde{e}\in B(I)
for which
there exist
(h_{R},h_{B})\in\textup{H}_{r,b}
and
e^{\prime}\in[e]
such that
h_{B}(e^{\prime})=\tilde{e}
.
Let
x_{r,b}\coloneqq|X_{r,b}|
. Clearly,
x_{r,b}\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
.

Claim 1:

|\textup{H}_{r,b}|=(x_{r,b})^{z}
for all

(r,b)\in\mathcal{RB}
.

Proof of Claim 1:
Let
e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{z}
be a list of all elements in
[e]
.
For every tuple
t=(\tilde{e}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{e}_{z})\in(X_{r,b})^{z}
let

h_{b,t}:B(J)\to B(I)
be defined by
h_{b,t}(e^{\prime}_{i})=\tilde{e}_{i}
for
all
i\in[z]
and
h_{b,t}(e^{\prime})=b(e^{\prime})
for all
e^{\prime}\in B(J)\setminus[e]
.
It is straightforward to verify that the mapping
\pi

defined by
\pi(t)\coloneqq(r,h_{b,t})
for all
t\in(X_{r,b})^{z}
is a
bijection from
(X_{r,b})^{z}
to the set
\textup{H}_{r,b}
.
∎
{}_{\textit{Claim 1}}

From Claim 1 we obtain:

\operatorname{hom}(J,I)\ \ =\ \ \sum_{(r,b)\in\mathcal{RB}}(x_{r,b})^{z}\;.

(1)

Consider, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the incidence graph
J_{n}\coloneqq J+n{\cdot}s
obtained from
J
by inserting
n
new blue nodes

\hat{e}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\hat{e}_{n}
and according edges such that
N_{J_{n}}(\hat{e}_{i})=s
for all
i\in[n]
.

Let
Y_{r,b}
be the set of all
\tilde{e}\in B(I)
for which
there exist
(h_{R},h_{B})\in\operatorname{Hom}(J_{1},I)
such that
h_{R}=r
,

h_{B}(\hat{e}_{1})=\tilde{e}
and
h_{B}(e^{\prime})=b(e^{\prime})
for all

e^{\prime}\in B(J)\setminus[e]
.
Note that
Y_{r,b}\supseteq X_{r,b}
.
Let
y_{r,b}\coloneqq|Y_{r,b}|
. Clearly,
y_{r,b}\geqslant x_{r,b}
.

Claim 2:
For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have:

\operatorname{hom}(J_{n},I)\ =\ \sum_{(r,b)\in\mathcal{RB}}\ (x_{r,b})^{z}\cdot(y_{r,b})^{n}\;.

(2)

Proof of Claim 2:
Let
e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{z}
be a list of all elements in
[e]
.
For every tuple
t=(\tilde{e}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{e}_{z},e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})\in(X_{r,b})^{z}\times(Y_{r,b})^{n}
let

h_{b,t}:B(J_{n})\to B(I)
be defined by
h_{b,t}(e^{\prime}_{i})=\tilde{e}_{i}
for
all
i\in[z]
,
h_{b,t}(\hat{e}_{i})=e_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
, and
h_{b,t}(e^{\prime})=b(e^{\prime})
for all
e^{\prime}\in B(J)\setminus[e]
.
It is straightforward to verify that the mapping
\pi

defined by
\pi(t)\coloneqq(r,h_{b,t})
for all
t\in(X_{r,b})^{z}\times(Y_{r,b})^{n}
is a bijection from
(X_{r,b})^{z}\times(Y_{r,b})^{n}
to the set
\textup{H}_{r,b}^{(n)}
of all

(h_{R},h_{B})\in\operatorname{Hom}(J_{n},I)
with
h_{R}=r
and
h_{B}(e^{\prime\prime})=b(e^{\prime\prime})
for
all
e^{\prime\prime}\in B(J)\setminus[e]
.

Thus,
|\textup{H}_{r,b}^{(n)}|=(x_{r,b})^{z}\cdot(y_{r,b})^{n}
.
The claim follows by noting that the sets
\textup{H}_{r,b}^{(n)}
are
pairwise disjoint and

\operatorname{Hom}(J_{n},I)=\bigcup_{(r,b)\in\mathcal{RB}}\textup{H}_{r,b}^{(n)}
.
∎
{}_{\textit{Claim 2}}

By performing the analogous reasoning with
I^{\prime}
instead of
I
we
obtain a set
\mathcal{RB}^{\prime}
and positive integers
x^{\prime}_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
and
y^{\prime}_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
with

y^{\prime}_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\geqslant x^{\prime}_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
for all
(r^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\mathcal{RB}^{\prime}
such that

\operatorname{hom}(J,I^{\prime})\ \ =\ \ \sum_{(r^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\mathcal{RB}^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}})^{z}

(3)

and, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\operatorname{hom}(J_{n},I^{\prime})\ =\ \sum_{(r^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\mathcal{RB}^{\prime}}\ (x^{\prime}_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}})^{z}\cdot(y^{\prime}_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}})^{n}\;.

(4)

If there exists an
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geqslant m
such that

\operatorname{Hom}(J_{n},I)\neq\operatorname{Hom}(J_{n},I^{\prime})
, we are done.
Assume for contradiction that no such
n
exists. Then, for all

n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geqslant m
we have
\operatorname{Hom}(J_{n},I)=\operatorname{Hom}(J_{n},I^{\prime})
. By
(
2
) and (
4
) this yields

\sum_{(r,b)\in\mathcal{RB}}(x_{r,b})^{z}\cdot(y_{r,b})^{n}\ =\ \sum_{(r^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\mathcal{RB}^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}})^{z}\cdot(y^{\prime}_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}})^{n}

and hence

\sum_{(r,b)\in\mathcal{RB}}\!\!\!(x_{r,b})^{z}\cdot(y_{r,b})^{n}\ -\!\!\!\sum_{(r^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\mathcal{RB}^{\prime}}\!\!\!(x^{\prime}_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}})^{z}\cdot(y^{\prime}_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}})^{n}\ =\ 0

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geqslant m
.

We view this as a system of equations, and we group the summands with
respect to the distinct values in

Z\coloneqq\{y_{r,b}:(r,b)\in\mathcal{RB}\}\cup\{y^{\prime}_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}}:(r^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\mathcal{RB}^{\prime}\}
.
To this end let
k\coloneqq|Z|
and let
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}
be a list of all
elements in
Z
.
For every
i\in[k]
let
M_{i}\coloneqq\{(r,b)\in\mathcal{RB}:y_{r,b}=z_{i}\}
and

M^{\prime}_{i}\coloneqq\{(r^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\mathcal{RB}^{\prime}:y^{\prime}_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}}=z_{i}\}
, and let

a_{i}\ \coloneqq\ \sum_{(r,b)\in M_{i}}\!\!(x_{r,b})^{z}\ -\sum_{(r^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in M^{\prime}_{i}}\!\!(x^{\prime}_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}})^{z}.

The above system of equations yields that

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,a_{i}\cdot(z_{i})^{n}\ \ =\ 0\qquad\text{for all $n\geqslant m$.}

By assumption we know that
\operatorname{hom}(J,I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J,I^{\prime})
, and hence
(
1
) and (
3
) yield that

\sum_{(r,b)\in\mathcal{RB}}(x_{r,b})^{z}\ \ \neq\ \ \sum_{(r^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\mathcal{RB}^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}})^{z}\;.

Therefore,

a_{i}\neq 0\quad\text{for at least one $i\in[k]$}.

(5)

The system of equations

\Big(\,{\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}}a_{i}{\cdot}\,(z_{i})^{n}=0\Big)_{n\in\{m,\ldots,m+k-1\}}

can be expressed as

\underbrace{\left(\begin{array}[]{lll}(z_{1})^{m}&\dots&(z_{k})^{m}\\
(z_{1})^{m+1}&\dots&(z_{k})^{m+1}\\
\ \ \vdots&\ddots&\ \ \vdots\\
(z_{1})^{m+k-1}&\dots&(z_{k})^{m+k-1}\end{array}\right)}_{\textstyle=:V}\cdot\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}\\
a_{2}\\
\vdots\\
a_{k}\end{pmatrix}}_{\textstyle=:\bar{a}}=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{pmatrix}}_{\textstyle=:\bar{0}}

But
V
is a
generalised Vandermonde matrix
on pairwise
distinct non-zero values
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}
, and is known to be an
invertible matrix
[
10
]
3
3

3

to see this, combine
“application 2” with “facts 8” and “18” of
[
10
]
.
Hence, the equation
V\cdot\bar{a}=\bar{0}
implies that

\bar{a}=\bar{0}
.
This contradicts (
5
) and completes the proof of
Lemma
4.2
.
∎

Proof sketch for Theorem
4.1
:
.

The direction “
\Longrightarrow
” of Theorem
4.1
is trivial.
For the direction “
\Longleftarrow
” it suffices to prove the following:
If there is a
J\in\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
with
\operatorname{hom}(J,I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J,I^{\prime})
,
then there also exists a
J^{\prime}\in\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
with
\operatorname{hom}(J^{\prime},I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J^{\prime},I^{\prime})
.
We construct such a
J^{\prime}
in a 2-step process.
We start with a ghd
D=(T,\textit{bag},\textit{cover})
of
J
with
\operatorname{w}(D)\leqslant k
.
First, we transform
D
into a ghd
D^{1}
of an incidence graph
J^{1}
such that
\operatorname{w}(D^{1})\leqslant\operatorname{w}(D)
and

\operatorname{hom}(J^{1},I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J^{1},I^{\prime})
and
D^{1}
satisfies
condition
4
of
Definition
2.5
(but
condition
5

might still be violated).
Afterwards, we transform
D^{1}
into a ghd
D^{2}
of an
incidence graph
J^{2}
such that
\operatorname{w}(D^{2})=\operatorname{w}(D^{1})
and

\operatorname{hom}(J^{2},I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J^{2},I^{\prime})
and
D^{2}
satisfies
conditions
4

and
5

of Definition
2.5
and hence
is an ehd. Letting
J^{\prime}\coloneqq J^{2}
then completes the proof.

For the construction of
D^{1},J^{1}
we consider all those

t\in V(T)
and
e\in\textit{cover}(t)
where
N_{J}(e)\not\subseteq\textit{bag}(t)
and let
s\coloneqq N_{J}(e)\cap\textit{bag}(t)
.
We use Lemma
4.2
to choose
a suitable number
n_{s}\geqslant 1
and replace
J
by

J+n_{s}{\cdot}s
(let us write
e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots e^{\prime}_{n_{s}}
for
the
n_{s}
newly inserted blue nodes).
In
D
we replace
e
with
e^{\prime}_{1}
in
\textit{cover}(t)
, and
we add new leaves
t_{j}
for
j\in\{2,\ldots,n_{s}\}
adjacent to
t

with
\textit{cover}(t_{j})=\{e^{\prime}_{j}\}
and
\textit{bag}(t_{j})=s
.
After having done this for all combinations of
t
and
e
, we end up
with the desired incidence graph
J^{1}
and ghd
D^{1}=(T^{1},\textit{bag}^{1},\textit{cover}^{1})
.

For the construction of
D^{2},J^{2}
, for
each
e\in B(J^{1})
we let
m_{e}
be the
number of connected components of the subgraph

T^{1}_{e}
, i.e., the subgraph of
T^{1}
induced on

V_{e}\coloneqq\{t\in V(T^{1}):e\in\textit{cover}^{1}(t)\}
.
Let
V_{e,0},\ldots,V_{e,m_{e}-1}

be the sets of tree-nodes (i.e., nodes in
V(T^{1})
) of these
connected components.
We consider all those
e\in B(J^{1})
where
m_{e}\geqslant 2

and let
s\coloneqq N_{J^{1}}(e)
. We use Lemma
4.2
to choose
a suitable number
n_{e}\geqslant m_{e}{-}1
and replace
{J}
with

{J}+n_{e}{\cdot}s
(let us write

e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{n_{e}}
for the
n_{e}
newly inserted blue nodes).
In
D^{1}
we consider for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{e}{-}1\}

all
t\in V_{e,i}
and replace
e
with
e^{\prime}_{i}
in

\textit{cover}^{1}(t)
.
Furthermore, we pick an arbitrary
t\in V_{e,0}
, and for each

i\in[n_{e}]
with
i\geqslant m_{e}
, we insert into
T^{1}
a new leaf
t_{e,i}

adjacent to
t
and let
\textit{bag}^{1}(t_{e,i})\coloneqq s
and

\textit{cover}^{1}(t_{e,i})\coloneqq\{e^{\prime}_{e,i}\}
.
After having done this for all
e\in B(J^{1})
with
m_{e}\geqslant 2
, we
end up with the desired incidence graph
J^{2}
and ehd
D^{2}
.
This completes the proof sketch of Theorem
4.1
(see
Appendix
B.1
and

B.2
for more details and
illustrations).
∎

5
Notation for Partial Functions

Let us introduce some further notation that will be convenient for the remaining parts of the paper.

We write
f:A\rightharpoonup B
to indicate that
f
is a partial function from

A
to
B
. By
\operatorname{dom}(f)
we denote the domain of
f
, i.e., the set of
all
a\in A
on which
f(a)
is defined.
By
\operatorname{img}(f)
we denote the image of
f
, i.e.,

\operatorname{img}(f)=\{f(a):a\in\operatorname{dom}(f)\}
.
Two partial functions
f:A\rightharpoonup B
and
g:A\rightharpoonup B
are called
compatible
if
f(a)=g(a)
holds for all

a\in\operatorname{dom}(f)\cap\operatorname{dom}(g)
.

We identify a partial function
f
with the set
\{(a,f(a)):a\in\operatorname{dom}(f)\}
. This allows us to compare and combine partial functions
via standard notation from set theory.
E.g.,
f\subseteq g
indicates that
\operatorname{dom}(f)\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(g)
and
f(a)=g(a)
for all
a\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
.
And
f\cup g
denotes the partial function
h
with

\operatorname{dom}(h)=\operatorname{dom}(f)\cup\operatorname{dom}(g)
and
h(a)=f(a)
for all
a\in\operatorname{dom}(f)

and
h(a)=g(a)
for all
a\in\operatorname{dom}(g)\setminus\operatorname{dom}(f)
(note that

f
has precedence over
g
in case that
f
and
g
are
not compatible).
For a set
S
we write
f-S
to denote the partial function
g
with
g\subseteq f
and
\operatorname{dom}(g)=\operatorname{dom}(f)\setminus S
.

6
A 2-Sorted Counting Logic with Guards:
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}

This section provides the syntax and semantics of our 2-sorted logic.
Formulas of this logic are evaluated on incidence graphs
(cf. Definition
2.2
). We fix a
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
.

To address
blue
nodes (i.e.,
edges
of a hypergraph), we have available
k

blue
variables

\texttt{{e}}_{1},\ldots,\texttt{{e}}_{k}
. To address
red
nodes
(i.e.,
vertices
of a hypergraph), we have
available countably many
red variables

\texttt{{v}}_{1},\texttt{{v}}_{2},\texttt{{v}}_{3},\ldots{}
.
An atomic formula
E(\texttt{{e}}_{j},\texttt{{v}}_{i})
states that a
hypergraph’s vertex
\texttt{{v}}_{i}
is included in the hypergraph’s edge
\texttt{{e}}_{j}
.

Let
\textsf{Var}_{B}\coloneqq\{\texttt{{e}}_{1},\ldots,\texttt{{e}}_{k}\}
,
\textsf{Var}_{R}\coloneqq\{\texttt{{v}}_{i}:i\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}\}
, and
\textsf{Var}\coloneqq\textsf{Var}_{B}\cup\textsf{Var}_{R}
.
An
interpretation

\mathcal{I}=(I,\beta)
consists of an incidence
graph
I=(R(I),B(I),E(I))
and an
assignment
\beta
in
I
, i.e., a mapping

\beta:\textsf{Var}\to R(I)\cup B(I)
with
\beta(\texttt{{e}}_{j})\in B(I)
for all

\texttt{{e}}_{j}\in\textsf{Var}_{B}
and
\beta(\texttt{{v}}_{i})\in R(I)
for all
\texttt{{v}}_{i}\in\textsf{Var}_{R}
.

In the formulas of our logic, red variables

\texttt{{v}}_{i}
have to be
guarded
by a blue variable
\texttt{{e}}_{j}
in the
sense that
E(\texttt{{e}}_{j},\texttt{{v}}_{i})
holds. This is formalised by a

guard function
, i.e., a
partial function

g:\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}\rightharpoonup[k]
with

finite
domain
\operatorname{dom}(g)
.
Every guard function
g
corresponds to the formula

\Delta_{g}\ \coloneqq\ \ \bigwedge_{i\in\operatorname{dom}(g)}\;E(\texttt{{e}}_{g(i)},\texttt{{v}}_{i})\;,

and for the special case where
\operatorname{dom}(g)=\varnothing
we let

\Delta_{g}\coloneqq\top
where
\top
is a special atomic formula
satisfied by
every
interpretation
\mathcal{I}
.
We let
\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g})
be the set of all (red or blue) variables that occur in

\Delta_{g}
.

An interpretation
\mathcal{I}=(I,\beta)

satisfies
a guard function

g
(in symbols:
\mathcal{I}\models\Delta_{g}
) if
for all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g)
we have:
(\beta(\texttt{{e}}_{g(i)}),\beta(\texttt{{v}}_{i}))\in E(I)
.
I.e., for every
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g)
, the red variable
\texttt{{v}}_{i}
is guarded by
the blue variable
\texttt{{e}}_{g(i)}
in
the sense that it is connected to it by an edge of the incidence graph.

For any formula
\chi
we write

\textit{ifree}_{B}(\chi)
for
the set of all indices
j\in[k]
such that the blue variable
\texttt{{e}}_{j}

belongs to
\operatorname{free}(\chi)
. Accordingly,

\textit{ifree}_{R}(\chi)\coloneqq\{i\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}:\texttt{{v}}_{i}\in\operatorname{free}(\chi)\}
.
The definition of the syntax of

\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
is inductively given as follows.

Base cases:
The atomic formulas in
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
are of the form
\top
,
E(\texttt{{e}}_{j},\texttt{{v}}_{i})
,
\texttt{{e}}_{j}{=}\texttt{{e}}_{j^{\prime}}
, and
\texttt{{v}}_{i}{=}\texttt{{v}}_{i^{\prime}}
for
j,j^{\prime}\in[k]
and
i,i^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geqslant 1}
.

We let
\operatorname{free}(\top)=\varnothing
,

\operatorname{free}(E(\texttt{{e}}_{j},\texttt{{v}}_{i}))=\{\texttt{{e}}_{j},\texttt{{v}}_{i}\}
,
\operatorname{free}(\texttt{{e}}_{j}{=}\texttt{{e}}_{j^{\prime}})=\{\texttt{{e}}_{j},\texttt{{e}}_{j^{\prime}}\}
, and

\operatorname{free}(\texttt{{v}}_{i}{=}\texttt{{v}}_{i^{\prime}})=\{\texttt{{v}}_{i},\texttt{{v}}_{i^{\prime}}\}
.

Inductive cases:

1.

If
\psi\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
, then

\lnot\psi\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
; let
\operatorname{free}(\lnot\psi)=\operatorname{free}(\psi)
;

2.

If
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
, then

(\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2})\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
; let
\operatorname{free}((\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2}))=\operatorname{free}(\psi_{1})\cup\operatorname{free}(\psi_{2})
;

3.

If
\psi\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
and
g
is a guard function with
\operatorname{dom}(g)=\textit{ifree}_{R}(\psi)
and
n,\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geqslant 1}
and, for
\chi\coloneqq(\Delta_{g}\land\psi)
and
i_{1}<\dots<i_{\ell}
with

(a)

i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell}\in\textit{ifree}_{R}(\chi)
, then
\varphi\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
for
\varphi\coloneqq\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n}(\texttt{{v}}_{i_{1}},\dots,\texttt{{v}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\land\psi)
;
let
\operatorname{free}(\varphi)=\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{g}\land\psi))\setminus\{\texttt{{v}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{v}}_{i_{\ell}}\}
;

(b)

i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell}\in\textit{ifree}_{B}(\chi)
, then
\varphi\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
for
\varphi\coloneqq\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n}(\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\dots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\land\psi)
;
let
\operatorname{free}(\varphi)=\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{g}\land\psi))\setminus\{\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}\}
.

The semantics are defined as expected.
In particular, an interpretation
\mathcal{I}=(I,\beta)
satisfies the
formula
\varphi\coloneqq\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n}(\texttt{{v}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{v}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)

iff there are
at least
n
tuples
(v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{\ell}})\in R(I)^{\ell}
such that

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=(I,\beta^{\prime})
satisfies
(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)
, where

\beta^{\prime}(\texttt{{v}}_{i_{j}})=v_{i_{j}}
for all
j\in[\ell]
and

\beta^{\prime}(\texttt{{x}})=\beta(\texttt{{x}})
for all

\texttt{{x}}\in\textsf{Var}\setminus\{\texttt{{v}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{v}}_{i_{\ell}}\}
.
Similarly,
\mathcal{I}=(I,\beta)
satisfies

\varphi\coloneqq\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n}(\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)
iff
there are at least
n
tuples

(e_{i_{1}},\ldots,e_{i_{\ell}})\in B(I)^{\ell}
such that

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=(I,\beta^{\prime})
satisfies
(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)
, where

\beta^{\prime}(\texttt{{e}}_{i_{j}})=e_{i_{j}}
for all
j\in[\ell]
and

\beta^{\prime}(\texttt{{x}})=\beta(\texttt{{x}})
for all

\texttt{{x}}\in\textsf{Var}\setminus\{\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}\}
.
Obviously we can emulate the
\forall
-quantifier (and disjunction) using
\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}1}
and
\lnot
(and
\land
and
\lnot
, respectively).

We write
\mathcal{I}\models\chi
to indicate that
\mathcal{I}
satisfies
the formula
\chi
; and

\mathcal{I}\not\models\chi
indicates that
\mathcal{I}
does not satisfy
\chi
.

Sentences
of
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
are formulas
\chi\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
with
\operatorname{free}(\chi)=\varnothing
.

For an incidence graph
I
and a sentence
\chi\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
we write

I\models\chi
to indicate that
\mathcal{I}\models\chi

where
\mathcal{I}=(I,\beta)
for any assignment
\beta
in
I
(since

\chi
has no free variable, the assignment does not matter).
For a hypergraph
H
and a sentence
\chi\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
we write
H\models\chi
to
indicate that
{I}_{H}\models\chi
.

For two incidence graphs
I
and
I^{\prime}
we write
I\equiv_{\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}}I^{\prime}
and
say that
I
and
I^{\prime}
are
indistinguishable by the logic
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
if
for all sentences
\chi\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
we have:
I\models\chi

\iff

I^{\prime}\models\chi
.

The subsequent sections of this paper are devoted to proving the following
theorem, stating that indistinguishability by the logic
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}

coincides with homomorphism indistinguishability over the class

\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
of incidence graphs of entangled hypertree width
\leqslant k
.

Theorem 6.1
.

For all incidence graphs
I,I^{\prime}
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
we have:

I\equiv_{\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}}I^{\prime}\iff\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IEHW}_{k}}(I)=\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IEHW}_{k}}(I^{\prime})
.

This result can be viewed as a lifting of Dvořák’s theorem

[
12
]
stating that any two graphs
G,G^{\prime}
are
indistinguishable by the
k{+}1
-variable logic
C^{k+1}
if, and only
if, they are homomorphism indistinguishable over the class
\textsl{TW}_{k}
of
graphs of tree-width
\leqslant k
.
Our proof of Theorem
6.1
is heavily inspired by
Dvořák’s proof.
But in order to proceed along a similar construction, we first have to provide
a “normal form” for
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
, which we present in Section
7
, and
a suitable inductive characterisation of the class
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
. The latter is
presented in Section
8
, where we also provide the machinery
of
quantum
incidence graphs as an analogue of the quantum
graphs used in Dvořák’s proof.
Section
9
is devoted to the proof of
Theorem
6.1
.

Before closing this section, let us
provide examples of formulas in
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
.

Example 6.2
.

Let
k=2
.
Consider the following formula
\psi_{1}\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
:

\psi_{1}\;\;\coloneqq\;\;\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}1}(\texttt{{v}}_{1}).\bigl(E(\texttt{{e}}_{1},\texttt{{v}}_{1})\land E(\texttt{{e}}_{2},\texttt{{v}}_{1})\bigr).

\psi_{1}
expresses that the hyperedges
\texttt{{e}}_{1}
and
\texttt{{e}}_{2}
share at least one vertex
\texttt{{v}}_{1}
, i.e. they intersect. Since we quantify over
\texttt{{v}}_{1}
, the definition of
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
requires us to insert a guard ranging over the set of free red variables, i.e. over
\{\texttt{{v}}_{1}\}
. We chose
E(\texttt{{e}}_{1},\texttt{{v}}_{1})
as the guard but note that
E(\texttt{{e}}_{2},\texttt{{v}}_{1})
would have been a valid choice as well.
Next, consider the formula
\psi_{2}\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
:

\psi_{2}\;\;\coloneqq\;\;\!\!\!\bigwedge_{j\in\{1,2\}}\!\!\!\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}3}(\texttt{{v}}_{1}).\bigl(E(\texttt{{e}}_{j},\texttt{{v}}_{1})\land E(\texttt{{e}}_{j},\texttt{{v}}_{1})\bigr).

\psi_{2}
expresses that each of the hyperedges
\texttt{{e}}_{1}
,
\texttt{{e}}_{2}
contains at least three vertices. Again, we have to insert a guard after the quantifier, which is why
E(\texttt{{e}}_{j},\texttt{{v}}_{1})
appears twice in
\psi_{2}
— as a guard
and
as our “actual” subformula.

Finally, we use the formulas
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{2}
to construct a sentence
\varphi\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
:

\varphi\;\;\coloneqq\;\;\lnot\,\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}1}(\texttt{{e}}_{1},\texttt{{e}}_{2}).\bigl(\top\land(\,(\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2})\,\land\lnot\;\texttt{{e}}_{1}{=}\texttt{{e}}_{2}\,)\,\bigr).

\varphi
expresses that there is no pair of non-equal hyperedges

(\texttt{{e}}_{1},\texttt{{e}}_{2})
that intersect and that both contain at least 3
vertices. I.e.,
\varphi
expresses that all hyperedges that contain at
least 3 vertices are pairwise disjoint. Once again, the quantification
requires us to insert a guard; since there are no free red variables,
we insert
\top
as the guard.

For a tuple
\bar{\texttt{{x}}}
of either blue or red variables, we write

\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$=$}n}\,\bar{\texttt{{x}}}.\varphi
as a shorthand for

(\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n}\,\bar{\texttt{{x}}}.\varphi\,\land\,\lnot\,\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n+1}\,\bar{\texttt{{x}}}.\varphi)
.

Example 6.3
.

Let
H
be
the hypergraph with vertices
1,2,3,4,5,6
, edges
e_{S}
for
each
S\in\{\,\{1,2,3\},\allowbreak\{4,5,6\},\allowbreak\{1,2\},\allowbreak\{2,3\},\allowbreak\{3,1\},\allowbreak\{4,5\},\allowbreak\{5,6\},\allowbreak\{6,4\}\,\}
and
incidence function
f_{H}
with

f_{H}(e_{S})=S
for each edge
e_{S}
of
H
.
We construct a sentence
\chi\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{2}}
that describes
H
up to isomorphism.

\chi
is the conjunction of
the following statements
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{5}
.
It can easily be checked that for
each
i\in[5]
the formula
\varphi_{i}
is a sentence in

\textsf{{GC}}^{{2}}
. Hence,
\chi
is also a
sentence in
\textsf{{GC}}^{{2}}
.

1.

There exist exactly 8 blue nodes (i.e., edges of
H
):

\varphi_{1}\coloneqq\ \exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$=$}8}(\texttt{{e}}_{1}).(\top\wedge\texttt{{e}}_{1}{=}\texttt{{e}}_{1})

2.

There exist exactly 2 blue nodes each of which has exactly 3 red neighbours
(i.e.,
H
has exactly 2 edges that are each
incident with exactly 3 vertices of
H
):

\varphi_{2}\coloneqq\ \exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$=$}2}(\texttt{{e}}_{1}).\big(\top\wedge\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$=$}3}(\texttt{{v}}_{1}).\big(E(\texttt{{e}}_{1},\texttt{{v}}_{1})\wedge E(\texttt{{e}}_{1},\texttt{{v}}_{1})\big)\big)

The first and 2nd occurrence of
E(\texttt{{e}}_{1},\texttt{{v}}_{1})
are used as the
guard and the formula, respectively.

3.

A similar formula
\varphi_{3}
expresses that there are exactly 6 blue
nodes each of which has exactly 2 red neighbours

4.

All blue nodes that have at least 3 red neighbours have
disjoint neighbourhoods:

\varphi_{4}\coloneqq\ \neg\,\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}1}(\texttt{{e}}_{1},\texttt{{e}}_{2}).\big(\top\,\wedge\,(\alpha\,\wedge\,\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}1}(\texttt{{v}}_{1}).(\bigwedge_{j\in[2]}E(\texttt{{e}}_{j},\texttt{{v}}_{1})))\big)

where

\alpha\coloneqq\ \big(\neg\,\texttt{{e}}_{1}{=}\texttt{{e}}_{2}\;\wedge\bigwedge_{j\in[2]}\!\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}3}(\texttt{{v}}_{1}).(E(\texttt{{e}}_{j},\texttt{{v}}_{1})\,\wedge\,E(\texttt{{e}}_{j},\texttt{{v}}_{1}))\big)

The formula
\alpha
expresses that
\texttt{{e}}_{1}
and
\texttt{{e}}_{2}
are
distinct blue nodes that each have at least
3
red neighbours.

5.

The final statement is expressed by the formula

\varphi_{5}\coloneqq\ \exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$=$}2}(\texttt{{e}}_{1}).(\top\,\wedge\;\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}1}(\texttt{{v}}_{1},\texttt{{v}}_{2},\texttt{{v}}_{3}).\big(\Delta\wedge(\chi\wedge\psi)\big)

where

\Delta\coloneqq\bigwedge_{i=1}^{3}E(\texttt{{e}}_{1},\texttt{{v}}_{i})\quad\text{and}\quad\chi\coloneqq\!\!\!\!\bigwedge_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 3}\!\!\!\!\neg\;\texttt{{v}}_{i}{=}\texttt{{v}}_{j}

and

\psi\coloneqq\big((\,\psi_{1,2}\wedge\psi_{2,3}\,)\wedge\psi_{3,1}\big)

where

\psi_{i,j}\ \coloneqq\ \exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$=$}1}(\texttt{{e}}_{2}).\big(\Delta_{i,j}\wedge\vartheta_{i,j}\big)\quad\text{with}

\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta_{i,j}\coloneqq\big(&E(\texttt{{e}}_{1},\texttt{{v}}_{i})\,\wedge\,E(\texttt{{e}}_{1},\texttt{{v}}_{j}))\qquad\text{and}\\[8.61108pt]
\vartheta_{i,j}\coloneqq\big(&E(\texttt{{e}}_{2},\texttt{{v}}_{i})\wedge E(\texttt{{e}}_{2},\texttt{{v}}_{j})\,\wedge\,\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$=$}2}(\texttt{{v}}_{4}).(E(\texttt{{e}}_{2},\texttt{{v}}_{4})\wedge E(\texttt{{e}}_{2},\texttt{{v}}_{4}))\big)\,.\end{array}

The formula
\varphi_{5}
states that there exist exactly 2 blue nodes

e_{1}
(i.e., edges of
H
)
each of which is adjacent to at least 3 distinct red nodes

v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}
(i.e., vertices of
H
) such that
H
contains an edge
incident with exactly
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, an edge incident with exactly

v_{2}
and
v_{3}
, and an edge incident with exactly
v_{3}
and
v_{1}
.

Finally,
\chi\coloneqq\bigwedge_{i=1}^{5}\varphi_{i}
is a sentence in
\textsf{{GC}}^{{2}}
.
It is not difficult to verify that
\chi
describes
H
up to isomorphism.

7
A Normal Form for
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
:
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}

In this section we provide a normal form for sentences of
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
that
will be crucial for our proof of
Theorem
6.1
. In the restriction
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
of

\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
,
every formula is of the form
(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)
,
where
g
is a guard function whose domain
\operatorname{dom}(g)

consists of
all indices
i\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
such that the red
variable
\texttt{{v}}_{i}
is a free variable of
\psi
.
Note that

\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi))\coloneqq\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g})\cup\operatorname{free}(\psi)

is the set of free variables of the formula
(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)
.
The definition of the syntax of

\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
is inductively given as follows.

Base cases:

(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
for all
\psi
and all
g:\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}\rightharpoonup[k]
matching one of the following:

1.

\psi
is
E(\texttt{{e}}_{j},\texttt{{v}}_{i})
and
\operatorname{dom}(g)=\{i\}
and
j\in[k]

(note that
g(i)
can be an arbitrary element in
[k]
);

2.

\psi
is
\texttt{{e}}_{j}{=}\texttt{{e}}_{j^{\prime}}
with
\operatorname{dom}(g)=\varnothing
and
j,j^{\prime}\in[k]
;

3.

\psi
is
\texttt{{v}}_{i}{=}\texttt{{v}}_{i^{\prime}}
with
\operatorname{dom}(g)=\{i,i^{\prime}\}
.

Inductive cases
:

4.

If
(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
, then

(\Delta_{g}\wedge\neg{\psi})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
;

5.

If
(\Delta_{g_{i}}\wedge\psi_{i})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
for
i\in[2]
and

g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are compatible (i.e., they agree on
\operatorname{dom}(g_{1})\cap\operatorname{dom}(g_{2})
),
then

(\Delta_{g}\wedge\varphi)\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
for

g\coloneqq g_{1}\cup g_{2}
and
\varphi\coloneqq(\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2})
;

6.

If
(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
and
n,\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
, and

i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\in\operatorname{dom}(g)
with
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
,
then
(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi)\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
for

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n}(\texttt{{v}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{v}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\tilde{g}

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle g-\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}

(note that

\operatorname{free}(\varphi)=\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi))\setminus\{\texttt{{v}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{v}}_{i_{\ell}}\}
);

7.

If
(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
and

n,\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
, and

S\coloneqq\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}\subseteq\textit{ifree}_{B}(\chi)
for
\chi\coloneqq(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)

with

i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
, and
if
\tilde{g}:\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}\rightharpoonup[k]
with

\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{g})=\operatorname{dom}(g)

such that
all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g)
satisfy

\tilde{g}(i)=g(i)\quad\text{or}\quad\tilde{g}(i)\in S\quad\text{or}\quad\tilde{g}(i)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)

(6)

then
(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi)\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
for

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n}(\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)\,.

(note that

\operatorname{free}(\varphi)=\operatorname{free}(\chi)\setminus\{\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}\}
).

Let us have
a closer look at rule
7
): The
formula
\varphi
has exactly the same free red variables as the formula

\chi
.
But the guard of red variable
\texttt{{v}}_{i}
in

\tilde{\chi}\coloneqq(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi)

is
j^{\prime}\coloneqq\tilde{g}(i)
, whereas in
\chi
it is
j\coloneqq g(i)
. Condition (
6
) is equivalent to the
following: the guard remains unchanged
(i.e.,
j^{\prime}{=}j
), or the new guard
j^{\prime}
has become “available”
by the quantification (i.e.,
j^{\prime}\in S
) or it has not been used as a
guard by
g
(i.e.,
j^{\prime}\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)
).

Example 7.1
.

Let
\varphi
be the
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
-sentence from Example
6.2
.
It is straightforward to see that
(\top\wedge\varphi)
is an equivalent
sentence in
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.

Let
\chi
be the
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
-sentence from Example
6.3
.
It can be shown that
(\top\wedge\chi)
is an equivalent sentence in

\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
(cf. Appendix
C
).

Inductively one obtains for all
\chi\coloneqq(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
that

\operatorname{dom}(g)\ =\ \{i\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}:\texttt{{v}}_{i}\in\operatorname{free}(\chi)\}\ =\ \{i\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}:\texttt{{v}}_{i}\in\operatorname{free}(\psi)\}\,.

(7)

Note that
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}\subseteq\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
. We use the same notions and notation
as for
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
.

Sentences
of
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
are formulas
\chi\coloneqq(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)
in
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
with
\operatorname{free}(\chi)=\varnothing
.
From (
7
) we know that this implies that

\operatorname{dom}(g)=\varnothing
, i.e.,
g=g_{\varnothing}
where
g_{\varnothing}
is
the uniquely defined partial mapping with empty domain; recall that

\Delta_{g_{\varnothing}}=\top
.
For two incidence graphs
I
and
I^{\prime}
we write
I\equiv_{\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}}I^{\prime}
and
say that
I
and
I^{\prime}
are
indistinguishable by the logic
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
if
for all sentences
\chi\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
we have:
I\models\chi

\iff

I^{\prime}\models\chi
.

Let us introduce some shorthands, that will be useful in subsequent sections.
If
(\Delta_{g_{i}}\wedge\psi_{i})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
for

i\in[2]
,
g_{1},g_{2}
are compatible, and
g=g_{1}\cup g_{2}
, we write

•

\big(\Delta_{g}\land(\psi_{1}\lor\psi_{2})\big)
for
\big(\Delta_{g}\land\neg(\neg\psi_{1}\wedge\neg\psi_{2})\big)
, and

•

\big(\Delta_{g}\land(\psi_{1}\rightarrow\psi_{2})\big)
for
\big(\Delta_{g}\land(\neg\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2})\big)
.

If
(\Delta_{g}\land\psi)\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
and
i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\in\operatorname{dom}(g)
with

i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
and
\tilde{g}=g-\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}
and

\bar{\texttt{{v}}}=(\texttt{{v}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{v}}_{i_{\ell}})
, then we write

•

\big(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\land\forall\,\overline{\texttt{{v}}}.(\Delta_{g}\to\psi)\big)
for

\big(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\land\neg\,\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}1}\,\overline{\texttt{{v}}}.(\Delta_{g}\wedge\neg\psi)\big)
and

•

\big(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\land\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$=$}n}\,\overline{\texttt{{v}}}.(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)\big)
for

\big(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\land\big(\,\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n}\,\overline{\texttt{{v}}}.(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)\;\wedge\;\neg\,\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n+1}\,\overline{\texttt{{v}}}.(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)\,\big)\big)
.

And we use the analogous shorthands for formulas quantifying over blue
variables
\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}
.

Two
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
-formulas
\varphi
and
\psi
are said to be
equivalent

(for short:
\varphi\equiv\psi
) if for every interpretation
\mathcal{I}
we
have:
\mathcal{I}\models\varphi

\iff

\mathcal{I}\models\psi
.
This section’s main result shows that every
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
-sentence is
equivalent to an
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
-sentence and, in general,
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
can be be
viewed as a “normal form” for
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
in the following sense:

Theorem 7.2
.

For all formulas
\varphi\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
and all guard functions
f

with
\operatorname{dom}(f)=\textit{ifree}_{R}(\varphi)
, there exists a formula

\varphi_{f}
such that
(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
and

(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi)\ \equiv\ (\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land\varphi))\;=\;\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f}))\,.

In particular, if
\varphi
is a
sentence
in
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
, then for
the empty guard function
f
,

(\top\wedge\varphi_{f})
is a sentence in
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
that is equivalent to
\varphi
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the construction of
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
-formulas.

Base cases
:
For the atomic formula
\varphi\coloneqq\top\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
note that the only
applicable guard function is the function
f
with empty domain and

\Delta_{f}=\top
.
We can choose
\varphi_{f}\coloneqq\neg\,\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}1}(\texttt{{e}}_{1}).(\top\,\wedge\,\neg\,\texttt{{e}}_{1}{=}\texttt{{e}}_{1})
.
It is straightforward to verify that
(\top\land\varphi_{f})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
is a
sentence equivalent to
(\top\land\top)
.

For all the remaining atomic formulas
\varphi\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
we can set
\varphi_{f}\coloneqq\varphi
since, in the base case,
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
does not impose any restrictions on
f
other than
\operatorname{dom}(f)=\textit{ifree}_{R}(\varphi_{f})
.

Induction hypothesis
: The theorem’s statement holds for formulas
\chi,\xi\in\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
.

Inductive step
:

Case 1
:
\varphi
is of the form
\lnot\chi
. By induction hypothesis we have:
(\Delta_{f}\land\chi)\equiv(\Delta_{f}\land\chi_{f})
and

\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land\chi))=\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land\chi_{f}))

and
(\Delta_{f}\land\chi_{f})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
. Then, also

(\Delta_{f}\land\lnot\chi_{f})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
and

\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land\lnot\chi_{f}))=\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land\varphi))
.
We let
\varphi_{f}\coloneqq\lnot\chi_{f}
and claim that

(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi)\equiv(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})
: Consider an arbitrary interpretation
\mathcal{I}
. If
\mathcal{I}\not\models\Delta_{f}
, then
\mathcal{I}\not\models(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi)
and
\mathcal{I}\not\models(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})
. If
\mathcal{I}\models\Delta_{f}
then

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\models(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi)

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{I}\models\varphi

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{I}\not\models\chi

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{I}\not\models(\Delta_{f}\land\chi)

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{I}\not\models(\Delta_{f}\land\chi_{f})

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{I}\not\models\chi_{f}

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{I}\models\varphi_{f}

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{I}\models(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})\,.

Case 2
:
\varphi
is of the form
(\chi\land\xi)
. Let
f_{\chi}
and
f_{\xi}
be the restrictions of
f
to

\textit{ifree}_{R}(\chi)
and
\textit{ifree}_{R}(\xi)
, respectively. It
easily follows from
f=f_{\chi}\cup f_{\xi}
and the
induction hypothesis, that
(\Delta_{f}\land(\chi\land\xi))\equiv(\Delta_{f}\land(\chi_{f_{\chi}}\land\xi_{f_{\xi}}))
and

\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land(\chi\land\xi)))=\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land(\chi_{f_{\chi}}\land\xi_{f_{\xi}})))
.
Thus, we use
\varphi_{f}\coloneqq(\chi_{f_{\chi}}\land\xi_{f_{\xi}})
.
It is straightforward to verify that
(\Delta_{f}\wedge\varphi_{f})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.

Case 3
:
\varphi
is of the form

\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n}(\texttt{{v}}_{i_{1}},\dots,\texttt{{v}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\land\chi)
, where
\operatorname{dom}(g)=\textit{ifree}_{R}(\chi)
and
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
and

i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\in\textit{ifree}_{R}((\Delta_{g}\wedge\chi))
.
Let
f
be an arbitrary guard function with
\operatorname{dom}(f)=\textit{ifree}_{R}(\varphi)
.

Let
S\coloneqq\{i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell}\}
.
Note that
\operatorname{dom}(f)=\operatorname{dom}(g)\setminus S
.
For all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
choose
g^{\prime}(i)\coloneqq f(i)
, and for all
i\in S
choose
g^{\prime}(i)\coloneqq g(i)
(thus,
g^{\prime}=f\cup g
according to the
notation introduced in Section
5
). Let

\varphi_{f}\ \ \coloneqq\ \ \exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n}(\texttt{{v}}_{i_{1}},\dots,\texttt{{v}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land(\Delta_{g}\land\chi_{g^{\prime}})).

It is easy to see that
(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
:
(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land\chi_{g^{\prime}})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
holds by induction hypothesis, and
(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land\Delta_{g})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
can easily be verified using
\operatorname{dom}(g^{\prime})=\operatorname{dom}(g)
. Therefore,
(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land(\Delta_{g}\land\chi_{g^{\prime}}))\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
. Since
\operatorname{dom}(f)=\operatorname{dom}(g^{\prime})\setminus S
it follows from rule
6
that
(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.

It is straightforward to verify that
\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f}))=\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land\varphi))
: The red variables in
\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f}))
are exactly the red variables in

\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{f})
, and these are exactly the red variables
in
\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land\varphi))
.
The blue variables in
\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f}))
are exactly
the blue variables in
\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{f})
or in

\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g^{\prime}})
or in
\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g})
or in

\operatorname{free}(\chi_{g^{\prime}})
. These are exactly the blue variables in
\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{f})
or in

\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g})
or in
\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\wedge\chi_{g^{\prime}}))
. By
induction hypothesis, these are exactly the blue variables in
\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{f})
or in

\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g})
or in
\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\wedge\chi))
. These,
in turn, are exactly the blue variables in
\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{f})
or in

\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{g}\wedge\chi))
or in

\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g^{\prime}})
. By definition of
g^{\prime}
we have

\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g^{\prime}})\subseteq\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{f})\cup\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g})
. Thus,
the blue variables in
\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{f})
or in

\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{g}\wedge\chi))
or in

\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g^{\prime}})
are exactly the blue variables in
\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{f})
or in

\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{g}\wedge\chi))
; and these are exactly the blue
variables in
\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\wedge\varphi))
.

All that remains to be done is to verify that
(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})\equiv(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi)
: Consider an arbitrary interpretation
\mathcal{I}\coloneqq(I,\beta)
.
If
\mathcal{I}\not\models\Delta_{f}
, then
\mathcal{I}\not\models(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi)
and
\mathcal{I}\not\models(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})
. If
\mathcal{I}\models\Delta_{f}
,
then
\mathcal{I}\models(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi)

\iff

\mathcal{I}\models\varphi
, and
\mathcal{I}\models(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})

\iff

\mathcal{I}\models\varphi_{f}
.
We show that
\mathcal{I}\models\varphi

\iff

\mathcal{I}\models\varphi_{f}
.
For this it suffices to show that
for all
\overline{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell})\in R(I)^{\ell}
we have
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{a}}\models(\Delta_{g}\land\chi)

\iff

\mathcal{I}^{\overline{a}}\models(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land(\Delta_{g}\land\chi_{g^{\prime}}))
,
where
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{a}}\coloneqq(I,\beta^{\prime})
with

\beta^{\prime}(\texttt{{v}}_{i_{j}})=a_{j}
for all
j\in[\ell]
and

\beta^{\prime}(\texttt{{x}})=\beta(\texttt{{x}})
for all variables
\texttt{{x}}\in\textsf{Var}\setminus\{\texttt{{v}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{v}}_{i_{\ell}}\}
.
Recall that
\mathcal{I}\models\Delta_{f}
and
\operatorname{dom}(f)=\operatorname{dom}(g)\setminus S=\operatorname{dom}(g^{\prime})\setminus S
. Thus, also
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{a}}\models\Delta_{f}
.

For “
\Longrightarrow
” assume that
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{a}}\models(\Delta_{g}\land\chi)
. Since for all
i\in S
we have
g^{\prime}(i)=g(i)
and, furthermore,
g^{\prime}=f\cup g
and

\mathcal{I}^{\overline{a}}\models\Delta_{f}
, it also holds that

\mathcal{I}^{\overline{a}}\models(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land\chi)
. Thus, by induction hypothesis it holds that

\mathcal{I}^{\overline{a}}\models(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land\chi_{g^{\prime}})
. Hence, we have

\mathcal{I}^{\overline{a}}\models(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land(\Delta_{g}\land\chi_{g^{\prime}}))
.

For “
\Longleftarrow
” assume that
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{a}}\models(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land(\Delta_{g}\land\chi_{g^{\prime}}))
. By induction hypothesis we get

\mathcal{I}^{\overline{a}}\models(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land\chi)
. Hence,
we have
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{a}}\models(\Delta_{g}\land\chi)
.

In summary, we have shown that
(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi)\equiv(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})
; this completes Case 3.

Case 4
:
\varphi
is of the form

\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n}(\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\dots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\land\chi)
, where
\operatorname{dom}(g)=\textit{ifree}_{R}(\chi)
and
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
and

i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\in\textit{ifree}_{B}((\Delta_{g}\wedge\chi))
.
Let
f
be an arbitrary guard function with
\operatorname{dom}(f)=\textit{ifree}_{R}(\varphi)
.
Note that
\operatorname{dom}(f)=\operatorname{dom}(g)
.

Let
S\coloneqq\{i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell}\}
.
Let
g^{\prime}
be the
guard function with
\operatorname{dom}(g^{\prime})=\operatorname{dom}(f)=\operatorname{dom}(g)
and

g^{\prime}(i)\ \coloneqq\ \begin{cases}g(i),&\text{if }f(i)\in S\text{ or }f(i)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)\\
f(i),&\text{otherwise, i.e. }f(i)\not\in S\text{ and }f(i)\in\operatorname{img}(g).\end{cases}

Let

\varphi_{f}\quad\coloneqq\quad\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n}(\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\dots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land(\Delta_{g}\land\chi_{g^{\prime}}))\,.

Claim 1:

(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi)\equiv(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})
.

Proof of Claim 1
: Consider an arbitrary interpretation
\mathcal{I}=(I,\beta)
. If
\mathcal{I}\not\models\Delta_{f}
, then
\mathcal{I}\not\models(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi)
and
\mathcal{I}\not\models(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})
.
If
\mathcal{I}\models\Delta_{f}
, then
\mathcal{I}\models(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi)

\iff

\mathcal{I}\models\varphi
, and
\mathcal{I}\models(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})

\iff

\mathcal{I}\models\varphi_{f}
. We show that
\mathcal{I}\models\varphi
iff
\mathcal{I}\models\varphi_{f}
.

For this it suffices to show that for all
\overline{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{\ell})\in B(I)^{\ell}

we have
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{u}}\models(\Delta_{g}\land\chi)

\iff

\mathcal{I}^{\overline{u}}\models(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land(\Delta_{g}\land\chi_{g^{\prime}}))
, where
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{u}}=(I,\beta^{\prime})
with

\beta^{\prime}(\texttt{{e}}_{i_{j}})=u_{j}
for all
j\in[\ell]
and
\beta^{\prime}(\texttt{{x}})=\beta(\texttt{{x}})
for all variables
\texttt{{x}}\in\textsf{Var}\setminus\{\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}\}
.

For “
\Longleftarrow
” assume that
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{u}}\models(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land(\Delta_{g}\land\chi_{g^{\prime}}))
. In particular,

\mathcal{I}^{\overline{u}}\models(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land\chi_{g^{\prime}})
. By
induction hypothesis we obtain:
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{u}}\models(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land\chi)
. Hence, we have
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{u}}\models(\Delta_{g}\land\chi)
.

For “
\Longrightarrow
” assume that
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{u}}\models(\Delta_{g}\land\chi)
. It suffices to show that

\mathcal{I}^{\overline{u}}\models\Delta_{g^{\prime}}
, because then we can proceed
as follows: We then have
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{u}}\models(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land\chi)
, and hence the
induction hypothesis yields that
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{u}}\models(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land\chi_{g^{\prime}})
, and thus
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{u}}\models(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land(\Delta_{g}\land\chi_{g^{\prime}}))
.

All that remains to be done is to prove that
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{u}}\models\Delta_{g^{\prime}}
.
We know that
\mathcal{I}\models\Delta_{f}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{u}}\models\Delta_{g}
. We have to show that
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{u}}\models E(\texttt{{e}}_{g^{\prime}(i)},\texttt{{v}}_{i})
for every
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g^{\prime})
. Consider an
arbitrary
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g^{\prime})
. If
g^{\prime}(i)=g(i)
we are done since
\mathcal{I}^{\overline{u}}\models\Delta_{g}
. If

g^{\prime}(i)\neq g(i)
then, by definition of
g^{\prime}
we have:
g^{\prime}(i)=f(i)\not\in S
.
We are done
since
\beta^{\prime}(\texttt{{e}}_{g^{\prime}(i)})=\beta^{\prime}(\texttt{{e}}_{f(i)})=\beta(\texttt{{e}}_{f(i)})
and
\mathcal{I}\models\Delta_{f}
.
∎
Claim 1

Claim 2
:
(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.

Proof of Claim 2
: By induction hypothesis we have

(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land\chi_{g^{\prime}})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
. It is easy to see that

(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land\Delta_{g})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
, and hence also

(\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\land(\Delta_{g}\land\chi_{g^{\prime}}))\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
. We
want to apply rule
7
of the inductive definition
of
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
to show that
(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
. For
this we have to show that all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g^{\prime})
satisfy:

f(i)=g^{\prime}(i)\quad\text{or}\quad f(i)\in S\quad\text{or}\quad f(i)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g^{\prime})\,.

Assume for contradiction, that there exists an
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g^{\prime})
such that:

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad f(i)\neq g^{\prime}(i)

(8)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\qquad\qquad f(i)\not\in S

(9)

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad\;f(i)\in\operatorname{img}(g^{\prime}).

(10)

Then, by definition of
g^{\prime}
we must have
g^{\prime}(i)=g(i)
and

f(i)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g).

(11)

By (
10
)
there exists a
j\in\operatorname{dom}(g^{\prime})
such that
f(i)=g^{\prime}(j)
; and
since
f(i)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)
it must hold that
g^{\prime}(j)\neq g(j)
(because otherwise
f(i)=g^{\prime}(j)=g(j)\in\operatorname{img}(g)
,
which is obviously contradictory).

From
g^{\prime}(j)\neq g(j)
and
the definition of
g^{\prime}
it follows that
g^{\prime}(j)=f(j)
. Thus,

f(i)=g^{\prime}(j)=f(j)
, i.e.
f(i)=f(j)
. Thus, from
(
11
) we obtain
that
f(j)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)
. This means that by
definition of
g^{\prime}
we have
g^{\prime}(j)=g(j)
. This is a
contradiction, since we already know that
g^{\prime}(j)\neq g(j)
.

In summary, we obtain that such an
i
cannot exist, i.e. all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g^{\prime})
satisfy the conditions imposed by rule
7
. Hence,
(\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.
∎
Claim 2

Claim 3:

\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land\varphi))\ =\ \operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f}))
.

Proof of Claim 3
:
The red variables in
\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land\varphi))
are exactly
the red variables in
\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{f})
, and these are exactly the red
variables in
\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f}))
.

The blue variables in
\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{f}\land\varphi_{f}))
are exactly
the blue variables in
\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{f})
or in
\operatorname{free}(\varphi_{f})
.
Let
V\coloneqq\{\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}\}
.
Note that
\operatorname{free}(\varphi_{f})=\big(\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g})\cup\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\wedge\chi_{g^{\prime}}))\big)\setminus V
.
By induction hypothesis,
\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\wedge\chi_{g^{\prime}}))=\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\wedge\chi))
. Hence,

\displaystyle\operatorname{free}(\varphi_{f})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g})\cup\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{g^{\prime}}\wedge\chi))\big)\setminus V

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{g}\wedge\chi))\cup\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g^{\prime}})\big)\setminus V

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{g}\wedge\chi))\setminus V\big)\cup\big(\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g^{\prime}})\setminus V\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{free}(\varphi)\cup\big(\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g^{\prime}})\setminus V\big).

To complete the proof it suffices to show that every blue variable in

\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g^{\prime}})\setminus V

belongs to
\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{f})\cup\operatorname{free}(\varphi)
.
Blue variables in
\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{g^{\prime}})\setminus V
are of the form

\texttt{{e}}_{g^{\prime}(i)}
with
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g^{\prime})=\operatorname{dom}(g)=\operatorname{dom}(f)
and
g^{\prime}(i)\not\in S
. By the
definition of
g^{\prime}
we know that
g^{\prime}(i)=f(i)
or
g^{\prime}(i)=g(i)
.

If
g^{\prime}(i)=f(i)
then

\texttt{{e}}_{g^{\prime}(i)}=\texttt{{e}}_{f(i)}\in\operatorname{free}(\Delta_{f})
, and we are done.

If
g^{\prime}(i)=g(i)
then
g(i)\not\in S
, and hence

\texttt{{e}}_{g^{\prime}(i)}=\texttt{{e}}_{g(i)}\in\operatorname{free}(\varphi)
, and we are done.
∎
Claim 3

This completes the proof of Theorem
7.2
.∎

8
An Inductive Characterisation of
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}

In this section we give an inductive definition of what we call

guarded
k
-labeled incidence graphs
, and we prove that these are
equivalent to the incidence graphs of entangled hypertree width
\leqslant k
.
Throughout this section, we fix an arbitrary number
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
.

8.1
k
-Labeled Incidence Graphs and the Class
\textsl{GLI}_{k}

We enrich an incidence graph
I
by labeling some of its blue nodes with labels
in
[k]
, by labeling some of its red nodes with labels in
\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
, and by providing,
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
that is used as a label for a red node,
a “blue label”
g(i)\in[k]
that should be regarded as “the guard” of
i
.
Each label can only be
used once,
not all labels have to be used, not all vertices have to be labeled,
one vertex may have multiple labels, and “guards” can be chosen
arbitrarily.
This is formalised as follows.

Definition 8.1
.

A
k
-labeled incidence graph

L=(I,r,b,g)
consists of an
incidence graph
I
and partial mappings

r:\mathbb{N}_{\geqslant 1}\rightharpoonup R(I)
,

b:[k]\rightharpoonup B(I)
, and

g:\mathbb{N}_{\geqslant 1}\rightharpoonup[k]
such that

\operatorname{dom}(g)=\operatorname{dom}(r)
is finite.

We write
I_{L},r_{L},b_{L},g_{L}
to address
L
’s components
I,r,b,g
.

Let
L=(I,r,b,g)
be a
k
-labeled incidence graph.
If
j\in\operatorname{dom}(b)
, then the blue node
b(j)
of
I
is labeled with the number
j
.
