If
i\in\operatorname{dom}(r)
, then the red node
r(i)
of
I
is labeled with the
number
i
, and
g(i)=j
indicates that the blue node labeled with the
number
j
(if it exists) should be regarded as “the guard” of the
red node labeled with the number
i
.

We say that
L

has real guards
if for every
i\in\operatorname{dom}(r)

the red node
v
labeled
i
is “guarded” by the blue node
e

labeled
j\coloneqq g(i)
in the sense that
I
contains an edge from
e
to
v
.
This is formalised in the following definition.

Definition 8.2
.

A
k
-labeled incidence graph
L=(I,r,b,g)
is said to

have real guards w.r.t.
f
for a partial function
f:\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}\rightharpoonup[k]

if

\operatorname{dom}(f)\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(r)
and
for all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
we have
f(i)\in\operatorname{dom}(b)
and
(b(f(i)),r(i))\in E(I)
.

We say that
L

has real guards
if it has real guards w.r.t.
g
.

Particularly simple examples of
k
-labeled incidence graphs with real guards are provided by the following definition.

Definition 8.3
.

Let
f:\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}\rightharpoonup[k]
with finite, non-empty
\operatorname{dom}(f)
. The
k
-labeled incidence graph
M_{f}

defined by
f
is
the
k
-labeled incidence graph

L=(I,r,b,g)
with
g\coloneqq f
,
where
I
consists of a red node
v_{i}
for every
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
, a
blue node
e_{j}
for every
j\in\operatorname{img}(f)
, and an edge
(e_{f(i)},v_{i})

for every
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
, and where
\operatorname{dom}(r)=\operatorname{dom}(f)
and
r(i)=v_{i}

for all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(r)
, and
\operatorname{dom}(b)=\operatorname{img}(f)
and
b(j)=e_{j}
for all

j\in\operatorname{dom}(b)
.
Note that
M_{f}
has real guards.

We introduce a number of operations on
k
-labeled incidence graphs. The
first kind of operations provides ways to modify the labels (the latter
two of these do not necessarily preserve real guards).

Definition 8.4

(Changing labels)
.

Let
L=(I,r,b,g)
be a
k
-labeled incidence graph.

Let
{X_{r}}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{\geqslant 1}
be finite, and let
{X_{b}}\subseteq[k]
.

1.

Removing from the red nodes all the labels in
{X_{r}}
is achieved by
the operation

L[{X_{r}}{\to}\bullet]\coloneqq(I,r^{\prime},b,g^{\prime})
with
r^{\prime}\coloneqq r-{X_{r}}
and
g^{\prime}\coloneqq g-{X_{r}}
.

2.

Removing from the blue nodes all the labels in
{X_{b}}
is
achieved by the operation

L\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle\coloneqq(I,r,b^{\prime},g)
with
b^{\prime}\coloneqq b-{X_{b}}
.

3.

Let
{X_{r}}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}
for
\ell\coloneqq|{X_{r}}|
and
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
.

For every
\overline{v}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell})\in R(I)^{\ell}
we let

L[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}]\coloneqq(I,r^{\prime},b,g)
with

\operatorname{dom}(r^{\prime})=\operatorname{dom}(r)\cup{X_{r}}

and
r^{\prime}(i_{j})=v_{j}
for all
j\in[\ell]
and
r^{\prime}(i)=r(i)
for all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(r)\setminus{X_{r}}

(i.e., for each
j\in[\ell]
, the red label
i_{j}
is moved onto the
red node
v_{j}
, and all other labels remain unchanged).

4.

Let
{X_{b}}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}
for
\ell\coloneqq|{X_{b}}|
and
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
.

For every
\overline{e}=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell})\in B(I)^{\ell}
we let

L\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle\coloneqq(I,r,b^{\prime},g)
with

\operatorname{dom}(b^{\prime})=\operatorname{dom}(b)\cup{X_{b}}

and
b^{\prime}(i_{j})=e_{j}
for all
j\in[\ell]
and
b^{\prime}(i)=b(i)
for all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(b)\setminus{X_{b}}

(i.e., for each
j\in[\ell]
, the blue label
i_{j}
is moved onto the blue node
e_{j}
, and all other labels remain unchanged).

The next operation enables us to
glue
two
k
-labeled
incidence graphs
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
. This is achieved by first taking the
disjoint union of
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
and then merging all red
(blue) nodes that carry the same label into a single red (blue) node
that inherits all neighbours of the merged nodes; we write

(L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2})
to denote the resulting
k
-labeled incidence graph.
This is achieved by the following formal definition.

Definition 8.5

(Glueing
k
-labeled incidence graphs)
.

Let
L_{i}\coloneqq({I}_{i},r_{i},b_{i},g_{i})

be a
k
-labeled incidence graph for
i\in[2]
. The
glueing
operation
produces the
k
-labeled incidence graph

(L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2})\coloneqq({I},r,b,g)
in the following
way:

Let
{I}^{\prime}\coloneqq{I}_{1}\mathbin{\dot{\cup}}{I}_{2}
be
the disjoint union of
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
.
I.e.,
R(I^{\prime})=\bigcup_{i\in[2]}(R(I_{i})\times\{i\})
,

B(I^{\prime})=\bigcup_{i\in[2]}(B(I_{i})\times\{i\})
,

E(I^{\prime})=\bigcup_{i\in[2]}\{\big((e,i),(v,i)\big):(e,v)\in E(I_{i})\}
.

Let
\sim_{R}
be the equivalence relation on
R(I^{\prime})
obtained as the reflexive, symmetric, and transitive closure of the relation

\big\{\big((r_{1}(j),1),(r_{2}(j),2)\big):j\in\operatorname{dom}(r_{1})\cap\operatorname{dom}(r_{2})\big\}
.

Let
[v]_{\sim_{R}}
denote the equivalence class of each
v\in R(I^{\prime})
.

We let
R(I)\coloneqq\{[v]_{\sim_{R}}:v\in R(I^{\prime})\}
.

Let
\sim_{B}
be the equivalence relation on
B(I^{\prime})
obtained as the reflexive, symmetric, and transitive closure of the relation

\big\{\big((b_{1}(j),1),(b_{2}(j),2)\big):j\in\operatorname{dom}(b_{1})\cap\operatorname{dom}(b_{2})\big\}
.

Let
[e]_{\sim_{B}}
denote the equivalence class of each
e\in B(I^{\prime})
.

We let
B(I)\coloneqq\{[e]_{\sim_{B}}:e\in B(I^{\prime})\}
.

E(I)\coloneqq\big\{\big([(e,i)]_{\sim_{B}}\,,\,[(v,i)]_{\sim_{R}}\big):i\in[2],\ (e,v)\in E(I_{i})\big\}
.

Let
I=(R(I),B(I),E(I))
.

Let
r:\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}\rightharpoonup R(I)
with
\operatorname{dom}(r)=\operatorname{dom}(r_{1})\cup\operatorname{dom}(r_{2})
be defined by

r(j)\coloneqq[(r_{1}(j),1)]_{\sim_{R}}
for all
j\in\operatorname{dom}(r_{1})
and

r(j)\coloneqq[(r_{2}(j),2)]_{\sim_{R}}
for all
j\in\operatorname{dom}(r_{2})\setminus\operatorname{dom}(r_{1})
.

Let
b:\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}\rightharpoonup B(I)
with
\operatorname{dom}(b)=\operatorname{dom}(b_{1})\cup\operatorname{dom}(b_{2})
be defined by

b(j)\coloneqq[(b_{1}(j),1)]_{\sim_{B}}
for all
j\in\operatorname{dom}(b_{1})
and

b(j)\coloneqq[(b_{2}(j),2)]_{\sim_{B}}
for all
j\in\operatorname{dom}(b_{2})\setminus\operatorname{dom}(b_{1})
.

Let
g\coloneqq g_{1}\cup g_{2}
(recall from
Section
5
that
this means that
g(j)=g_{1}(j)
for all
j\in\operatorname{dom}(g_{1})
and
g(j)=g_{2}(j)
for all

j\in\operatorname{dom}(g_{2})\setminus\operatorname{dom}(g_{1})
).

Finally, we let
(L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2})\coloneqq({I},r,b,g)
.

For
i\in[2]
we define mappings

\pi_{i,R}:R(I_{i})\to R(I)
and

\pi_{i,B}:B(I_{i})\to B(I)
by

\pi_{i,R}(v)\coloneqq[(v,i)]_{\sim_{R}}
for all
v\in R(I_{i})
and

\pi_{i,B}(e)\coloneqq[(e,i)]_{\sim B}
for all
e\in B(I_{i})
.
Note that
\pi_{i,R}(v)
is the red node of

I_{(L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2})}
that corresponds to
v\in R(I_{i})
, and

\pi_{i,B}(e)
is the blue node of

I_{(L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2})}
that corresponds to
e\in B(I_{i})
.

The following lemma’s proof is straightforward (see Appendix
D.1
).

Lemma 8.6
.

If
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are
k
-labeled incidence graphs with real guards, then also

(L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2})
has real guards.

We need one further operation on
k
-labeled incidence graphs, namely,
one that admits us to change its guard function.
This is provided by the following definitions.

Definition 8.7

(Transition)
.

Consider a partial function
g:\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}\rightharpoonup[k]
.
A
transition for
g
is a partial function
f:\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}\rightharpoonup[k]

with
\varnothing\neq\operatorname{dom}(f)\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(g)

satisfying the following:
for every
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g)
with
g(i)\in\operatorname{img}(f)
we have
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
.

Definition 8.8

(Applying a transition)
.

Let
L=(I,r,b,g)
be a
k
-labeled incidence graph
and let
f
be a transition for
g
.
We can
apply
the transition
f

to
L
and obtain the

k
-labeled incidence graph

L[{\leadsto}f]\coloneqq(\,M_{f}\,\mathbin{\cdot}\;L\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle\,)
,
where
{X_{b}}\coloneqq\operatorname{img}(g)\cap\operatorname{img}(f)\cap\operatorname{dom}(b)

and
M_{f}
is provided by Definition
8.3
.

The idea of applying a transition
f
to a
k
-labeled incidence graph

L=(I,r,b,g)
is to assign new guards to a set of
labeled red vertices (i.e. the domain of
f
). These new guards
should by newly inserted nodes, and they should
be
real
guards. To this end, for every
j\in\operatorname{img}(f)
we add a new blue
node
e^{\prime}_{j}
labeled
j
; and in case that the label
j
had already
been used by a blue node
e
of
L
(i.e.,
j\in\operatorname{dom}(b)
)

and
served as a guard according to
g
(i.e.,
j\in\operatorname{img}(g)
), we
remove this label from
e
.
For each
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
with
f(i)=j
we add an edge from the red
node of
L
labeled
i
to the new blue node
e^{\prime}_{j}
.

The formal definition
L[{\leadsto}f]\coloneqq(M_{f}\mathbin{\cdot}L\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle)

achieves this as follows:
By
L\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle
we release from
L
all blue labels
j

that are present in
L
and that we want to assign to newly created
nodes. This is achieved by letting

{X_{b}}=\operatorname{img}(g)\cap\operatorname{img}(f)\cap\operatorname{dom}(b)
.
Afterwards, adding the edges from the nodes of
L
that carry a
red label
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
to the new blue node
e^{\prime}_{f(i)}
is achieved
by glueing
M_{f}
to
L\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle
.

Note that releasing from
L
all blue labels in
{X_{b}}
might be
problematic:
Consider a red node
v
labeled
i
that
was originally guarded by the blue node
e
of
L
that carried the label

j\coloneqq g(i)
. Releasing the label
j
from node
e
means that
v

loses its guard in case that
i\not\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
.
Therefore, for
f
to be a transition for

g
, we require in Definition
8.7
that it assigns a new guard to all the affected
labeled red vertices, i.e. we require
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
, if
g(i)\in\operatorname{img}(f)
.

The next lemma’s proof is straightforward.

Lemma 8.9
.

If
L
is a
k
-labeled incidence graph with real guards and
f
is a transition for

g_{L}
, then
L[{\leadsto}f]
has real guards.

Now we have available all the tools needed to define the class
\textsl{GLI}_{k}

of
guarded
k
-labeled incidence graphs
.

Definition 8.10

(
\textsl{GLI}_{k}
)
.

The class
\textsl{GLI}_{k}
of

guarded
k
-labeled incidence graphs
is inductively defined as follows.

Base case
:

1.

Any
k
-labeled incidence graph
L=(I,r,b,g)

with
R({I})=\operatorname{img}(r)
,
B({I})=\operatorname{img}(b)
, and
with real guards belongs to
\textsl{GLI}_{k}
.

Inductive cases
:
Let
L=(I,r,b,g)\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
.

2.

L[{X_{r}}{\to}\bullet]\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
for every

{X_{r}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(r)
.

3.

L\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
for every

{X_{b}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(b)\setminus\operatorname{img}(g)
.

4.

L[{\leadsto}f]\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
for every transition
f
for
g
.

5.

(L\mathbin{\cdot}L^{\prime})\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
for every

L^{\prime}=(I^{\prime},r^{\prime},b^{\prime},g^{\prime})\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
such that
g
and
g^{\prime}
are
compatible.

By induction, and using the
Lemma
8.6
and

8.9
, one easily obtains:

Lemma 8.11
.

Every
L\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
has real guards.

8.2
\textsl{GLI}_{k}
Corresponds to
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}

Recall from Section
2
that

\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
is the class of all incidence graphs
I
with
\operatorname{ehw}(I)\leqslant k
, where

\operatorname{ehw}(I)
is
I
’s entangled hypertree width (cf. Definition
2.6
).
This subsection is devoted to proving that
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
is characterised
by
\textsl{GLI}_{k}
(Theorem
8.12
). A
k
-labeled incidence
graph
L
is called
label-free
if
\operatorname{dom}(r_{L})=\operatorname{dom}(b_{L})=\operatorname{dom}(g_{L})=\varnothing
.

Theorem 8.12
.

1.

The incidence graph
I_{L}
of every
L\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
is in
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
.

2.

For every incidence graph
I\in\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
there exists a label-free

L\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
such that
I\cong I_{L}
.

Proof idea.

1
:
By induction on the definition of
\textsl{GLI}_{k}
we show for every

L\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
that there exists an
ehd
D=(T,\textit{bag},\textit{cover})
of
I_{L}
of width
\leqslant k
.
To be able to carry out the induction step, we additionally ensure
that there is a tree-node
\omega\in V(T)
with

\textit{cover}(\omega)=\operatorname{img}(b_{L})
.
The idea is fairly simple: From Lemma
8.11

we know that
L
has real guards. Thus, the (at most
k
) blue
labels given by
b
guard all the red labels given by
r
, i.e.,

(b({g(i)}),r(i))\in E(I)
for every
i\in\operatorname{dom}(r)
. Hence, we
can use the set of labeled blue vertices to cover the bag containing
all the labeled red vertices.
See Appendix
D.2
for a detailed proof.

2
:
Given an
I\in\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
, the proof proceeds as follows.
We pick an ehd
D=(T,\textit{bag},\textit{cover})
of
I
of width
\leqslant k
that
has a particularly suitable shape, namely, there exists
a tree-node
\omega
such that the rooted tree
(T,\omega)

is binary (i.e., every node has at most 2 children) and monotone
(i.e., for all parent-child pairs
(t_{p},t_{c})
we have

|\textit{cover}(t_{p})|\geqslant|\textit{cover}(t_{c})|
).

For every
t\in V(T)
, the information provided by
\textit{cover}(t)
is viewed
as a description of a guarded
k
-labeled incidence graph
L_{t}

corresponding to the base case of
Definition
8.10
.
We then perform a bottom-up traversal of the rooted tree
(T,\omega)
and glue
together all the
L_{t}
’s.
But this has to be done with care: before glueing them, we have to
ensure that the guards are compatible; we achieve this by adequately
changing labels and applying transitions, so that finally we end up
with an
L\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
whose incidence graph
I_{L}
is isomorphic
to
I
.
Appendix
D.3
provides a detailed description
of the construction;
a specific example of the construction can be found
in Appendix
D.5
.
∎

8.3
Homomorphisms on
k
-Labeled Incidence Graphs

We define the notion of
homomorphisms
of
k
-labeled incidence
graphs in such a way that it respects labels, but ignores the guard
function.

Definition 8.13
.

Let
L=(I,r,b,g)
and
L^{\prime}=(I^{\prime},r^{\prime},b^{\prime},g^{\prime})
be
k
-labeled incidence graphs.
If
\operatorname{dom}(r)\not\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(r^{\prime})
or
\operatorname{dom}(b)\not\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(b^{\prime})
,
then there exists no homomorphism from
L
to
L^{\prime}
.
Otherwise, a homomorphism from
L
to
L^{\prime}
is a homomorphism
h=(h_{R},h_{B})
from
I
to

I^{\prime}
satisfying the following condition:

h(r(i))=r^{\prime}(i)
for all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(r)
and

h(b(j))=b^{\prime}(j)
for all
j\in\operatorname{dom}(b)
.

By
\operatorname{Hom}(L,L^{\prime})
we denote the set of all homomorphisms from
L
to
L^{\prime}
,
and we let
\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime}):=|\operatorname{Hom}(L,L^{\prime})|
be the number of
homomorphisms from
L
to
L^{\prime}
. In particular, if
L
is

label-free
, then
\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime})=\operatorname{hom}(I_{L},I_{L^{\prime}})
.

In order to enable us to “aggregate” homomorphism counts, we
proceed in a similar way as Dvořák
[
12
]
: we use a variant
of the

quantum graphs
of Lovász and Szegedy
[
21
]
, tailored towards our setting.
We say that
k
-labeled incidence graphs
L_{1},\ldots,L_{d}
are
compatible
if
their labeling functions all have the same domain and they all have the same guard function, i.e.,

\operatorname{dom}(r_{L_{1}})=\operatorname{dom}(r_{L_{i}})
,
\operatorname{dom}(b_{L_{1}})=\operatorname{dom}(b_{L_{i}})
, and
g_{L_{1}}=g_{L_{i}}
for all
i\in[d]
.

Definition 8.14
.

A
k
-labeled quantum incidence graph

Q
is a formal finite
non-empty linear combination
with real coefficients of compatible
k
-labeled incidence graphs. We represent a
k
-labeled quantum incidence graph
Q
as

\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}\,L_{i}
,
where
d\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
,
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
L_{i}
is a
k
-labeled incidence graph for
i\in[d]
.
We let

\textup{dr}_{Q}\coloneqq\operatorname{dom}(r_{L_{1}})=\cdots=\operatorname{dom}(r_{L_{d}})
,

\textup{db}_{Q}\coloneqq\operatorname{dom}(b_{L_{1}})=\cdots=\operatorname{dom}(b_{L_{d}})
, and

g_{Q}\coloneqq g_{L_{1}}=\cdots=g_{L_{d}}
.
The
\alpha_{i}
’s and
L_{i}
’s are called the
coefficients
and

components
, respectively, and
d
is called the
degree

of
Q
.

Note that a
k
-labeled incidence graph is a
k
-labeled quantum incidence graph with
degree 1 and coefficient 1.

For a
k
-labeled quantum incidence graph
Q=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}L_{i}
and an arbitrary
k
-labeled incidence graph
L^{\prime}
we let

\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime})\ \ \coloneqq\ \ \sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}{\cdot}\operatorname{hom}(L_{i},L^{\prime})\ \ \ \in\ \mathbb{R}.

We adapt the operations for
k
-labeled incidence graphs to their
quantum equivalent in the expected way:

\displaystyle Q[{X_{r}}{\to}\bullet]

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}L_{i}[{X_{r}}{\to}\bullet]\,,

\displaystyle Q\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}L_{i}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle\,,

\displaystyle Q[{\leadsto}f]

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}L_{i}[{\leadsto}f]\,.

Glueing two
k
-labeled quantum incidence graphs
Q=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}\,L_{i}
and
Q^{\prime}=\sum_{j=1}^{d^{\prime}}\alpha^{\prime}_{j}\,L^{\prime}_{j}
is achieved by pairwise glueing of their components and multiplication of their respective coefficients, i.e.

(Q\mathbin{\cdot}Q^{\prime})\ \coloneqq\ \ \sum_{i\in[d]\atop j\in[d^{\prime}]}(\alpha_{i}{\cdot}\alpha^{\prime}_{j})\;(L_{i}\mathbin{\cdot}L^{\prime}_{j})\,.

The following can easily be proved for the case where
Q,Q^{\prime}
have
degree 1 and coefficient 1 (i.e.,
Q,Q^{\prime}
are
k
-labeled
incidence graphs), and
then be generalised to quantum incidence graphs by simple linear
arguments (for proof details see
Appendix
D.6
).

Lemma 8.15
.

For all
k
-labeled quantum incidence graphs
Q,Q^{\prime}
and all
k
-labeled incidence graphs
L
we have:

1.

\operatorname{hom}((Q\mathbin{\cdot}Q^{\prime}),L)=\operatorname{hom}(Q,L)\mathbin{\cdot}\operatorname{hom}(Q^{\prime},L)
.

2.

\operatorname{hom}(Q[{X_{r}}{\to}\bullet],L)=\sum_{\overline{v}\in R({I}_{L})^{\ell}}\operatorname{hom}(Q,L[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}])

for all
{X_{r}}\subseteq\textup{dr}_{Q}
and
\ell\coloneqq|{X_{r}}|
.

3.

\operatorname{hom}(Q\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle,L)=\sum_{\overline{e}\in B({I}_{L})^{\ell}}\operatorname{hom}(Q,L\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)

for all
{X_{b}}\subseteq\textup{db}_{Q}
and
\ell\coloneqq|{X_{b}}|
.

4.

\operatorname{hom}(Q[{\leadsto}f],L)=\operatorname{hom}(M_{f},L)\cdot\sum_{\overline{e}\in B(I_{L})^{\ell}}\operatorname{hom}(Q,L\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)

for all transitions
f
for
g_{Q}
, for

{X_{b}}\coloneqq\textup{db}_{Q}\cap\operatorname{img}(f)\cap\operatorname{img}(g)

and
\ell\coloneqq|{X_{b}}|
.
Note that
\operatorname{hom}(M_{f},L)\in\{0,1\}
.

The class
\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
of
guarded
k
-labeled quantum incidence graphs
consists of those

k
-labeled quantum incidence graphs where all components belong to
\textsl{GLI}_{k}
.

The following lemma was provided for series-parallel quantum graphs by
Lovász and Szegedy
[
21
]
and for labeled quantum graphs of
tree-width

\leqslant k
by Dvořák
[
12
]
; their proof also works for
labeled quantum incidence graphs in
\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
.

Lemma 8.16
.

Let
X,Y\subseteq\mathbb{N}
be disjoint and finite, and let
Q\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
. There exists a

Q[X,Y]\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}

with the
same parameters
\textup{dr}_{Q},\textup{db}_{Q},g_{Q}
as
Q
,
such that for all
k
-labeled incidence graphs
L

with
real guards w.r.t.
g_{Q}

we have:

1.

\operatorname{hom}(Q,L)\in X\implies\operatorname{hom}(Q[X,Y],L)=0
,

2.

\operatorname{hom}(Q,L)\in Y\implies\operatorname{hom}(Q[X,Y],L)=1
.

Proof idea.

Pick a polynomial

p(x)
such that
p(z)=0
for all
z\in X
and
p(z)=1
for all
z\in Y
. If

p(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}a_{i}x^{i}
, one chooses

Q[X,Y]\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{d}a_{i}Q^{i}
,
where
Q^{i}
is the result of glueing
i
copies of
Q
together, and

Q^{0}\coloneqq M_{g_{Q}}
.
∎

9
Proof of Theorem
6.1

Finally, we have available all the machinery so that, from a
high-level point of view, our
proof of Theorem
6.1
can follow a similar
approach as Dvořák’s proof in
[
12
]
. The technical details,
however, are quite intricate because the concept of generalised
hypertree width (as well as the classes
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
and
\textsl{GLI}_{k}
) is much
more complicated than the concept of tree-width.
Analogously to two main lemmas in
[
12
]
, we provide a key
lemma for each of the directions “
\Longleftarrow
” and
“
\Longrightarrow
” of Theorem
6.1
formulated
for
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
instead of
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
.
These lemmas use the following notion:
The
interpretation

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}
associated with a

k
-labeled incidence graph
L^{\prime}
is an interpretation
(I,\beta)

with
I\coloneqq I_{L^{\prime}}
and

\beta(\texttt{{v}}_{i})\coloneqq r_{L^{\prime}}(i)
for all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(r_{L^{\prime}})
and

\beta(\texttt{{e}}_{j})\coloneqq b_{L^{\prime}}(j)
for all

j\in\operatorname{dom}(b_{L^{\prime}})
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
L=(I,b,r,g)\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
. For every
m\in\mathbb{N}
there is a formula

\varphi_{L,m}
with
(\Delta_{g}\wedge\varphi_{L,m})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
and

\operatorname{free}((\Delta_{g}\wedge\varphi_{L,m}))=\{\texttt{{v}}_{i}:i\in\operatorname{dom}(r)\}\cup\{\texttt{{e}}_{j}:j\in\operatorname{dom}(b)\}

such that
for every
k
-labeled incidence graph
L^{\prime}

with

\operatorname{dom}(b_{L^{\prime}})\supseteq\operatorname{dom}(b)
,

\operatorname{dom}(r_{L^{\prime}})\supseteq\operatorname{dom}(r)
,
and
with real guards w.r.t.
g
we have:
\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\Delta_{g}
and

\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime})=m\ \ \iff\ \ \mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\varphi_{L,m}\,.

Proof sketch.

Throughout this proof we use the following notation:
A
segmentation
for a number
m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geqslant 1}
is a pair of
d
-tuples

(\overline{c},\overline{m})\ \ =\ \ \big((c_{1},\dots,c_{d}),(m_{1},\dots,m_{d})\big)

of numbers in
\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
,
such that
d\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
and
m_{1}<\cdots<m_{d}
and

\sum_{i\in[d]}c_{i}m_{i}=m
.
We call
d
the
degree
of
(\overline{c},\overline{m})

and
c\coloneqq\sum_{i\in[d]}c_{i}
its
size
.

By
\textit{Seg}(m)
we denote the set of all segmentations of
m
.
Note that
\textit{Seg}(m)
is finite and non-empty for every
m\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
.

The proof of Lemma
9.1
proceeds by induction based on Definition
8.10
.
The base case can be handled straightforwardly (see Appendix
E.1
).
For the inductive step
we assume that the lemma’s statement is already shown for
L=(I,r,b,g)\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
and
for
L_{i}=(I_{i},r_{i},b_{i},g_{i})\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
for
i\in[2]
where
g_{1},g_{2}
are compatible.
The goal is to prove the lemma’s statement for the particular

\tilde{L}=(\tilde{I},\tilde{r},\tilde{b},\tilde{g})\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}

considered in the following case distinction.
The case where
\tilde{L}=(L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2})
can be handled
rather easily by utilising part
1
) of Lemma
8.15
.
The case where
\tilde{L}=L[{X_{r}}{\to}\bullet]
for some

{X_{r}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(r)
can be handled similarly as the following case.

The case where
\tilde{L}=L\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle
for some

{X_{b}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(b)\setminus\operatorname{img}(g)
, is handled as follows.
If
{X_{b}}=\varnothing
we are done since
\tilde{L}=L
.
If
{X_{b}}\neq\varnothing
let
\ell\coloneqq|{X_{b}}|
and let

{X_{b}}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}
with
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
. Note that

\tilde{r}=r
,
\tilde{b}=b-{X_{b}}
, and
\tilde{g}=g
.
We let
\varphi_{\tilde{L},0}\coloneqq\forall(\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\dots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\to\varphi_{L,0})\,.

For every
m\geqslant 1
we let

\varphi_{\tilde{L},m}\coloneqq\bigvee_{(\overline{c},\overline{m})\in\textit{Seg}(m)}\varphi^{\prime}_{\overline{c},\overline{m}}

where for every

(\overline{c},\overline{m})=((c_{1},\ldots,c_{d}),(m_{1},\ldots,m_{d}))\in\textit{Seg}(m)

and
c\coloneqq\sum_{i\in[d]}c_{i}
,

\begin{array}[]{ll}\varphi^{\prime}_{\overline{c},\overline{m}}\ \ \coloneqq&\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$=$}c}(\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\dots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\land\lnot\varphi_{L,0})\ \ \land\\[4.30554pt]
&\bigwedge_{j\in[d]}\left(\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$=$}c_{j}}(\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\dots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\land\varphi_{L,m_{j}}\right)\,.\end{array}

Let
L^{\prime}=(I^{\prime},r^{\prime},b^{\prime},g^{\prime})
satisfy the lemma’s assumptions for
\tilde{L}
instead of
L
. I.e.,
we have

\operatorname{dom}(b^{\prime})\supseteq\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{b})=\operatorname{dom}(b)\setminus{X_{b}}
,

\operatorname{dom}(r^{\prime})\supseteq\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{r})=\operatorname{dom}(r)
, and

L^{\prime}
has real guards w.r.t.
\tilde{g}
. Thus,

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\Delta_{\tilde{g}}
.
From Lemma
8.15
we know that

n\ \coloneqq\ \operatorname{hom}(\tilde{L},L^{\prime})\ \ =\sum_{\bar{e}\in B(I^{\prime})^{\ell}}\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle).

(12)

Note that
n=0

\iff

\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)=0
for all

\bar{e}\in B(I^{\prime})^{\ell}

\iff
for all
\bar{e}\in B(I^{\prime})^{\ell}
we
have
either
\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle}\not\models\Delta_{g}
or

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle}\models(\Delta_{g}\wedge\varphi_{L,0})

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\varphi_{\tilde{L},0}
.
Thus, the formula
\varphi_{\tilde{L},0}
has the desired meaning.

Let us now consider the case where
n\geqslant 1
.
For every number
m^{\prime}\in[n]
let

S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}
be the set of all
\bar{e}\in B(I^{\prime})^{\ell}
such that

m^{\prime}=\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)
.
Let
d^{\prime}
be the number of distinct
m^{\prime}\in[n]
for which
S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\neq\varnothing
, and let

m^{\prime}_{1}<\cdots<m^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}
be an ordered list of all
m^{\prime}\in[n]
with
S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\neq\varnothing
.
For each
j\in[d^{\prime}]
let
c^{\prime}_{j}\coloneqq|S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}_{j}}|
. Let
\overline{c}^{\prime}\coloneqq(c^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,c^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}})
and

\overline{m}^{\prime}\coloneqq(m^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,m^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}})
.
Note that

\sum_{j\in[d^{\prime}]}c^{\prime}_{j}m^{\prime}_{j}=n
. Hence

(\overline{c}^{\prime},\overline{m}^{\prime})\in\textit{Seg}(n)
is a segmentation of
n

of size
c^{\prime}\coloneqq\sum_{j\in[d^{\prime}]}c^{\prime}_{j}
and of degree
d^{\prime}
.

Note that
\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\varphi^{\prime}_{\overline{c}^{\prime},\overline{m}^{\prime}}
. Furthermore,
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
we have:

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\varphi_{\tilde{L},m}

\iff

(\overline{m}^{\prime},\overline{c}^{\prime})\in\textit{Seg}(m)

\iff

m=n=\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{L},L^{\prime})
.
This verifies that for every
m\geqslant 1
the formula
\varphi_{\tilde{L},m}
has the
desired meaning.

It remains to verify that

(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi_{\tilde{L},m})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Recall that
\tilde{g}=g
.
Thus,
\tilde{g}
satisfies the condition (
6
) of
rule
7
) of the syntax definition of
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.
Since, by the induction hypothesis,

(\Delta_{g}\wedge\varphi_{L,m_{j}})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
, we obtain by rule

7
) that the conjunction of
\Delta_{\tilde{g}}
with
the formula in the second line of
\varphi^{\prime}_{\overline{c},\overline{m}}

belongs to
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.
The same reasoning (combined with rule
4
)) yields that the conjunction of
\Delta_{\tilde{g}}
with
the formula in the first line of
\varphi^{\prime}_{\overline{c},\overline{m}}

belongs to
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.
Applying rule
5
)
yields that each formula

(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi^{\prime}_{\overline{c},\overline{m}})

as well as the formula

(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi_{\tilde{L},m})

belongs to
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
, for every
m\geqslant 1
.
A similar reasoning shows that also
(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi_{\tilde{L},0})

belongs to
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
. This completes the inductive step for the case
where
\tilde{L}=L\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle
.

The final case to consider is
\tilde{L}\coloneqq L[{\leadsto}f]
,
where
f
is a transition for
g
.
A closer inspection shows that for every
L^{\prime}
that satisfies the
lemma’s assumptions for
\tilde{L}
instead of
L
, the
following is true:

\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{L},L^{\prime})=\sum_{\bar{e}\in B(I^{\prime})^{\ell}}\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)
,
for

{X_{b}}\coloneqq\operatorname{dom}(b)\cap\operatorname{img}(f)\cap\operatorname{img}(g)
.
I.e., we have the same situation as in (
12
).
We therefore can choose the exact same formulas

\varphi_{\tilde{L},m}
as in the previous case
(for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
) and obtain that these formulas have the desired meaning.

But it remains to verify that

(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi_{\tilde{L},m})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Note that now we have
\tilde{g}=f\cup g
(whereas previously we had
to deal with the case where
\tilde{g}=g
).
All we have to do is argue that
\tilde{g}
satisfies the
condition (
6
) of
rule
7
) of the syntax definition of
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.
Once having achieved this, the same reasoning as in the previous case
proves that the formula
(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi_{\tilde{L},m})
belongs to

\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Note that according to the induction hypothesis, for all
m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
we have:

\operatorname{dom}(b)=\textit{ifree}_{B}((\Delta_{g}\wedge\varphi_{L,m^{\prime}}))\supseteq\operatorname{img}(g)
.
Therefore,
{X_{b}}=\operatorname{img}(f)\cap\operatorname{img}(g)
.

Consider an arbitrary
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g)
. We have to show that

\tilde{g}(i)=g(i)
or
\tilde{g}(i)\in{X_{b}}
or
\tilde{g}(i)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)
.
If
\tilde{g}(i)=g(i)

or
\tilde{g}(i)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)
,
we are done.
Consider the case that

\tilde{g}(i)\neq g(i)

and

\tilde{g}(i)\in\operatorname{img}(g)
.
In this case,
\tilde{g}(i)=f(i)
and there exists an
i^{\prime}\in\operatorname{dom}(g)

with
g(i^{\prime})=\tilde{g}(i)=f(i)
.
Hence,
\tilde{g}(i)\in\operatorname{img}(f)\cap\operatorname{img}(g)={X_{b}}
, and we are done.
This completes the proof sketch of Lemma
9.1
.
∎

We proceed with the second lemma necessary for our proof of
Theorem
6.1
.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
\chi\coloneqq(\Delta_{g}\land\psi)\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
and let

m,d\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\geqslant 1
.
There exists a
Q\coloneqq{Q}_{\chi,m,d}\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}

with
g_{Q}=g
,

\textup{db}_{Q}=\textit{ifree}_{B}(\chi)

and

\textup{dr}_{Q}=\operatorname{dom}(g)=\textit{ifree}_{R}(\chi)

such that for all
k
-labeled incidence graphs

L^{\prime}=(I^{\prime},b^{\prime},r^{\prime},g^{\prime})
with

|B(I^{\prime})|=m
and

\max\{|N_{I^{\prime}}(e)|:e\in B(I^{\prime})\}\leqslant d

and

\operatorname{dom}(b^{\prime})\supseteq\textup{db}_{Q}
,

\operatorname{dom}(r^{\prime})\supseteq\textup{dr}_{Q}
,
and

g^{\prime}\supseteq g
and with real guards w.r.t.
g

we have
\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\Delta_{g}
and

\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\ \mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\chi\quad\text{and}\\
0&\text{if }\ \mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\not\models\chi\;.\end{cases}

Proof sketch.

We proceed by induction on the construction of
\chi
.
The base cases can be handled straightforwardly (see
Appendix
E.2
).
To the induction base we also add the “special case” where

\psi
is unfulfillable for all
k
-labeled incidence graphs
L^{\prime}
matching
the requirements imposed by the lemma. This case is handled by
choosing
Q\coloneqq 0{\cdot}L
, for a suitably chosen
L
that has
the desired parameters, i.e.,
g_{L}=g
,
\operatorname{dom}(b_{L})=\textit{ifree}_{B}(\chi)
,
and
\operatorname{dom}(r_{L})=\operatorname{dom}(g)=\textit{ifree}_{R}(\chi)
.

Most cases of the induction step can be handled rather
straightforwardly. A notable exception is the case where the formula
is built using rule
7
) of the syntax definition
of
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.
We assume that the lemma’s statement is already shown for

\chi\coloneqq(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.
Fix arbitrary
m,d\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\geqslant 1
.
Let
Q\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
be provided by
the lemma’s statement for

\chi
and the parameters
m,d
.
The goal is to prove the lemma’s statement for parameters
m,d
and the particular

\tilde{\chi}\coloneqq(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\land\tilde{\psi})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
given as follows:

\tilde{\psi}=\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n}(\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\land\psi)
,
where
n,\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
and

S\coloneqq\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}\subseteq\textit{ifree}_{B}(\chi)

with
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
and
\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{g})=\operatorname{dom}(g)
and
all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g)
satisfy

\tilde{g}(i)=g(i)
or

\tilde{g}(i)\in S
or

\tilde{g}(i)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)
.

The case where
n>m^{\ell}
is handled by the “special case”
mentioned above. The case
n\leqslant m^{\ell}
is quite intricate and relies
on a series of claims (see Appendix
E.2
for proofs):

Claim 1:
Consider an arbitrary
k
-labeled incidence graph
L^{\prime}
satisfying
the lemma’s assumptions for
\tilde{\chi}
.

For

\textit{num}(L^{\prime})\coloneqq\operatorname{hom}(Q\langle S{\to}\bullet\rangle,L^{\prime})

we have:

\textit{num}(L^{\prime})=\sum_{\overline{e}\in B(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell}}\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime}\langle S{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)

and

0\leqslant\textit{num}(L^{\prime})\leqslant|B(I_{L^{\prime}})|^{\ell}

and
\textit{num}(L^{\prime})
is exactly the number of tuples

\overline{e}\in B(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell}
such that

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle}\models\chi
.

For the remainder of the proof, our aim is to
construct a
Q^{\prime}\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}

that has the parameters

g_{Q^{\prime}},\textup{db}_{Q^{\prime}},\textup{dr}_{Q^{\prime}}

required by the
lemma for formula
\tilde{\chi}
,
such
that
\operatorname{hom}(Q^{\prime},L^{\prime})=\textit{num}(L^{\prime})
for all
k
-labeled incidence
graphs
L^{\prime}

satisfying the lemma’s assumptions for
\tilde{\chi}
.

Once having achieved this, we can use
Lemma
8.16
to obtain

\tilde{Q}\coloneqq Q^{\prime}[X,Y]\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
for

X\coloneqq\{0,\ldots,n{-}1\}
and
Y\coloneqq\{n,\ldots,m^{\ell}\}
.
Note that
\tilde{Q}
has the properties desired for the formula

\tilde{\chi}
.
But how to construct a suitable
Q^{\prime}
?
— We let

Z\ \coloneqq\ \{\,i\in\operatorname{dom}(g)\ :\ \tilde{g}(i)\neq g(i)\text{ \ or \ }g(i)\in S\,\}\,.

If
Z=\varnothing
, we choose

Q^{\prime}\coloneqq Q\langle S{\to}\bullet\rangle
and are done:
Z=\varnothing
means that

\tilde{g}=g
and
\operatorname{img}(g)\cap S=\varnothing
, and hence
Q\langle S{\to}\bullet\rangle\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
.
If
Z\neq\varnothing
, we cannot simply choose
Q^{\prime}
as above, because
there is no guarantee that
Q\langle S{\to}\bullet\rangle
belongs to
\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
.
Instead, we proceed as follows.
Let
f
be the partial function with
\operatorname{dom}(f)=Z
and

f(i)\coloneqq\tilde{g}(i)
for all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
.

Claim 2:

\tilde{g}=f\cup g
, and

f
is a transition for
g
, and
all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
satisfy:
f(i)\in S
or
f(i)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)
.

Let
D_{1}\coloneqq(S\cap\operatorname{img}(f))\setminus\operatorname{img}(g)
,
let
D_{2}\coloneqq S\setminus\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})
, and
let
D_{3}\coloneqq\operatorname{img}(g)\cap\operatorname{img}(f)
.

Claim 3:

D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}
are pairwise disjoint,
S=\bigcup_{i\in[3]}D_{i}
.

We let
Q_{1}\coloneqq Q\langle D_{1}{\to}\bullet\rangle
and

Q_{2}\coloneqq Q_{1}[{\leadsto}f]
and

Q^{\prime}\coloneqq Q_{2}\langle D_{2}{\to}\bullet\rangle
.

Claim 4:

Q_{1},Q_{2},Q^{\prime}\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
and

g_{Q_{1}}=g
,
\textup{db}_{Q_{1}}=(\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus D_{1})
and

g_{Q^{\prime}}=\tilde{g}
,

\textup{dr}_{Q^{\prime}}=\textit{ifree}_{R}(\tilde{\chi})
,
\textup{db}_{Q^{\prime}}=\textit{ifree}_{B}(\tilde{\chi})
.

According to Claim 4,
Q^{\prime}\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
and
Q^{\prime}
has the desired
parameters
g_{Q^{\prime}},\textup{db}_{Q^{\prime}},\textup{dr}_{Q^{\prime}}
.
The next claim ensures that
Q^{\prime}
indeed satisfies what we aimed for.

Claim 5:

\operatorname{hom}(Q^{\prime},L^{\prime})=\textit{num}(L^{\prime})
,
for all
k
-labeled incidence graphs
L^{\prime}
that satisfy
the lemma’s assumptions for
\tilde{\chi}
.

This completes the proof sketch for Lemma
9.2
.
∎

Finally, the proof of Theorem
6.1
can easily be achieved
by using the Theorems
7.2
and
8.12

and the Lemmas
9.1
(for direction “
\Longleftarrow
”) and

9.2
(for direction “
\Longrightarrow
”):

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Let
I
and
I^{\prime}
be arbitrary incidence graphs, and let

k\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
. Our aim is to prove that

I\not\equiv_{\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}}I^{\prime}

\iff

\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IEHW}_{k}}(I)\neq\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IEHW}_{k}}(I^{\prime})
.

The case where

|B(I)|\neq|B(I^{\prime})|
can be handled easily:
Choose a sentence in
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
stating that the number of blue nodes is
exactly
|B(I)|
, and
choose an incidence
graph
J
that has one blue node and neither red nodes nor edges.
This yields that
I\not\equiv_{\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}}I^{\prime}

and
\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IEHW}_{k}}(I)\neq\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IEHW}_{k}}(I^{\prime})
.

Consider the case
|B(I)|=|B(I^{\prime})|
.
Let
L,L^{\prime}
be the label-free
k
-labeled incidence graphs
corresponding to
I,I^{\prime}
.

For “
\Longleftarrow
” let
J\in\textsl{IEHW}_{k}

such that
\operatorname{hom}(J,I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J,I^{\prime})
.
Let
m\coloneqq\operatorname{hom}(J,I)
.
From Theorem
8.12
2

we obtain a label-free
\tilde{L}\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
such that

I_{\tilde{L}}\cong J
.
From Lemma
9.1
we obtain a sentence

\chi\coloneqq(\top\wedge\varphi_{\tilde{L},m})
in
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
(and therefore also in
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
) such that

\mathcal{I}_{L}\models\chi
(because
\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{L},L)=m
) and

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\not\models\chi
(because

\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{L},L^{\prime})\neq m
).
Thus,
I\models\chi
and
I^{\prime}\not\models\chi
. I.e.,
\chi
witnesses
that
I\not\equiv_{\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}}I^{\prime}
.

For “
\Longrightarrow
” let
\varphi

be a sentence
in
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
such that
I\models\varphi
and
I^{\prime}\not\models\varphi
.
By Theorem
7.2
there exists a
\chi\coloneqq(\top\land\psi)\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
such that
\varphi\equiv(\top\land\varphi)\equiv\chi
, i.e.
I\models\chi
and
I^{\prime}\not\models\chi
.
Let
m\coloneqq|B(I)|=|B(I^{\prime})|
.
Choose a
d\in\mathbb{N}
such
that
d\geqslant|N_{I}(e)|
for all
e\in B(I)
and
d\geqslant|N_{I^{\prime}}(e)|
for
all
e\in B(I^{\prime})
.
Let
Q\coloneqq{Q}_{\chi,m,d}\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
be provided by
Lemma
9.2
.
From Lemma
9.2
we know that

\operatorname{hom}(Q,L)=1
(because
I\models\chi
) and

\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime})=0
(because
I^{\prime}\not\models\chi
).
Since
Q\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
, there are an
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
and

\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}
and

L_{1},\ldots,L_{\ell}\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
such that
Q=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\alpha_{i}L_{i}
. We have:

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\alpha_{i}\operatorname{hom}(L_{i},L)=\operatorname{hom}(Q,L)\neq\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\alpha_{i}\operatorname{hom}(L_{i},L^{\prime})
.
Hence there exists an
i\in[\ell]
with
\alpha_{i}\neq 0
and

\operatorname{hom}(L_{i},L)\neq\operatorname{hom}(L_{i},L^{\prime})
.
We know that
L_{i}\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
,
and
L_{i}=(I_{i},r_{i},b_{i},g_{i})
has
parameters
\textup{dr}_{Q}=\textup{db}_{Q}=\varnothing
and guard function

g_{i}=g_{\varnothing}
.
I.e.,
\operatorname{dom}(r_{i})=\operatorname{dom}(b_{i})=\varnothing
.
From Theorem
8.12
1
we know that

I_{i}\in\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
. Thus,
I_{i}
witnesses that

\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IEHW}_{k}}(I)\neq\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IEHW}_{k}}(I^{\prime})
.
This completes the proof of Theorem
6.1
.
∎

10
Conclusion

Combining the Theorems
3.1
,
4.1
,

6.1
yields:

Theorem 10.1

(Main Theorem)
.

Let
H,H^{\prime}
be hypergraphs.

1.

{I}_{H}\equiv_{\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}}{I}_{H^{\prime}}

\iff

\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{GHW}_{k}}(H)=\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{GHW}_{k}}(H^{\prime})
.

2.

If
H
and
H^{\prime}
are simple hypergraphs, then

{I}_{H}\equiv_{\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}}{I}_{H^{\prime}}

\iff

\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{sGHW}_{k}}(H)=\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{sGHW}_{k}}(H^{\prime})
.

For our proofs it was crucial to consider

entangled hypertree decompositions
(ehds, for short)
instead of
generalised hypertree decompositions.
To the best of our knowledge,
ehds have not been studied before.
From Theorem
2.7
we know that there exist
arbitrarily large
k
such that

\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
is a strict
subclass of
\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
; but nevertheless, according to Theorem
4.1

homomorphism indistinguishability coincides for both classes.
Many questions remain open, in particular:

How hard is it, given a hypergraph
H
and a number
k
, to
determine whether
\operatorname{ehw}(H)\leqslant k
?

For
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
:  how hard is it to compute the function
(or, “vector”)
\textsf{{HOM}}_{\mathcal{C}}(H)
for a given hypergraph
H
?
Which properties does it have?

What is the expressive power of the logic
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
?
What properties of hypergraphs can be described by this logic?
How does a suitable pebble game for
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
look like?

Our result lifts Dvořák’s result for tree-width
\leqslant k

[
12
]
from graphs to hypergraphs.
Does there also exist a lifting of Grohe’s result for

tree-depth

\leqslant k

[
17
]
from graphs to hypergraphs?

Seeing that Dvořák’s result lifted nicely to hypergraphs, we
believe that there should also be a lifting of
Cai, Fürer and Immerman’s result

[
8
]
, i.e.,
a hypergraph-variant of the Weisfeiler-Leman
algorithm, whose distinguishing power matches
precisely the logic
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
. We plan to study this in future work.

APPENDIX

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
3.1

Notice that this paper’s Theorem
3.1
looks similar to
Bökers Lemma 6 in
[
7
]
.
4
4

4

Consult the full version
for a proof of Lemma 6; you can find it at

arXiv:1903.12432 [cs.DM]
.
Note that the numbering of the lemmas is increased by one, i.e. Lemma 6 is Lemma 7 in the full version etc.

Also observe that this is the only Lemma that he proves just for
Berge-acyclic hypergraphs. Lemmas 3, 4, and 5 are proven for arbitrary
hypergraphs (as he notes himself). So all we have to do is to verify,
that his proof for Lemma 6 works if we consider the class of
hypergraphs of generalised hypertree width at most
k
instead of the
class of Berge-acyclic hypergraphs.

Upon examining his proof of Lemma 6 (see the full version on arXiv) we
see that closure under pumping and local merging are the only
properties of Berge-acylic hypergraphs, that Böker uses. Hence, since
these closures also hold for hypergraphs of generalised hypertree
width at most
k
, the proof remains valid.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
:
.

1
: “
\Longrightarrow
” is trivial since
\textsl{GHW}_{k}\supseteq\textsl{sGHW}_{k}
.
“
\Longleftarrow
”
holds because for every hypergraph
F
and every homomorphism
h
from

F
to a simple hypergraph, all edges
e,e^{\prime}\in E(F)
with

f_{F}(e)=f_{F}(e^{\prime})
have to be mapped onto the same edge of the simple
hypergraph. Thus, if
F\in\textsl{GHW}_{k}
distinguishes between
H
and

H^{\prime}
, then also the “simplified version” of
F
(i.e., the simple
hypergraph obtained by letting each edge of
F
occur with
multiplicity 1) also distinguishes between
H
and
H^{\prime}
.

2
:
Böker
[
7
]
proved the analogous statement for

BA
,
IBA
instead of
\textsl{GHW}_{k}
,
\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
, where
BA
is the
class of all Berge-acyclic hypergraphs and
IBA
is the class of
all incidence graphs of hypergraphs in
BA
.
Böker’s proof, however, works for all classes
C
of
hypergraphs and the associated class
IC
of all incidence
graphs of hypergraphs in
C
, provided that
C
is
closed under
local merging
and
pumping
5
5

5

Böker
calls this “leaf adding”, since he is working with incidence
graphs that are also always trees. We call it pumping, because this
is the more intuitive name in our environment. Note that our
definition of pumping is the same as Böker’s definition of
leaf-adding.
:
We say that
C
is closed under
local merging
, if every

F\in\textsl{C}
remains in
C
when merging two vertices

u_{1},u_{2}\in V(F)
that are adjacent via a common edge
e\in E(F)

(i.e.,
u_{1},u_{2}\in f_{F}(e)
), into a single new vertex
u
.
We say that
C
is closed under
pumping
, if every

F\in\textsl{C}
remains in
C
when inserting a newly created
vertex into an edge
e\in E(F)
(but note that we must not insert
vertices in the intersection of multiple edges).
Now, all that remains to be shown is that the class
\textsl{GHW}_{k}
is closed under local merging and pumping.

Closure under pumping is verified as follows: Assume we are given a
ghd
D=(T,\textit{bag},\textit{cover})
of a hypergraph
F
(resp., of its incidence
graph
I_{F}
). Let
F^{\prime}
be the hypergraph obtained from
F
by pumping
edge
e\in E(F)
with a new vertex
u
, i.e.,

V(F^{\prime})=V(F)\cup\{u\}
,
E(F^{\prime})=E(F)
,
f_{F^{\prime}}(e)=f_{F}(e)\cup\{u\}
, and
f_{F^{\prime}}(e^{\prime})=f_{F}(e^{\prime})
for all
e^{\prime}\in E(F)\setminus\{e\}
.
We can turn
D
into a ghd
D^{\prime}
of
F^{\prime}
as follows:
We choose one particular tree-node
t\in V(T)
such that

e\in\textit{cover}(t)
and
f_{F}(e)\subseteq\textit{bag}(t)
(such a
t
exists
because
D
satisfies the
completeness
condition of
Definition
2.3
), and we insert the new
vertex
u
into the bag
\textit{bag}(t)
. I.e.,
D^{\prime}=(T^{\prime},\textit{bag}^{\prime},\textit{cover}^{\prime})

with
T^{\prime}=T
,
\textit{cover}^{\prime}=\textit{cover}
, and
\textit{bag}^{\prime}(t)=\textit{bag}(t)\cup\{u\}

and
\textit{bag}^{\prime}(t^{\prime})=\textit{bag}(t^{\prime})
for all
t^{\prime}\in V(T)\setminus\{t\}
.
It is straightforward to verify that
D^{\prime}
is a ghd of
F^{\prime}
and
\operatorname{w}(D^{\prime})=\operatorname{w}(D)
.
Thus,
F\in\textsl{GHW}_{k}
implies that
F^{\prime}\in\textsl{GHW}_{k}
.

Closure under local merging is verified as follows:
Assume we are given a ghd
D=(T,\textit{bag},\textit{cover})
of a hypergraph
F
(resp., of its
incidence graph
{I}_{F}
). Let
F^{\prime}
be the hypergraph obtained from
F
by merging
two vertices
u_{1},u_{2}
for which there exists an
e\in E(F)
with

u_{1},u_{2}\in f_{F}(e)
. By Definition
2.3
,

D
satisfies
the completeness and connectedness conditions. I.e.,
there is a tree-node
t\in V(T)
with
e\in\textit{cover}(t)
and
f_{F}(e)\subseteq\textit{bag}(t)
.
In particular,
u_{1},u_{2}\in\textit{bag}(t)
.
Hence, the subtrees
T_{u_{1}}
and
T_{u_{2}}
touch each other in
t
.
The hypergraph
F^{\prime}
is obtained from
F
by
identifying the vertices
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
with a single new vertex

u
. More precisely, let
\pi
be the mapping with

\pi(u_{1})=\pi(u_{2})=u
and
\pi(v)=v
for all

v\in V(F)\setminus\{u_{1},u_{2}\}
. Then,

F^{\prime}=(V(F^{\prime}),E(F^{\prime}),f_{F^{\prime}})
with
V(F^{\prime})=\{\pi(v):v\in V(F)\}
,
E(F^{\prime})=E(F)
and

f_{F^{\prime}}(e^{\prime})=\{\pi(v):v\in e^{\prime}\}
for all
e^{\prime}\in E(F^{\prime})
.
For every tree-node
t\in V(T)
let

\textit{bag}^{\prime}(t)\coloneqq\{\pi(v):v\in\textit{bag}(t)\}
. Let
D^{\prime}\coloneqq(T,\textit{bag}^{\prime},\textit{cover})
. It is straightforward to verify that
D^{\prime}
is a
ghd of
F^{\prime}
. Obviously,
\operatorname{w}(D^{\prime})=\operatorname{w}(D)
. Thus,
F\in\textsl{GHW}_{k}
implies
that
F^{\prime}\in\textsl{GHW}_{k}
.
∎

Appendix B
Details omitted in Section
4

B.1
Detailed Proof of Theorem
4.1

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

The direction “
\Longrightarrow
” is trivial since
\textsl{IGHW}_{k}\supseteq\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
.
For the direction “
\Longleftarrow
” it suffices to prove the following:
If there is a
J\in\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
with
\operatorname{hom}(J,I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J,I^{\prime})
, then there also exists a
J^{\prime}\in\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
with

\operatorname{hom}(J^{\prime},I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J^{\prime},I^{\prime})
.
The remainder of this proof is devoted to showing that such a
J^{\prime}

indeed exists (for illustrations depicting the following
constructions see
Appendix
B.2
).

We start with a
J\in\textsl{IGHW}_{k}
with
\operatorname{hom}(J,I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J,I^{\prime})
. Let

D=(T,\textit{bag},\textit{cover})
be a ghd of
J
with
\operatorname{w}(D)\leqslant k
.
We use a 2-step process to construct the desired
J^{\prime}
:
First, we transform
D
into a ghd
D^{1}
of an incidence graph
J^{1}
such that
\operatorname{w}(D^{1})\leqslant\operatorname{w}(D)
and

\operatorname{hom}(J^{1},I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J^{1},I^{\prime})
and
D^{1}
satisfies
condition
4
of
Definition
2.5
(but
condition
5

might still be violated).
Afterwards, we transform
D^{1}
into a ghd
D^{2}
of an
incidence graph
J^{2}
such that
\operatorname{w}(D^{2})\leqslant\operatorname{w}(D^{1})
and

\operatorname{hom}(J^{2},I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J^{2},I^{\prime})
and
D^{2}
satisfies
conditions
4

and
5

of
Definition
2.5
and hence
is an ehd. Letting
J^{\prime}\coloneqq J^{2}
then completes the proof.

Construction of
D^{1}
and
J^{1}
:
For each tree-node
t\in V(T)
and each blue node
e\in B(J)

consider the set of red nodes
s(t,e)\coloneqq N_{J}(e)\cap\textit{bag}(t)
.
Let

S\coloneqq\{s(t,e):t\in V(T),\ e\in\textit{cover}(t),\ N_{J}(e)\not\subseteq\textit{bag}(t)\}
. Note that
S=\varnothing
implies that
condition
4
is
satisfied and we can choose
D^{1}=D
and
J^{1}=J
. If
S\neq\varnothing
, we let
\tilde{J}\coloneqq J
, and we loop through all
s\in S
and proceed as follows:
We use Lemma
4.2
to choose
a suitable number
n_{s}\geqslant 1
and insert into
\tilde{J}
exactly
n_{s}
new blue nodes

e^{\prime}_{s,i}
(for
i\in[n_{s}]
) along with edges from
e^{\prime}_{s,i}
to every
red node
v\in s

— i.e., we replace
\tilde{J}
with
\tilde{J}+n_{s}{\cdot}s
.
Let
J^{1}
be the resulting
\tilde{J}
after having looped
through all
s\in S
.
Since we have chosen the
n_{s}
(for
s\in S
) according to
Lemma
4.2
, we know that

\operatorname{hom}(J^{1},I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J^{1},I^{\prime})
.
The ghd
D
of
J
is transformed into a ghd
D^{1}=(T^{1},\textit{bag}^{1},\textit{cover}^{1})
of
J^{1}

satisfying
condition
4
as follows:
For every
t\in V(T)
let
\textit{bag}^{1}(t)\coloneqq\textit{bag}(t)
and

\textit{cover}^{1}(t)\coloneqq\{e\in\textit{cover}(t):N_{J}(e)\subseteq\textit{bag}(t)\}\cup\{e^{\prime}_{s(t,e),1}:e\in\textit{cover}(t),\ N_{J}(e)\not\subseteq\textit{bag}(t)\}
.
This ensures that
condition
4
is
satisfied when choosing
T^{1}\coloneqq T
. Clearly,
conditions
2

and
3
are satisfied; and
condition
1
is
satisfied for all those blue nodes that have already been present in

J
and for
e^{\prime}_{s,1}
for all
s\in S
. To meet
condition
1
also for
the newly inserted blue nodes
e^{\prime}_{s,i}
with
s\in S
and

i\in\{2,\ldots,n_{s}\}
, we loop trough all
s\in S
with
n_{s}\geqslant 2
and proceed as follows: Pick an arbitrary tree-node
t
in

T^{1}
such that
e^{\prime}_{s,1}\in\textit{cover}^{1}(t)
. For each

i\in\{2,\ldots,n_{s}\}
insert into
T^{1}
a new leaf
t_{s,i}

adjacent to
t
and let
\textit{bag}^{1}(t_{s,i})\coloneqq s
and

\textit{cover}^{1}(t_{s,i})\coloneqq\{e^{\prime}_{s,i}\}
.
This completes the construction of
D^{1}
and
J^{1}
.
Note that
D^{1}
might violate condition
5
.

Construction of
D^{2}
and
J^{2}
:

For each
e\in B(J^{1})
let
m_{e}
denote the
number of connected components of the subgraph

T^{1}_{e}
, i.e., the subgraph of
T^{1}
induced on

V_{e}\coloneqq\{t\in V(T^{1}):e\in\textit{cover}^{1}(t)\}
;
and let
V_{e,0},\ldots,V_{e,m_{e}-1}

be the sets of tree-nodes (i.e., nodes in
V(T^{1})
) of these
connected components.
In order to meet
condition
5
,
we let
\tilde{J}\coloneqq J^{1}
, and
we loop through all those
e\in B(J^{1})
where
m_{e}\geqslant 2
and
proceed as follows:
We let
s\coloneqq N_{J^{1}}(e)
and use Lemma
4.2
to choose
a suitable number
n_{e}\geqslant m_{e}{-}1
and insert into
\tilde{J}

exactly
n_{e}
new blue nodes

e^{\prime}_{e,i}
(for
i\in[n_{e}]
) and connect each of them to all red
nodes in
s

— i.e., we replace
\tilde{J}
with
\tilde{J}+n_{e}{\cdot}s
.
Let
J^{2}
be the resulting
\tilde{J}
after having looped
through all the relevant
e
.
Since the numbers
n_{e}
were chosen according to
Lemma
4.2
, we have

\operatorname{hom}(J^{2},I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J^{2},I^{\prime})
.
The ghd
D^{1}
of
J^{1}
is transformed into an ehd

D^{2}=(T^{2},\textit{bag}^{2},\textit{cover}^{2})

of
J^{2}

as follows:
We start by letting
D^{2}\coloneqq D^{1}
. Then, we loop through all

e\in B(J^{1})
where
m_{e}\geqslant 2
and
proceed as follows:
For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{e}{-}1\}
we loop through all tree-nodes

t\in V_{e,i}
and replace
e
with
e^{\prime}_{e,i}
in

\textit{cover}^{2}(t)
. This will ensure that
condition
5

is met. To ensure that also
condition
1
is
satisfied, we
pick an arbitrary tree-node
t\in V_{e,0}
. For each

i\in[n_{e}]
with
i\geqslant m_{e}
, we insert into
T^{2}
a new leaf
t_{e,i}

adjacent to
t
and let
\textit{bag}^{2}(t_{e,i})\coloneqq N_{J^{2}}(e^{\prime}_{e,i})
and

\textit{cover}^{2}(t_{e,i})\coloneqq\{e^{\prime}_{e,i}\}
.

This completes the construction of
D^{2}
and
J^{2}
.
It is straightforward to verify that
D^{2}
is an ehd of
J^{2}

and
\operatorname{w}(D^{2})=\operatorname{w}(D^{1})\leqslant\operatorname{w}(D)
. This completes the proof of Theorem
4.1
.
∎

B.2
Illustrations concerning the proof of
Theorem
4.1

In the first step we construct
J^{1}
and
D^{1}
that satisfy condition
4
of Definition
2.5
as shown in Figure
1
.
The left part of this figure depicts the hypergraphs associated with the incidence graphs
J,J^{1}
.

J
e
f
i
g
t
:

bag
(t)

​​​=

{ a,b,c,d,e,f,g }

cover
(t)

​​​=

{ { a,b,c }, { c,d },
{ e,f,i }
,
{ g,i }
}

J^{1}
e
f
i
g
t
:

bag
^1(t)

​​​=

{ a,b,c,d,e,f,g }

cover
^1(t)

​​​=

{ { a,b,c }, { c,d },
{ e,f }
,
{ g }
}

insert
\{e,f\}
,
\{g\}
.

replace
\{e,f,i\}
,
\{g,i\}
in cover.

Figure 1:
Example for a modification occurring in step 1 that ensures that condition
4
of Definition
2.5
is satisfied.

In the depicted tree-node
t
of
D
(on the top right) condition
4
is violated since
i\not\in\textit{bag}(t)
. Our construction inserts new
blue nodes representing the
hyperedges
\{e,f\}
and
\{g\}
into
J
, which we can then use to replace the corresponding blue nodes (or, hyperedges) in
\textit{cover}(t)
. After this modification,
t
no longer violates condition
4
. We repeatedly apply this operation until condition
4
is no longer violated.

We must be careful, since this “naive” modification also changes the number of homomorphisms. Therefore, the procedure described above might align the numbers
\operatorname{hom}(J^{1},I)
and
\operatorname{hom}(J^{1},I^{\prime})
. To avoid this, we use Lemma
4.2
and insert in every step of the modification a suitable number of copies (i.e. maybe more than just one), so that after every modification the number of homomorphisms remains distinguishing. We deal with the additional copies by inserting additional tree-nodes as leaves, each having one copy in its cover (and nothing else) and the neighbourhood of the copy as its bag.
Altogether, this gives us
\operatorname{hom}(J^{1},I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J^{1},I^{\prime})
.

In the second step, we modify
J^{1}
and
D^{1}
such that they also satisfy condition
5
, i.e. such that the resulting
D^{2}
is an ehd of
J^{2}
. Consider
Figure
2
.
The left part of the figure depicts the hypergraphs associated with the incidence graphs
J^{1}
and
J^{2}
.
In this example, the hyperedge
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}e}
in
J^{1}
induces 3 connected components in
D^{1}
. Therefore, we insert
2
copies of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}e}
— namely, one for each additional connected component. We apply this procedure until there is no hyperedge left that induces multiple connected components, which means that condition
5
is met.

Again, this naive approach might equalise the number of
homomorphisms. Therefore, we use Lemma

4.2
to insert a suitable,
large enough number of copies, so that after every modification the
number of homomorphisms remains distinguishing. We deal with the
excess copies in the same way as before.

D.1
Proof of Lemma
8.6

Proof of Lemma
8.6
:
.

The proof consists of a lengthy (but otherwise straightforward) case
distinction that only checks requirements along Definition
8.5
.

Let
L_{1}=(I_{1},r_{1},b_{1},g_{1})
and
L_{2}=(I_{2},r_{2},b_{2},g_{2})
.
Let
L\coloneqq(L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2})
and
let
(I,r,b,g)=L
.
By assumption,
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
have real guards. We have to show
that also
L
has real guards.
To this end,
consider an arbitrary
i\in\operatorname{dom}(r)
. Let
j=g(i)
. We have to show the following:

1.

j\in\operatorname{dom}(b)
, and

2.

(b(j),r(i))\in E({I})
.

We distinguish between two cases:

Case 1:

i\in\operatorname{dom}(r_{1})
.
By Definition
8.5
we then have:
r(i)=[(r_{1}(i),1)]_{\sim_{R}}
and
j=g(i)=g_{1}(i)
.
Since
L_{1}
has real guards, we have

1.

j\in\operatorname{dom}(b_{1})
, and

2.

(b_{1}(j),r_{1}(i))\in E({I}_{1})
.

By Definition
8.5
and (
1
) we obtain that
j\in\operatorname{dom}(b)
. Thus, (
1
) is proved. To prove (
2
), we proceed as follows:
From (
2
) we know for
e\coloneqq b_{1}(j)
and
v\coloneqq r_{1}(i)
that
(e,v)\in E({I}_{1})
. By Definition
8.5
this yields:

3.

([(e,1)]_{\sim B},[(v,1)]_{\sim_{R}})\ \in\ E({I})
and

4.

[(e,1)]_{\sim_{B}}\ \in\ B({I})
and

5.

[(v,1)]_{\sim R}\ \in\ R({I})
.

By Definition
8.5
we have:
b(j)=[(b_{1}(j),1)]_{\sim_{B}}=[(e,1)]_{\sim_{B}}
.
Furthermore, recall that
r(i)=[(r_{1}(i),1)]_{\sim_{R}}=[(v,1)]_{\sim_{R}}
.
Thus, (
3
) means that
(b(j),r(i))\in E({I})
, i.e., (
1
) is proved.

Case 2:

i\not\in\operatorname{dom}(r_{1})
. Then,
i\in\operatorname{dom}(r_{2})
. (
1
) and
(
2
) can be proven
in the same way as in Case 1 by replacing every subscript 1 with
subscript 2.
This completes the proof of Lemma
8.6
.
∎

D.2
Detailed Proof of Part
1
of Theorem
8.12

Proof of part
1
of Theorem
8.12
:
.

By induction on the definition of
\textsl{GLI}_{k}
we show for every

L\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
that there exists an
entangled hypertree decomposition
D=(T,\textit{bag},\textit{cover})
of
I_{L}
of width
\leqslant k
.
To be able to carry out the induction step, we additionally ensure that there is a tree-node
\omega\in V(T)
with
\textit{cover}(\omega)=\operatorname{img}(b_{L})
.

The idea is fairly simple: From Lemma
8.11

we know that
L
has real guards. Thus, the (at most
k
) blue
labels given by
b
guard all the red labels given by
r
in the sense
that
(b({g(i)}),r(i))\in E(I)
for every
i\in\operatorname{dom}(r)
. Hence, we
can use the set of labeled blue vertices to cover the bag containing
all the labeled red vertices.
This is utilised as follows.

Base case
:

1.

Let
L=(I,r,b,g)
be a
k
-labeled incidence graph with
R(I)=\operatorname{img}(r)
,
B(I)=\operatorname{img}(b)
, and with real guards.
We choose
D\coloneqq(T,\textit{bag},\textit{cover})
as follows: Let
t
be a single tree-node,

V(T)\coloneqq\{t\}
,
E(T)\coloneqq\varnothing
,
\textit{bag}(t)\coloneqq R({I})=\operatorname{img}(r)
,

\textit{cover}(t)\coloneqq B({I})=\operatorname{img}(b)
.
Clearly,
D
is an ehd of
I
of width
|\operatorname{img}(b)|\leqslant k
, and
\omega:=t
satisfies
\textit{cover}(\omega)=\operatorname{img}(b)
.

Inductive step
: Let
L=(I,b,r,g)\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
, let

D=(T,\textit{bag},\textit{cover})
be an ehd of
I
of width
\leqslant k
,
and let

\omega\in V(T)
be such that

\textit{cover}(\omega)=\operatorname{img}(b)
.

2.

Let
L^{\prime}\coloneqq L[{X_{r}}{\to}\bullet]
for some

{X_{r}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(r)
. Obviously,
D
also is an ehd of
I_{L^{\prime}}
,
since
I_{L^{\prime}}=I
. And since
b_{L^{\prime}}=b
, we also have

\textit{cover}(\omega)=\operatorname{img}(b_{L^{\prime}})
.
I.e.,
D
is an ehd of
I_{L^{\prime}}
with the desired properties.

3.

Let
L^{\prime}\coloneqq L\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle
for some
{X_{b}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(b)\setminus\operatorname{img}(g)
.
Then,
D
is also on ehd of
I_{L^{\prime}}
, since
I_{L^{\prime}}=I
.
But note that we might have
\operatorname{img}(b_{L^{\prime}})\varsubsetneq\operatorname{img}(b)
and hence

\textit{cover}(\omega)\varsupsetneq\operatorname{img}(b_{L^{\prime}})
.
To fix this, we create a new tree-node
\omega^{\prime}
that we insert into

T
as a new leaf adjacent to
\omega
, and we let

\textit{cover}(\omega^{\prime})\coloneqq\operatorname{img}(b_{L^{\prime}})
and
\textit{bag}(\omega^{\prime})\coloneqq\bigcup_{e\in\textit{cover}(\omega^{\prime})}N_{I}(e)
.
This results in an ehd
D^{\prime}
of
I_{L^{\prime}}
with the desired
properties.

4.

Let
L^{\prime}\coloneqq L[{\leadsto}f]
for a transition
f
for
g_{L}
. Let
I^{\prime}\coloneqq I_{L^{\prime}}
. As in the previous step, we create a new tree-node
\omega^{\prime}
that we insert into
T
as
a new leaf adjacent to
\omega
, and we let
\textit{cover}^{\prime}(\omega^{\prime})\coloneqq\operatorname{img}(b_{L^{\prime}})
and

\textit{bag}^{\prime}(\omega^{\prime})=\bigcup_{e\in\textit{cover}^{\prime}(\omega^{\prime})}N_{I^{\prime}}(e)
.

To define
\textit{cover}^{\prime}(t)
for all
t\in V(T)\setminus\{\omega^{\prime}\}
,
need the following notation. By definition,

L^{\prime}=(L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2})
where
L_{1}\coloneqq M_{f}
and

L_{2}\coloneqq L\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle
for
{X_{b}}\coloneqq\operatorname{dom}(g)\cap\operatorname{img}(f)
.
For
i\in[2]
let
I_{i}\coloneqq I_{L_{i}}
, and let

\pi_{i,R}
and
\pi_{i,B}
be the mappings provided at the end of
Definition
8.5
.
For all
t\in V(T)\setminus\{\omega^{\prime}\}
we let
\textit{cover}^{\prime}(t)\coloneqq\{\pi_{2,B}(e):e\in\textit{cover}(t)\}
and

\textit{bag}^{\prime}(t)\coloneqq\bigcup_{e^{\prime}\in\textit{cover}^{\prime}(t)}N_{I^{\prime}}(e)
.

It is straightforward to verify that this results in an ehd
D^{\prime}
of
I^{\prime}
with the desired properties.

5.

Let
L^{\prime}\coloneqq(L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2})
where, for each
i\in[2]
,

L_{i}=(I_{i},r_{i},b_{i},g_{i})\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
,
D_{i}=(T_{i},\textit{bag}_{i},\textit{cover}_{i})

is an ehd of
I_{i}
of width
\leqslant k
, and
\omega_{i}\in V(T_{i})
is a
tree-node satisfying
\textit{cover}(\omega_{i})=\operatorname{img}(b_{i})
.
W.l.o.g. we assume that
V(T_{1})\cap V(T_{2})=\varnothing
.
Let
I^{\prime}\coloneqq I_{L^{\prime}}
.

We create a new tree-node
\omega^{\prime}
and we let

\textit{cover}^{\prime}(\omega^{\prime})\coloneqq\operatorname{img}(b_{L^{\prime}})
and

\textit{bag}^{\prime}(\omega^{\prime})=\bigcup_{e\in\textit{cover}^{\prime}(\omega^{\prime})}N_{I^{\prime}}(e)
.
We choose

T^{\prime}=(V(T^{\prime}),E(T^{\prime}))
with

V(T^{\prime})\coloneqq V(T_{1})\cup V(T_{2})\cup\{\omega^{\prime}\}

and

E(T^{\prime})\coloneqq E(T_{1})\cup E(T_{2})\cup\big\{\{\omega^{\prime},\omega_{1}\}\;,\;\{\omega^{\prime},\omega_{2}\}\big\}
.

To define
\textit{cover}^{\prime}(t)
for all
t\in V(T_{1})\cup V(T_{2})
, we
need the following notation.
For
i\in[2]
let

\pi_{i,R}
and
\pi_{i,B}
be the mappings provided at the end of
Definition
8.5
.
For all
i\in[2]
and all
t\in V(T_{i})
we let
\textit{cover}^{\prime}(t)\coloneqq\{\pi_{i,B}(e):e\in\textit{cover}_{i}(t)\}
and

\textit{bag}^{\prime}(t)\coloneqq\bigcup_{e^{\prime}\in\textit{cover}^{\prime}(t)}N_{I^{\prime}}(e)
.

It is straightforward to verify that
D^{\prime}\coloneqq(T^{\prime},\textit{bag}^{\prime},\textit{cover}^{\prime})
is an ehd of
I^{\prime}
with the desired properties.

This completes the proof of part
1
of Theorem
8.12
.
∎

D.3
Detailed Proof of Part
2
of Theorem
8.12

Proof of part
2
of Theorem
8.12
:
.

Given an
I\in\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
, the proof proceeds as follows.
We pick an ehd
D=(T,\textit{bag},\textit{cover})
of
I
of width
\leqslant k
that
has a particularly suitable shape (details follow below), and we
single out a suitable tree-node
\omega
as the “root” of
T
. For
every
t\in V(T)
, the information provided by
\textit{cover}(t)
is viewed
as a description of a guarded
k
-labeled incidence graph
L_{t}

corresponding to the base case of
Definition
8.10
.
We then perform a bottom-up traversal of the rooted tree
(T,\omega)
and glue
together
all the
L_{t}
’s.
But this has to be done with care: before glueing them, we have to
ensure that the guards are compatible; we achieve this by adequately
changing labels and applying transitions, so that finally we end up
with an
L\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
whose incidence graph
I_{L}
is isomorphic
to
I
.
Below, we give a detailed description of the construction;
a specific example of the construction can be found
in Appendix
D.5
.

We start by picking an ehd
D=(T,\textit{bag},\textit{cover})
of
I
and an

\omega\in V(T)
as provided by the following lemma.

Lemma D.1
.

For every
I\in\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
there is an ehd
D=(T,\textit{bag},\textit{cover})
of
I

and an
\omega\in V(T)
with
|\textit{cover}(\omega)|\leqslant k
such that the
rooted tree
(T,\omega)
is binary and monotone, i.e.,
every tree-node has at most 2 children, and
for all parent-child pairs
(t_{p},t_{c})
we have
|\textit{cover}(t_{p})|\geqslant|\textit{cover}(t_{c})|
.

Proof.

We start with an arbitrary ehd
D=(T,\textit{bag},\textit{cover})
of
I
of width
\leqslant k
.
Fix an
\omega\in V(T)
where
|\textit{cover}(\omega)|
is as large as
possible. Perform a bottom-up traversal of the rooted tree

(T,\omega)
.
Whenever encountering a parent-child pair
(t_{p},t_{c})
where

|\textit{cover}(t_{p})|<|\textit{cover}(t_{c})|
, insert elements from
\textit{cover}(t_{c})

into
\textit{cover}(t_{p})
to ensure that
|\textit{cover}(t_{p})|=|\textit{cover}(t_{c})|
,
and modify
\textit{bag}(t_{p})
such that condition

4
of
Definition
2.5
is satisfied.

To ensure that the tree is binary, we perform a top-down traversal of
(T,\omega)
, and for every node

t
that has
n\geqslant 3
children
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
we introduce
n{-}2

new nodes
t^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{n-1}
with
\textit{bag}(t^{\prime}_{i})=\textit{bag}(t)
and

\textit{cover}(t^{\prime}_{i})=\textit{cover}(t)
,
we insert edges
\{t,t^{\prime}_{2}\}
,
\{t^{\prime}_{n-1},t_{n}\}
, and
\{t^{\prime}_{i},t^{\prime}_{i+1}\}
for
all
i\in\{2,\ldots,n{-}2\}
; and
for every
i\in\{2,\ldots,n{-}1\}
we replace the edge
\{t,t_{i}\}

by the new edge
\{t^{\prime}_{i},t_{i}\}
.
It is straightforward to verify that the resulting tree is an ehd of

I
with the desired properties.
This completes the proof of Lemma
D.1
.
∎

We perform a top-down traversal of the rooted tree
(T,\omega)
to construct a

k
-colouring of
I
according to
D
, i.e. a mapping

c:B(I)\to[k]
with the following properties, where
c(t)\coloneqq\{c(e):e\in\textit{cover}(t)\}
for all
t\in V(T)
:
For all
t\in V(T)
and all
e,e^{\prime}\in\textit{cover}(t)
with
e\neq e^{\prime}
we
have
c(e)\neq c(e^{\prime})
, and for all parent-child pairs
(t_{p},t_{c})
we
have
c(t_{p})\supseteq c(t_{c})
.

For all
t\in V(T)
and
j\in[k]
we let
c(t,j)\coloneqq e
for the
particular
e\in\textit{cover}(t)
with
c(e)=j
; and in case that no such

e
exists we let

c(t,j)\coloneqq\bot
.

We perform a further top-down traversal of
(T,\omega)
to construct a
schedule for
D
, i.e., a mapping

s:V(T)\times R(I)\to B(I)
with the following properties:

1.

For all
t\in V(T)
and all
v\in\textit{bag}(t)
,

s(t,v)
is a blue node of
I
that belongs to
\textit{cover}(t)
and is
adjacent in
I
to the red node
v
.

2.

For all parent-child pairs
(t_{p},t_{c})
and for all

v\in\textit{bag}(t_{p})\cap\textit{bag}(t_{c})
, the following is satisfied:
If
s(t_{p},v)\in\textit{cover}(t_{c})
, then
s(t_{c},v)=s(t_{p},v)
.

Finally, we let
n\coloneqq|R(I)|
, and we fix an arbitrary list
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
of all
red nodes of
I
.
We use this list and the mappings
s
and
c
to define for every

t\in V(T)

the guarded
k
-labeled incidence graph
L_{t}

associated with the
tree-node
t
as follows:

L_{t}\coloneqq(I_{t},r_{t},b_{t},g_{t})
, where

R(I_{t})\coloneqq\textit{bag}(t)
,

B(I_{t})\coloneqq\textit{cover}(t)
,

E(I_{t})\coloneqq E(I)\cap(B(I_{t})\times R(I_{t}))
,
and we let
\operatorname{dom}(r_{t})\coloneqq\operatorname{dom}(g_{t})\coloneqq\{i\in[n]:v_{i}\in\textit{bag}(t)\}
and
r_{t}(i)\coloneqq v_{i}
and
g_{t}(i)\coloneqq c(s(t,v_{i}))

for all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g_{t})
,
and we let
\operatorname{dom}(b_{t})\coloneqq\{c(e):e\in\textit{cover}(t)\}
and
b_{t}(j)\coloneqq c(t,j)
for all

j\in\operatorname{dom}(b_{t})
.
It is easy to see that
L_{t}\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
(according to the base case of
Definition
8.10
).
Below, we will make extensive use of the particular
functions
r_{t},b_{t},g_{t}
of
L_{t}
.

All that remains to be done is to glue together the
L_{t}
’s for all

t\in V(T)
.
This has to be done with care. To describe the
construction, we use the following notation.
For every
t\in V(T)
let
T_{t}
denote the subtree of the rooted tree

(T,\omega)
rooted at
t
. By
{\hat{I}_{t}}
we denote the
incidence graph
associated with
T_{t}
, i.e.,

R({\hat{I}_{t}})=\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in V(T_{t})}\textit{bag}(t^{\prime})
,

B({\hat{I}_{t}})=\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in V(T_{t})}\textit{cover}(t^{\prime})
, and

E({\hat{I}_{t}})=E(I)\cap(B({\hat{I}_{t}})\times R({\hat{I}_{t}}))
.

We perform a bottom-up traversal of
(T,\omega)
to construct for each

t\in V(T)
a guarded
k
-labeled incidence graph
L^{\prime}_{t}=(I^{\prime}_{t},r^{\prime}_{t},b^{\prime}_{t},g^{\prime}_{t})\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}

and an isomorphism
\pi_{t}=(\pi_{t,R},\pi_{t,B})
from
{\hat{I}_{t}}
to

I^{\prime}_{t}
and ensure that
g^{\prime}_{t}=g_{t}
,
\operatorname{dom}(r^{\prime}_{t})=\operatorname{dom}(r_{t})
and

r^{\prime}_{t}(i)=\pi_{t,R}\big(r_{t}(i)\big)
for all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(r_{t})
, and

\operatorname{dom}(b^{\prime}_{t})=\operatorname{dom}(b_{t})
and

b^{\prime}_{t}(j)=\pi_{t,B}\big(b_{t}(j)\big)
for all
j\in\operatorname{dom}(b_{t})
.

Note that once having achieved this, the proof of
part
2
of Theorem
8.12
is
complete because
I={\hat{I}_{\omega}}\cong I^{\prime}_{\omega}
; we are
therefore done by choosing

L\coloneqq\big(L^{\prime}_{\omega}[\operatorname{dom}(r^{\prime}_{\omega}){\to}\bullet]\big)\langle\operatorname{dom}(b^{\prime}_{\omega}){\to}\bullet\rangle
.

For each
leaf

t
of the rooted tree
(T,\omega)
, we can
simply choose
L^{\prime}_{t}\coloneqq L_{t}
.

Let us now consider a tree-node
t_{p}
that has exactly one child
t
.
Our goal is to combine the
k
-labeled incidence graph
L_{t_{p}}

associated with the tree-node
t_{p}
with the
k
-labeled incidence
graph
L^{\prime}_{t}
already constructed for
T_{t}
into a single

k
-labeled incidence graph
L^{\prime}_{t_{p}}
for
T_{t_{p}}
. We do this by
glueing them at the red nodes in
\textit{bag}(t)\cap\textit{bag}(t_{p})
and the blue
nodes in
\textit{cover}(t)\cap\textit{cover}(t_{p})
. But we have to take care to use the
correct labels and end up with the correct guard function.
To this end, we first release from
L^{\prime}_{t}
all the red labels of
nodes in
\textit{bag}(t)\setminus\textit{bag}(t_{p})
, i.e., we let

A_{1}\ \ \coloneqq\ \ L^{\prime}_{t}\,[\,(\operatorname{dom}(r_{t})\setminus\operatorname{dom}(r_{t_{p}}))\,{\to}\bullet]\;.

Note that
g_{A_{1}}=g_{t}-(\operatorname{dom}(r_{t})\setminus\operatorname{dom}(r_{t_{p}}))
.
Let
{X_{b}}\coloneqq\{j\in c(t):c(t,j)\neq c(t_{p},j)\}
; this is the set
of blue labels that are used in
t
for a different node than in
t_{p}
.
Consider the set

{X_{r}}\coloneqq\{i\in\operatorname{dom}(r_{t})\cap\operatorname{dom}(r_{t_{p}}):s(t,v_{i})\neq s(t_{p},v_{i})\}
;
this is the set of red labels used in both
t
and
t_{p}
but whose
guard changes when moving from
t
to its parent
t_{p}
.

In case that
{X_{r}}=\varnothing
, we let

A_{2}\ \coloneqq\ A_{1}\,\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad L^{\prime}_{t_{p}}\coloneqq\ (L_{t_{p}}\mathbin{\cdot}A_{2})\;.

It can be
verified
6
6

6

see Appendix
D.4

that
{X_{b}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(b_{A_{1}})\setminus\operatorname{img}(g_{A_{1}})
;
hence
A_{2}\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
. It can also be
verified
7
7

7

see Appendix
D.4

that
g_{t_{p}}
and

g_{A_{2}}
are compatible, and thus also
L^{\prime}_{t_{p}}\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
.

In case that
{X_{r}}\neq\varnothing
we let
f
be the restriction of

g_{t_{p}}
to
{X_{r}}
, i.e.,
\operatorname{dom}(f)={X_{r}}
and
f(i)=g_{t_{p}}(i)
for
all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
.
It can be
verified
8
8

8

see Appendix
D.4

that
\operatorname{img}(f)\subseteq{X_{b}}
and that
f
is a transition for
g_{A_{1}}
.
We let
\hat{X}_{b}\coloneqq(\operatorname{img}(b_{A_{1}})\cap\operatorname{img}(f))\setminus\operatorname{img}(g_{A_{1}})
and
{\tilde{X}_{b}}\coloneqq{X_{b}}\setminus\operatorname{img}(f)
. Note that

\hat{X}_{b}\cap{\tilde{X}_{b}}=\varnothing
. We choose
\hat{A}_{1}\coloneqq A_{1}\langle\hat{X}_{b}{\to}\bullet\rangle
. Obviously
\hat{A}_{1}\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
and

f
is also a transition for
\hat{A}_{1}
. Let
\tilde{A}_{1}\coloneqq\hat{A}_{1}[{\leadsto}f]
, which is also obviously in
\textsl{GLI}_{k}
and then

A_{2}\coloneqq\tilde{A}_{1}\langle{\tilde{X}_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle
. I.e. we have

A_{2}\ \coloneqq\ \;\big(\,\;\big(\,A_{1}\langle\hat{X}_{b}{\to}\bullet\rangle\,\big)\;[{\leadsto}f]\,\big)\;\langle{\tilde{X}_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle\;.

Finally, we let
L^{\prime}_{t_{p}}\coloneqq(L_{t_{p}}\mathbin{\cdot}A_{2})
.
It can be
verified
9
9

9

see Appendix
D.4
that

{\tilde{X}_{b}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(b_{\tilde{A}_{1}})\setminus\operatorname{img}(g_{\tilde{A}_{1}})
; hence

A_{2}\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
.
It can also be
verified
10
10

10

see Appendix
D.4

that
g_{t_{p}}
and

g_{A_{2}}
are compatible, and thus also
L^{\prime}_{t_{p}}\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
.

In both cases it can be verified that
L^{\prime}_{t_{p}}
has the desired
properties.

The case of a tree-node
t_{p}
that has two children
t
and
t^{\prime}
can
be handled in the same way: Let
A_{2}
be constructed for
t
as
described above, and let
A^{\prime}_{2}
be constructed for
t^{\prime}
in the
analogous way; the construction is completed by letting

L^{\prime}_{t_{p}}\ \coloneqq\ (\;(L_{t_{p}}\mathbin{\cdot}A_{2})\,\mathbin{\cdot}A^{\prime}_{2}\,)\,.

This completes the proof of part
2
of
Theorem
8.12
.
∎

See Appendix
D.5
for an example illustrating
the above construction. It helps to get some insight into
k
-labeled
incidence graphs and especially into transitions.

D.4
Missing pieces in the proof of part
2

of Theorem
8.12

In case that
{X_{r}}=\varnothing
:

Proof that
{X_{b}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(b_{A_{1}})\setminus\operatorname{img}(g_{A_{1}})
.

It is clear that
{X_{b}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(b_{A_{1}})
holds since
c(t)=\operatorname{dom}(b_{L_{t}})=\operatorname{dom}(b_{L_{t}})
. Assume for contradiction, that
there exists an
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g_{A_{1}})
such that
g_{A_{1}}(i)=\ell\in{X_{b}}
. We have
b_{A_{1}}(\ell)=b_{L_{t}^{\prime}}(\ell)
and
i\in\operatorname{dom}(r_{t})\cap\operatorname{dom}(r_{t_{p}})
, thus
c(s(t,v_{i}))=\ell
. Since
\ell\in{X_{b}}
we have
c(t,\ell)\neq c(t_{p},\ell)
,
which means
s(t,v_{i})\neq s(t_{p},v_{i})
. But this contradicts our
assumption that
{X_{r}}=\varnothing
. Therefore, such an
i
cannot
exist.
∎

Proof that
g_{t_{p}}
and
g_{A_{2}}
are compatible.

Assume for contradiction that there exists an
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g_{t_{p}})\cap\operatorname{dom}(g_{A_{2}})
such that
g_{t_{p}}(i)\neq g_{A_{2}}(i)
.
We have that
g_{A_{2}}(i)=g_{t}(i)
. Therefore,
g_{t_{p}}(i)=c(s(t_{p},v_{i}))\neq c(s(t,v_{i}))=g_{t}(i)
. Then
s(t_{p},v_{i})\neq s(t,v_{i})
must hold, which contradicts
{X_{r}}=\varnothing
.
∎

In case that
{X_{r}}\neq\varnothing
:

Proof that
\operatorname{img}(f)\subseteq{X_{b}}
and
f
is a transition for
g_{A_{1}}
.

Let
\ell
be an element of
\operatorname{img}(f)
, and
i\in(\operatorname{dom}(r_{t})\cap\operatorname{dom}(r_{t_{p}}))
such that
s(t,v_{i})\neq s(t_{p},v_{i})
and

f(i)=g_{t_{p}}(i)=\ell
. Now, assume for contradiction that
\ell\not\in{X_{b}}
, i.e.
c(t,\ell)=c(t_{p},\ell)
. This would mean that

s(t,v_{i})=s(t_{p},v_{i})
, because
s
is a schedule. This clearly is
a contradiction.
∎

Proof that
{\tilde{X}_{b}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(b_{\tilde{A}_{1}})\setminus\operatorname{img}(g_{\tilde{A}_{1}})
.

It is clear that

{\tilde{X}_{b}}\subseteq(\operatorname{dom}(b_{L_{t}^{\prime}})\setminus\hat{X}_{b})\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(b_{\tilde{A}_{1}})
. Assume for contradiction, that there exists an

i\in\operatorname{dom}(g_{\tilde{A}_{1}})
such that
g_{\tilde{A}_{1}}(i)=\ell\in{\tilde{X}_{b}}
. Since
\ell\not\in\operatorname{img}(f)
we have
g_{\tilde{A}_{1}}(i)=g_{L_{t}^{\prime}}(i)=\ell
. Since
\ell\in{\tilde{X}_{b}}\subseteq{X_{b}}
we
have
c(t,\ell)\neq c(t_{p},\ell)
, which means
s(t,v_{i})\neq s(t_{p},v_{i})
. Therefore,
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
and
f(i)=\ell
, i.e.
\ell\in\operatorname{img}(f)
. This contradicts our assumption that
\ell\in{\tilde{X}_{b}}={X_{b}}\setminus\operatorname{img}(f)
.
∎

Proof that
g_{t_{p}}
and
g_{A_{2}}
are compatible.

Assume for contradiction that there exists an
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g_{t_{p}})\cap\operatorname{dom}(g_{A_{2}})
such that
g_{t_{p}}(i)\neq g_{A_{2}}(i)
.
If
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
, then
f(i)=g_{t_{p}}(i)
and since
f\subseteq g_{A_{2}}
we have a contradiction. Therefore, let
i\not\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
. Then we have that
g_{A_{2}}(i)=g_{t}(i)
. Therefore,

g_{t_{p}}(i)=c(s(t_{p},v_{i}))\neq c(s(t,v_{i}))=g_{t}(i)
. Then

s(t_{p},v_{i})\neq s(t,v_{i})
must hold, which contradicts
i\not\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
.
∎
