Note that we did not remove blue labels in this construction,
which is possible (and necessary) by the definition of
k
-labeled
incidence graphs. We need to do this in cases where we do not
have labeled red vertices in the bags connecting the blue ones, so
that we can apply a transition. Imagine for example the case that
neighbouring bags are disjoint. We can handle this by removing all
red labels and then all blue labels from one of these incidence
graphs. On the other hand, we do need transitions, as we can easily
verify that we can not construct the incidence graph
{I}_{E}

by removing blue labels instead of applying transitions, without
“temporarily leaving labeled red vertices unguarded”, which
must be prohibited. Because if we were to allow this, then it would be
easy to construct
k
-guarded incidence graphs with entangled
hypertree width larger than
k
.

D.6
Proof of Lemma
8.15

We first prove the lemma on
“ordinary”

k
-labeled incidence graphs.

Claim 1:

Let
L
,
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
be
k
-labeled incidence
graphs. It holds that
\operatorname{hom}((L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2}),L)=\operatorname{hom}(L_{1},L)\cdot\operatorname{hom}(L_{2},L)
.

Proof.

We
provide
a bijection
f
from
\operatorname{Hom}((L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2}),L)
to

\operatorname{Hom}(L_{1},L)\times\operatorname{Hom}(L_{2},L)
.

For
(h_{R},h_{B})\in\operatorname{Hom}((L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2}),L)
we define

f((h_{R},h_{B}))\coloneqq((h_{1,R},h_{1,B}),(h_{2,R},h_{2,B}))

where

h_{i,R}(v)=h_{R}(\pi_{i,R}(v))\;\text{ and }\;h_{i,B}(e)=h_{B}(\pi_{i,B}(e))

for every
i\in[2]
and all
v\in R(I_{L_{i}})
and all
e\in B(I_{L_{i}})
.
We have to prove that

•

f
is well-defined, i.e.
(h_{1,R},h_{1,B})
and
(h_{2,R},h_{2,B})
are homomorphisms;

•

f
is injective;

•

and
f
is surjective.

We prove that
(h_{1,R},h_{1,B})
is a
homomorphism. In an analogous
way, one can prove that
(h_{2,R},h_{2,B})
is homomorphism as well.

Let
(e,v)\in E(I_{L_{1}})
. We must show that
this edge exists in the image as well, i.e.
(h_{1,B}(e),\allowbreak h_{1,R}(v))\in E(I_{L})
. We have
h_{1,R}(v)=h_{R}(\pi_{1,R}(v))=h_{R}([(v,1)]_{\sim_{R}})
and

h_{1,B}(e)=h_{B}(\pi_{1,B}(e))=h_{B}([(e,1)]_{\sim_{B}})
. By definition, we have

([(e,1)]_{\sim_{B}},[(v,1)]_{\sim_{R}})\in E(I_{(L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2})})
,
therefore
(h_{B}([(e,1)]_{\sim_{B}}),h_{R}([(v,1)]_{\sim_{R}}))\in E(I_{L})
, since
(h_{R},h_{B})
is a homomorphism.

Let
i\in\operatorname{dom}(r_{L_{1}})
and
j\in\operatorname{dom}(b_{L_{1}})
. We must show, that

h_{1,R}(r_{L_{1}}(i))=r_{L}(i)
and

h_{1,B}(b_{L_{1}}(j))=b_{L}(j)
. This is easy
as well, since

\displaystyle h_{1,R}(r_{L_{1}}(i))

\displaystyle=h_{R}(\pi_{1,R}(r_{L_{1}}(i)))

\displaystyle=h_{R}([(1,r_{L_{1}(i)})]_{\sim_{R}})

\displaystyle=h_{R}(r_{(L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2})}(i))=r_{L}(i)\;,

and

\displaystyle h_{1,B}(b_{L_{1}}(j))

\displaystyle=h_{B}(\pi_{1,B}(b_{L_{1}}(j)))

\displaystyle=h_{B}([(1,b_{L_{1}(j)})]_{\sim_{B}})

\displaystyle=h_{B}(b_{(L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2})}(j))=b_{L}(j)\;.

Thus,
(h_{1,R},h_{1,B})
and therefore,
f
is well-defined.

It is easy to see, that
f
is injective: Assume that
there exists a homomorphism
(h_{R}^{\prime},h_{B}^{\prime})\in\operatorname{Hom}((L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2}),L)
such that
f(h_{R}^{\prime},h_{B}^{\prime})=f(h_{R},h_{B})
. Then we have for every
i\in[2]
that
h_{R}([(v,i)]_{\sim_{R}})=h_{R}^{\prime}([(v,i)]_{\sim_{R}})
holds for all
v\in R(I_{L_{i}})
and
h_{B}([(e,i)]_{\sim_{B}})=h_{B}^{\prime}([(e,i)]_{\sim_{B}})
holds for all
e\in B(I_{L_{i}})
. Since these
make up
all vertices,
(h_{R}^{\prime},h_{B}^{\prime})
and
(h_{R},h_{B})
agree on all values, therefore they are equal.

Consider
(h_{1,R},h_{1,B})\in\operatorname{Hom}(L_{1},L)

and
(h_{2,R},h_{2,B})\in\operatorname{Hom}(L_{2},L)
. For
every
i\in[2]
let
h_{R}([(v,i)]_{\sim_{R}})=h_{i,R}(v)
for all
v\in R(I_{L_{i}})
and

h_{B}([(e,i)]_{\sim_{B}})=h_{i,B}(e)
for all
e\in B(I_{L_{i}})
.
Assume that
h_{R}
is ill-defined. Then there must be

v_{1}\in R(I_{L_{1}})
and
v_{2}\in R(I_{L_{2}})
such that
v_{1}\in[(v_{2},2)]_{\sim_{R}}
and
h_{1,R}(v_{1})\neq h_{2,R}(v_{2})
.
v_{1}\in[(v_{2},2)]_{\sim_{R}}
means
that there exists a
j\in\operatorname{dom}(r_{L_{1}})\cap\operatorname{dom}(r_{L_{2}})
such that
r_{L_{1}}(j)=v_{1}
and

r_{L_{2}}(j)=v_{2}
. But this means
h_{1,R}(v_{1})=r_{L}(j)=h_{2,R}(v_{2})
, which is a
contradiction. Thus,
h_{R}
is well-defined. The same
argument works for
h_{B}
. With similar arguments as we
have used so far, it is easy to verify that
(h_{R},h_{B})\in\operatorname{Hom}((L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2}),L)
.

We showed that
f
is well-defined, injective and
surjective, thus bijective. Therefore,

|\operatorname{Hom}((L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2}),L)|=|\operatorname{Hom}(L_{1},L)|\cdot|\operatorname{Hom}(L_{2},L)|
.
∎

Claim 2:

Let
L
,
L^{\prime}
be
k
-labeled incidence graphs and
let
{X_{r}}\coloneqq\{i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell}\}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(r_{L})
with
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
.
It holds that

\operatorname{hom}(L[{X_{r}}{\to}\bullet],L^{\prime})=\sum_{\overline{v}\in R(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell}}\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}])\;.

Proof.

Let
(h_{R},h_{B})\in\operatorname{Hom}(L[{X_{r}}{\to}\bullet],L^{\prime})
be a
homomorphism.
Recall
that
I_{L}=I_{L[{X_{r}}{\to}\bullet]}
,
therefore we can define the following tuple of red
vertices in
L^{\prime}
:

\overline{v}=(v_{i_{1}},\dots,v_{i_{\ell}})\coloneqq(h_{R}(r_{L}(i_{1})),\dots,h_{R}(r_{L}(i_{\ell})))\;.

Clearly,
(h_{R},h_{B})\in\operatorname{Hom}(L,L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}])
, since
it is a homomorphism from
I_{L}=I_{L[{X_{r}}{\to}\bullet]}
to

I_{L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}]}=I_{L^{\prime}}
,
and we moved the red labels onto
(v_{i_{1}},\dots,v_{i_{\ell}})
.

For any tuple
\overline{v}\in R(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell}
it is easy to see that

\operatorname{Hom}(L,L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}])\ \subseteq\ \operatorname{Hom}(L[{X_{r}}{\to}\bullet],L^{\prime})\;.

It is also easy to see, that for all
\overline{v}\neq\overline{v}^{\prime}\in R(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell}

\operatorname{Hom}(L,L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}])\ \cap\ \operatorname{Hom}(L,L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}^{\prime}])\ =\ \varnothing\;.

This finishes our proof.
∎

Claim 3:

Let
L
,
L^{\prime}
be
k
-labeled incidence graphs and let
{X_{b}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(b_{L})
and
\ell\coloneqq|{X_{b}}|
. It holds that

\operatorname{hom}(L\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle,L^{\prime})=\sum_{\overline{e}\in B(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell}}\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)\;.

Proof.

The same argument as in the previous case works for this claim as well.
∎

Claim 4:

Let
L
,
L^{\prime}
be
k
-labeled incidence graphs and
let
f
be a transition for
L
. Let
{X_{b}}\coloneqq\operatorname{dom}(b_{L})\cap\operatorname{img}(f)\cap\operatorname{img}(g)
. It
holds that
\operatorname{hom}(L[{\leadsto}f],L^{\prime})=

\operatorname{hom}(M_{f},L^{\prime})\ \cdot\!\!\!\sum_{\overline{e}\in B(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell}}\!\!\!\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)\;.

Proof.

This follows directly from 1) and 3) and
Definition
8.8
.
∎

Generalising these results to quantum
k
-labeled incidence graphs is straightforward using simple linear arguments:

Part 1:

\operatorname{hom}((Q_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}Q_{2}),L)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[d]\atop j\in[d^{\prime}]}(\alpha_{i}\cdot\alpha_{j}^{\prime})\operatorname{hom}((L_{i}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{j}^{\prime}),L)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[d]\atop j\in[d^{\prime}]}(\alpha_{i}\cdot\alpha_{j}^{\prime})\cdot\operatorname{hom}(L_{i},L)\cdot\operatorname{hom}(L^{\prime}_{j},L)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[d]}\Big(\alpha_{i}\operatorname{hom}(L_{i},L)\cdot\sum_{j\in[d^{\prime}]}\alpha_{j}^{\prime}\operatorname{hom}(L_{j}^{\prime},L)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[d]}\Big(\alpha_{i}\operatorname{hom}(L_{i},L)\cdot\operatorname{hom}(Q_{2},L)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{hom}(Q_{2},L)\cdot\sum_{i\in[d]}\alpha_{i}\operatorname{hom}(L_{i},L)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{hom}(Q_{2},L)\cdot\operatorname{hom}(Q_{1},L)

Part 2:

\operatorname{hom}(Q[{X_{r}}{\to}\bullet],L)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[d]}\alpha_{i}\operatorname{hom}(L_{i}[L{\to}\bullet],L)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[d]}\alpha_{i}\;\Big(\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\overline{v}\in R(I_{L})^{\ell}}\!\!\!\!\operatorname{hom}(L_{i},L[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}])\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\overline{v}\in R(I_{L})^{\ell}}\ \Big(\sum_{i\in[d]}\alpha_{i}\operatorname{hom}(L_{i},L[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}])\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\overline{v}\in R(I_{L})^{\ell}}\ \operatorname{hom}(Q,L[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}])

Part 3:

The same argument as in the previous case works for this part as well.

Part 4:

This follows directly from 1) and 3).∎
Lemma
8.15

Appendix E
Details ommitted in Section
9

E.1
Complete proof of Lemma
9.1

Proof of
Lemma
9.1
.

Throughout this proof we use the following notation:
A
segmentation
for a number
m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geqslant 1}
is a pair of
d
-tuples

(\overline{c},\overline{m})\ \ =\ \ \big((c_{1},\dots,c_{d}),(m_{1},\dots,m_{d})\big)

of numbers in
\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
,
such that
d\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
and
m_{1}<\cdots<m_{d}
and

\sum_{i\in[d]}c_{i}m_{i}=m
.
We call
d
the
degree
of
(\overline{c},\overline{m})

and
c\coloneqq\sum_{i\in[d]}c_{i}
its
size
.

By
\textit{Seg}(m)
we denote the set of all segmentations of
m
.
Note that
\textit{Seg}(m)
is finite and non-empty for every
m\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
.

The proof of Lemma
9.1
proceeds by induction based on Definition
8.10
.

Base case
:

1)
R(I)=\operatorname{img}(r)
,
B(I)=\operatorname{img}(b)
, and
L
has real guards.
Obviously,
\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime})\in\{0,1\}
.
Let

\displaystyle S_{E}

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\{(j,i)\in\operatorname{dom}(b)\times\operatorname{dom}(r):(b(j),r(i))\in E(I)\},

\displaystyle S_{R}

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\{(i,i^{\prime}):i,i^{\prime}\in\operatorname{dom}(r),\ i<i^{\prime},\ r(i)=r(i^{\prime})\}\,,

\displaystyle S_{B}

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\{(j,j^{\prime}):j,j^{\prime}\in\operatorname{dom}(b),\ j<j^{\prime},\ b(j)=b(j^{\prime})\}\,.

We choose
\varphi_{L,1}\coloneqq

\bigwedge_{(j,i)\in S_{E}}\!\!\!E(\texttt{{e}}_{j},\texttt{{v}}_{i})\ \ \wedge\bigwedge_{(i,i^{\prime})\in S_{R}}\!\!\!\texttt{{v}}_{i}{=}\texttt{{v}}_{i^{\prime}}\ \ \wedge\bigwedge_{(j,j^{\prime})\in S_{B}}\!\!\!\texttt{{e}}_{j}{=}\texttt{{e}}_{j^{\prime}}\,.

It is straightforward to verify
that
(\Delta_{g}\wedge\varphi_{L,1})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.

Let
L^{\prime}
satisfy the lemma’s assumptions. Since
L^{\prime}
has real guards w.r.t.
g
we
have
\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\Delta_{g}
. By construction of the
formula
\varphi_{L,1}
there is a homomorphism from
L
to

L^{\prime}
if, and only if,

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\varphi_{L,1}
.
Thus, we are done for the case
m=1
.

For
m=0
we are done by choosing
\varphi_{L,0}\coloneqq\neg\,\varphi_{L,1}
.

For
m\geqslant 2
we know that
\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime})\neq m
. Thus, we are done by choosing an arbitrary unsatisfiable formula; to be specific we choose

\varphi_{L^{\prime},m}\coloneqq(\varphi_{L^{\prime},0}\wedge\varphi_{L^{\prime},1})
.

Inductive step:

We assume that the lemma’s statement is already shown for
L=(I,r,b,g)\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
and
for
L_{i}=(I_{i},r_{i},b_{i},g_{i})\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
for
i\in[2]
where
g_{1},g_{2}
are compatible.
Our goal is to prove the lemma’s statement for the particular
\tilde{L}=(\tilde{I},\tilde{r},\tilde{b},\tilde{g})\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}

considered in the following case distinction.

2) Let
\tilde{L}\coloneqq L[{X_{r}}{\to}\bullet]
for some
{X_{r}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(r)
.
If
{X_{r}}=\varnothing
we are done since
\tilde{L}=L
.
If
{X_{r}}\neq\varnothing
let
\ell\coloneqq|{X_{r}}|
and let
{X_{r}}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}
with
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
.
Note that
\tilde{r}=r-{X_{r}}
,
\tilde{b}=b
, and
\tilde{g}=g-{X_{r}}
.
We let

\varphi_{\tilde{L},0}\ \ \coloneqq\ \ \forall(\texttt{{v}}_{i_{1}},\dots,\texttt{{v}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\to\varphi_{L,0})\,.

It is straightforward to verify that

(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi_{\tilde{L},0})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.

For every
m\geqslant 1
we let

\varphi_{\tilde{L},m}\ \ \coloneqq\ \ \bigvee_{(\overline{c},\overline{m})\in\textit{Seg}(m)}\varphi_{\overline{c},\overline{m}}

where for every

(\overline{c},\overline{m})=((c_{1},\ldots,c_{d}),(m_{1},\ldots,m_{d}))\in\textit{Seg}(m)

and
c\coloneqq\sum_{i\in[d]}c_{i}

we let

\begin{array}[]{ll}\varphi_{\overline{c},\overline{m}}\ \ \coloneqq&\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$=$}c}(\texttt{{v}}_{i_{1}},\dots,\texttt{{v}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\land\lnot\varphi_{L,0})\ \ \land\\[4.30554pt]
&\bigwedge_{j\in[d]}\left(\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$=$}c_{j}}(\texttt{{v}}_{i_{1}},\dots,\texttt{{v}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\land\varphi_{L,m_{j}}\right)\,.\end{array}

It is straightforward to verify
that
(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi_{\overline{c},\overline{m}})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
; and hence also
(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi_{\tilde{L},m})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.

Let
L^{\prime}=(I^{\prime},r^{\prime},b^{\prime},g^{\prime})
satisfy the lemma’s assumptions for
\tilde{L}
instead of
L
. I.e.,
we have

\operatorname{dom}(b^{\prime})\supseteq\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{b})=\operatorname{dom}(b)
,

\operatorname{dom}(r^{\prime})\supseteq\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{r})=\operatorname{dom}(r)\setminus{X_{r}}
, and

L^{\prime}
has real guards w.r.t.
\tilde{g}
. Thus,

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\Delta_{\tilde{g}}
.

From Lemma
8.15
we know that

n\ \coloneqq\ \operatorname{hom}(\tilde{L},L^{\prime})\ \ =\sum_{\bar{v}\in R(I^{\prime})^{\ell}}\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}]).

(13)

Note that
n=0

\iff

\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}])=0
for all

\bar{v}\in R(I^{\prime})^{\ell}

\iff
for all
\bar{v}\in R(I^{\prime})^{\ell}
we
have either

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}]}\not\models\Delta_{g}

(this is in case that
\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}]}
does
not have real guards w.r.t.
g
) or

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}]}\models(\Delta_{g}\wedge\varphi_{L,0})

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\varphi_{\tilde{L},0}
.
Thus, the formula
\varphi_{\tilde{L},0}
has the desired meaning.

Let us now consider the case where
n\geqslant 1
.
For every number
m^{\prime}\in[n]
let

S_{m^{\prime}}
be the set of all
\bar{v}\in R(I^{\prime})^{\ell}
such that

m^{\prime}=\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}])
.
Let
d^{\prime}
be the number of distinct
m^{\prime}\in[n]
for which
S_{m^{\prime}}\neq\varnothing
, and let

m^{\prime}_{1}<\cdots<m^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}
be an ordered list of all
m^{\prime}\in[n]
with
S_{m^{\prime}}\neq\varnothing
.
For each
j\in[d^{\prime}]
let
c^{\prime}_{j}\coloneqq|S_{m^{\prime}_{j}}|
. Let
\overline{c}^{\prime}\coloneqq(c^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,c^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}})
and

\overline{m}^{\prime}\coloneqq(m^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,m^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}})
.
Note that

\sum_{j\in[d^{\prime}]}c^{\prime}_{j}m^{\prime}_{j}=n
. Hence

(\overline{c}^{\prime},\overline{m}^{\prime})\in\textit{Seg}(n)
is a segmentation of
n

of size
c^{\prime}\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{d^{\prime}}c^{\prime}_{j}
and of degree
d^{\prime}
.

Note that
\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\varphi_{\overline{c}^{\prime},\overline{m}^{\prime}}
. Furthermore,
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
we have:

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\varphi_{\tilde{L},m}

\iff

(\overline{m}^{\prime},\overline{c}^{\prime})\in\textit{Seg}(m)

\iff

m=n=\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{L},L^{\prime})
.
This verifies that for every
m\geqslant 1
the formula
\varphi_{\tilde{L},m}
has the
desired properties.

3) Let
\tilde{L}\coloneqq L\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle
for some
{X_{b}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(b)\setminus\operatorname{img}(g)
.
If
{X_{b}}=\varnothing
we are done since
\tilde{L}=L
.
If
{X_{b}}\neq\varnothing
let
\ell\coloneqq|{X_{b}}|
and let

{X_{b}}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}
with
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
. Note that

\tilde{r}=r
,
\tilde{b}=b-{X_{b}}
, and
\tilde{g}=g
.
We let

\varphi_{\tilde{L},0}\ \ \coloneqq\ \ \forall(\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\dots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\to\varphi_{L,0})\,.

For every
m\geqslant 1
we let

\varphi_{\tilde{L},m}\ \ \coloneqq\ \ \bigvee_{(\overline{c},\overline{m})\in\textit{Seg}(m)}\varphi^{\prime}_{\overline{c},\overline{m}}

where for every

(\overline{c},\overline{m})=((c_{1},\ldots,c_{d}),(m_{1},\ldots,m_{d}))\in\textit{Seg}(m)

and
c\coloneqq\sum_{i\in[d]}c_{i}

we let

\begin{array}[]{ll}\varphi^{\prime}_{\overline{c},\overline{m}}\ \ \coloneqq&\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$=$}c}(\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\dots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\land\lnot\varphi_{L,0})\ \ \land\\[4.30554pt]
&\bigwedge_{j\in[d]}\left(\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$=$}c_{j}}(\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\dots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\land\varphi_{L,m_{j}}\right)\,.\end{array}

Let
L^{\prime}=(I^{\prime},r^{\prime},b^{\prime},g^{\prime})
satisfy the lemma’s assumptions for
\tilde{L}
instead of
L
. I.e.,
we have

\operatorname{dom}(b^{\prime})\supseteq\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{b})=\operatorname{dom}(b)\setminus{X_{b}}
,

\operatorname{dom}(r^{\prime})\supseteq\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{r})=\operatorname{dom}(r)
, and

L^{\prime}
has real guards w.r.t.
\tilde{g}
. Thus,

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\Delta_{\tilde{g}}
.
From Lemma
8.15
we know that

n\ \coloneqq\ \operatorname{hom}(\tilde{L},L^{\prime})\ \ =\sum_{\bar{e}\in B(I^{\prime})^{\ell}}\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle).

(14)

Note that
n=0

\iff

\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)=0
for all

\bar{e}\in B(I^{\prime})^{\ell}

\iff
for all
\bar{e}\in B(I^{\prime})^{\ell}
we
have
either
\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle}\not\models\Delta_{g}
or

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle}\models(\Delta_{g}\wedge\varphi_{L,0})

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\varphi_{\tilde{L},0}
.
Thus, the formula
\varphi_{\tilde{L},0}
has the desired meaning.

Let us now consider the case where
n\geqslant 1
.
For every number
m^{\prime}\in[n]
let

S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}
be the set of all
\bar{e}\in B(I^{\prime})^{\ell}
such that

m^{\prime}=\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)
.
Let
d^{\prime}
be the number of distinct
m^{\prime}\in[n]
for which
S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\neq\varnothing
, and let

m^{\prime}_{1}<\cdots<m^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}
be an ordered list of all
m^{\prime}\in[n]
with
S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\neq\varnothing
.
For each
j\in[d^{\prime}]
let
c^{\prime}_{j}\coloneqq|S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}_{j}}|
. Let
\overline{c}^{\prime}\coloneqq(c^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,c^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}})
and

\overline{m}^{\prime}\coloneqq(m^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,m^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}})
.
Note that

\sum_{j\in[d^{\prime}]}c^{\prime}_{j}m^{\prime}_{j}=n
. Hence

(\overline{c}^{\prime},\overline{m}^{\prime})\in\textit{Seg}(n)
is a segmentation of
n

of size
c^{\prime}\coloneqq\sum_{j\in[d^{\prime}]}c^{\prime}_{j}
and of degree
d^{\prime}
.

Note that
\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\varphi^{\prime}_{\overline{c}^{\prime},\overline{m}^{\prime}}
. Furthermore,
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
we have:

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\varphi_{\tilde{L},m}

\iff

(\overline{m}^{\prime},\overline{c}^{\prime})\in\textit{Seg}(m)

\iff

m=n=\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{L},L^{\prime})
.
This verifies that for every
m\geqslant 1
the formula
\varphi_{\tilde{L},m}
has the
desired meaning.

It remains to verify that

(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi_{\tilde{L},m})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Recall that
\tilde{g}=g
.
Thus,
\tilde{g}
satisfies the condition (
6
) of
rule
7
) of the syntax definition of
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.
Since, by the induction hypothesis,

(\Delta_{g}\wedge\varphi_{L,m_{j}})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
, we obtain by rule

7
) that the conjunction of
\Delta_{\tilde{g}}
with
the formula in the second line of
\varphi^{\prime}_{\overline{c},\overline{m}}

belongs to
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.
The same reasoning (combined with rule
4
)) yields that the conjunction of
\Delta_{\tilde{g}}
with
the formula in the first line of
\varphi^{\prime}_{\overline{c},\overline{m}}

belongs to
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.
Applying rule
5
)
yields that each formula

(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi^{\prime}_{\overline{c},\overline{m}})

as well as the formula

(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi_{\tilde{L},m})

belongs to
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
, for every
m\geqslant 1
.
A similar reasoning shows that also
(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi_{\tilde{L},0})

belongs to
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.

4) Let
\tilde{L}\coloneqq L[{\leadsto}f]
, where
f
is a transition for
g
.
I.e.,

\varnothing\neq\operatorname{dom}(f)\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(g)
, such that for every
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g)
with

g(i)\in\operatorname{img}(f)
we have
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
. By definition,

\tilde{L}=(M_{f}\mathbin{\cdot}L\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\bullet\rangle)
for
{X_{b}}\coloneqq\operatorname{img}(g)\cap\operatorname{img}(f)\cap\operatorname{dom}(b)
.
In particular,
\tilde{g}=f\cup g
, i.e.,
\tilde{g}(i)=f(i)
for all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
, and

\tilde{g}(i)=g(i)
for all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g)\setminus\operatorname{dom}(f)
.

Consider an arbitrary
L^{\prime}=(I^{\prime},r^{\prime},b^{\prime},g^{\prime})
that satisfies the lemma’s assumptions for
\tilde{L}
instead of
L
. I.e.,
we have

\operatorname{dom}(b^{\prime})\supseteq\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{b})=\operatorname{img}(f)\cup\operatorname{dom}(b)
,

\operatorname{dom}(r^{\prime})\supseteq\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{r})=\operatorname{dom}(f)\cup\operatorname{dom}(r)=\operatorname{dom}(f)\cup\operatorname{dom}(g)=\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{g})
,
and

L^{\prime}
has real guards w.r.t.
\tilde{g}
.
Since
L^{\prime}
has real guards w.r.t.
\tilde{g}
we have

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\Delta_{\tilde{g}}
.
From Lemma
8.15
we know that
n\coloneqq\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{L},L^{\prime})=

\operatorname{hom}(M_{f},L^{\prime})\ \ {\cdot}\!\!\sum_{\bar{e}\in B(I^{\prime})^{\ell}}\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)

for
{X_{b}}\coloneqq\operatorname{dom}(b)\cap\operatorname{img}(f)\cap\operatorname{img}(g)
.
Furthermore, we know that
\operatorname{hom}(M_{f},L^{\prime})\in\{0,1\}
.
Note that
\operatorname{hom}(M_{f},L^{\prime})=1

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\Delta_{f}
.
Since
\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\Delta_{\tilde{g}}
and
\tilde{g}\supseteq f
,
we obviously have
\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\Delta_{f}
.
Thus,
n=\sum_{\bar{e}\in B(I^{\prime})^{\ell}}\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)
, as
in case 3) in equation (
14
).
We therefore can choose the exact same formulas
\varphi_{\tilde{L},m}
as in case 3)
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and obtain that these formulas have the desired meaning.

It remains to verify that

(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi_{\tilde{L},m})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Note that now we have
\tilde{g}=f\cup g
(whereas in case 3) we had
to deal with the case where
\tilde{g}=g
).
All we have to do is argue that
\tilde{g}
satisfies the
condition (
6
) of
rule
7
) of the syntax definition of
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
.
Once having achieved this, the same reasoning as in case 3)
proves that the formula
(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi_{\tilde{L},m})
belongs to

\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Note that according to the induction hypothesis, for all
m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
we have:

\operatorname{dom}(b)=\textit{ifree}_{B}((\Delta_{g}\wedge\varphi_{L,m^{\prime}}))\supseteq\operatorname{img}(g)
.
Therefore,
{X_{b}}=\operatorname{img}(f)\cap\operatorname{img}(g)
.

Consider an arbitrary
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g)
. We have to show that

\tilde{g}(i)=g(i)
or
\tilde{g}(i)\in{X_{b}}
or
\tilde{g}(i)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)
.

If
\tilde{g}(i)=g(i)

or
\tilde{g}(i)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)
, we are done.
Consider the case that

\tilde{g}(i)\neq g(i)

and

\tilde{g}(i)\in\operatorname{img}(g)
.
In this case,
\tilde{g}(i)=f(i)
and there exists an
i^{\prime}\in\operatorname{dom}(g)

with
g(i^{\prime})=\tilde{g}(i)=f(i)
.
Hence,
\tilde{g}(i)\in\operatorname{img}(f)\cap\operatorname{img}(g)={X_{b}}
, and we are done.

5) Let
\tilde{L}\coloneqq(L_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{2})
. Recall from the
inductive step’s assumption that the lemma’s statement is already
shown for
L_{i}=(I_{i},r_{i},b_{i},g_{i})
for each
i\in[2]
and that

g_{1},g_{2}
are compatible.
Recall from Definition
8.5
that

\tilde{L}=(\tilde{I},\tilde{r},\tilde{b},\tilde{g})
with

\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{r})=\operatorname{dom}(r_{1})\cup\operatorname{dom}(r_{2})
,

\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{b})=\operatorname{dom}(b_{1})\cup\operatorname{dom}(b_{2})
, and

\tilde{g}=g_{1}\cup g_{2}
.
We let

\varphi_{\tilde{L},0}\ \ \coloneqq\ \ \big(\,\varphi_{L_{1},0}\ \vee\ \varphi_{L_{2},0}\,\big)\,.

For each
m\geqslant 1
we let
S_{m}
be the set of all tuples
(m_{1},m_{2})

of positive integers such that
m_{1}\cdot m_{2}=m
(note that
S_{m}
is
finite and non-empty), and let

\varphi_{\tilde{L},m}\ \ \coloneqq\ \ \bigvee_{(m_{1},m_{2})\in S_{m}}\big(\,\varphi_{L_{1},m_{1}}\ \wedge\ \varphi_{L_{2},m_{2}}\,\big)\,.

By the induction hypothesis we know that
(\Delta_{g_{i}}\wedge\varphi_{L_{i},m_{i}})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}

for each
i\in[2]
and every
m_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
\tilde{g}=g_{1}\cup g_{2}
and
g_{1},g_{2}
are compatible, we obtain that the formula

(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\wedge\varphi_{\tilde{L},m})
belongs to
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}

for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

All that remains to be done is to show that these formulas have the
desired meaning.
Consider an arbitrary
L^{\prime}=(I^{\prime},r^{\prime},b^{\prime},g^{\prime})
that satisfies the
lemma’s assumptions
for
\tilde{L}
instead of
L
. I.e., we have

\operatorname{dom}(b^{\prime})\supseteq\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{b})=\operatorname{dom}(b_{1})\cup\operatorname{dom}(b_{2})
,

\operatorname{dom}(r^{\prime})\supseteq\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{r})=\operatorname{dom}(b_{1})\cup\operatorname{dom}(b_{2})
, and

g^{\prime}\supseteq\tilde{g}=g_{1}\cup g_{2}
.

Since
L^{\prime}
has real guards w.r.t.
\tilde{g}
we have

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\Delta_{\tilde{g}}
.
From Lemma
8.15
we know that

n\ \coloneqq\ \operatorname{hom}(\tilde{L},L^{\prime})\ =\ \operatorname{hom}(L_{1},L^{\prime})\cdot\operatorname{hom}(L_{2},L^{\prime})\,.

(15)

Note that
n=0

\iff

\operatorname{hom}(L_{1},L^{\prime})=0
or

\operatorname{hom}(L_{2},L^{\prime})=0
.
Thus,
\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\varphi_{\tilde{L},0}

\iff

n=0
.
Hence, the formula
\varphi_{\tilde{L},0}
has the desired meaning.

Let us now consider the case where
n\geqslant 1
.
For
i\in[2]
let
n_{i}\coloneqq\operatorname{hom}(L_{i},L^{\prime})
.
By (
15
) we have
n=n_{1}\cdot n_{2}
. Thus,

(n_{1},n_{2})\in S_{n}
.
Note for every
m\geqslant 1
that

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\varphi_{\tilde{L},m}

\iff

(n_{1},n_{2})\in S_{m}

\iff

n=n_{1}\cdot n_{2}=m

\iff

\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{L},L^{\prime})=m
.
Hence, for every
m\geqslant 1
the formula
\varphi_{\tilde{L},m}
has the desired meaning.
This completes the proof of Lemma
9.1
.
∎

E.2
Complete proof of Lemma
9.2

Proof of Lemma
9.2
.

We proceed by induction on the construction of
\chi
.

Base cases
:

\chi
is
(\Delta_{g}\land\psi)
and matches one of the
following:

Case 1
:

\psi
is
E(\texttt{{e}}_{j},\texttt{{v}}_{i})
and
\operatorname{dom}(g)=\{i\}
and
j\in[k]
. Note that
\chi=(E(\texttt{{e}}_{g(i)},\texttt{{v}}_{i})\wedge E(\texttt{{e}}_{j},\texttt{{v}}_{i}))
.
Let
I
be the incidence graph defined as follows.

I
has exactly one red node, i.e.,
R(I)=\{v_{i}\}
.
If
g(i)=j
then
I
has exactly one blue node, i.e.,

B(I)=\{e_{j}\}
; otherwise
I
has exactly two blue nodes, i.e.,

B(I)=\{e_{j},e_{g(i)}\}
.

I
has edges from every blue node to the red node, i.e.,

E(I)=B(I)\times R(I)
.
Consider the
k
-labeled incidence graph
L=(I,r,b,g)
where

\operatorname{dom}(r)=\{i\}
and
r(i)=v_{i}
, and
\operatorname{dom}(b)=\{j,g(i)\}
and

b(j)=e_{j}
and
b(g(i))=e_{g(i)}
(and
g
is the formula
\chi
’s
guard function).
Note that
L\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
(use the base case of
Definition
8.10
).
For every
k
-labeled incidence graph
L^{\prime}
we have:

\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime})\in\{0,1\}
, and

\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime})=1

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\chi
.
Thus we are done by choosing
Q\coloneqq L
.

Case 2
:

\psi
is
\texttt{{e}}_{j}{=}\texttt{{e}}_{j^{\prime}}
and
\operatorname{dom}(g)=\varnothing
and

j,j^{\prime}\in[k]
.
Note that
\chi=(\top\wedge\texttt{{e}}_{j}{=}\texttt{{e}}_{j^{\prime}})
.
Let
I
be the incidence graph with no red node and
exactly one blue node, i.e.,
R(I)=\varnothing
,
B(I)=\{e\}
, and

E(I)=\varnothing
.
Consider the
k
-labeled incidence graph
L=(I,r,b,g)
where

\operatorname{dom}(r)=\varnothing
,
\operatorname{dom}(b)=\{j,j^{\prime}\}
and
b(j)=b(j^{\prime})=e
(and
g

is the formula
\chi
’s (empty) guard function).
Note that
L\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
(use the base case of
Definition
8.10
).
For every
k
-labeled incidence graph
L^{\prime}
we have:

\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime})\in\{0,1\}
, and

\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime})=1

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\chi
.
Thus we are done by choosing
Q\coloneqq L
.

Case 3
:

\psi
is
\texttt{{v}}_{i}{=}\texttt{{v}}_{i^{\prime}}
with
\operatorname{dom}(g)=\{i,i^{\prime}\}
.
Note that
\chi=(E(\texttt{{e}}_{g(i)},\texttt{{v}}_{i})\wedge E(\texttt{{e}}_{g(i^{\prime})},\texttt{{v}}_{i^{\prime}})\wedge\texttt{{v}}_{i}{=}\texttt{{v}}_{i^{\prime}})
.
Let
I
be the incidence graph defined as follows.

I
has exactly one red node, i.e.,
R(I)=\{v\}
.
If
g(i)=g(i^{\prime})
then
I
has exactly one blue node, i.e.,

B(I)=\{e\}
; otherwise
I
has exactly two blue nodes, i.e.,

B(I)=\{e_{g(i)},e_{g(i^{\prime})}\}
.

I
has edges from every blue node to the red node, i.e.,

E(I)=B(I)\times R(I)
.
Consider the
k
-labeled incidence graph
L=(I,r,b,g)
where

g
is the formula
\chi
’s
guard function, and

\operatorname{dom}(r)=\{i,i^{\prime}\}
and
r(i)=r(i^{\prime})=v
, and

\operatorname{dom}(b)=\{g(i),g(i^{\prime})\}
.
In case that
g(i)=g(i^{\prime})
we have
b(g(i))=e
, and otherwise we have

b(g(i))=e_{g(i)}
and
b(g(i^{\prime}))=e_{g(i^{\prime})}
.
Note that
L\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
(use the base case of
Definition
8.10
).
For every
k
-labeled incidence graph
L^{\prime}
we have:

\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime})\in\{0,1\}
, and

\operatorname{hom}(L,L^{\prime})=1

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\chi
.
Thus we are done by choosing
Q\coloneqq L
.

Special Case
:

\psi
is unfulfillable for all
k
-labeled incidence graphs
L^{\prime}
matching
the requirements imposed by the lemma.

If
\operatorname{dom}(g)\neq\varnothing
we choose
L\coloneqq M_{g}
and
Q\coloneqq 0{\cdot}L
.
Note that
Q\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
and
g_{Q},\textup{db}_{Q},\textup{dr}_{Q}
are as required by the
lemma. Furthermore,
\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime})=0
for every
k
-labeled incidence
graph
L^{\prime}
.

If
\operatorname{dom}(g)=\varnothing
and
\operatorname{free}(\chi)\neq\varnothing
we choose
I

to consist of a single blue node
e
and no red nodes, and we let

L=(I,r,b,g)
with
\operatorname{dom}(r)=\operatorname{dom}(g)=\varnothing
and

\operatorname{dom}(b)=\textit{ifree}_{B}(\chi)
and

b(j)=e
for
every
j\in\textit{ifree}_{B}(\chi)
. Let
Q\coloneqq 0{\cdot}L
.
Note that
Q\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
and
g_{Q},\textup{db}_{Q},\textup{dr}_{Q}
are as required by the
lemma. Furthermore,
\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime})=0
for every
k
-labeled incidence
graph
L^{\prime}
.

If
\operatorname{dom}(g)=\varnothing
and
\operatorname{free}(\chi)=\varnothing
we choose
I
to
be empty, i.e.,
R(I)=B(I)=E(I)=\varnothing
,
and we let

L=(I,r,b,g)
with
\operatorname{dom}(r)=\operatorname{dom}(b)=\operatorname{dom}(g)=\varnothing
.
Let
Q\coloneqq 0{\cdot}L
.
Note that
Q\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
and
g_{Q},\textup{db}_{Q},\textup{dr}_{Q}
are as required by the
lemma. Furthermore,
\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime})=0
for every
k
-labeled incidence
graph
L^{\prime}
.

Inductive step
: We assume that the lemma’s
statement is already shown for

\chi\coloneqq(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
and for

\chi_{i}\coloneqq(\Delta_{g_{i}}\wedge\psi_{i})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
for
i\in[2]
, where

g_{1},g_{2}
are compatible.

Fix arbitrary
m,d\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\geqslant 1
.
Let
Q,Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
be the quantum incidence graphs provided by
the lemma’s statement for

\chi,\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
and the parameters
m,d
.

Our goal is to prove the lemma’s statement for parameters
m,d
and the particular

\tilde{\chi}\coloneqq(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}\land\tilde{\psi})\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
considered in the
following case distinction.
We will write
\tilde{Q}
to denote the quantum incidence
graph that has to be constructed for
\tilde{\chi}
.

Case 4
:

\tilde{\psi}=\lnot\psi
and
\tilde{g}=g
.
We choose
\tilde{Q}\coloneqq Q[\{1\},\{0\}]

according to Lemma
8.16
.
Note that
\tilde{Q}\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
and
\tilde{Q}
has the parameters

g_{\tilde{Q}},\textup{db}_{\tilde{Q}},\textup{dr}_{\tilde{Q}}
required by the
lemma.
Furthermore, for all
k
-labeled incidence graphs
L^{\prime}
satisfying
the lemma’s assumptions we have:
\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\Delta_{g}

and
\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{Q},L^{\prime})\in\{0,1\}
. Furthermore,

\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{Q},L^{\prime})=1

\iff

\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime})=0

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\not\models\chi
. Since

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\Delta_{g}
by assumption, we have:

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\not\models\chi

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\not\models\psi

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\neg\psi

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\tilde{\chi}
.
This completes Case 4.

Case 5
:

\tilde{\psi}=(\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2})
and
\tilde{g}=g_{1}\cup g_{2}
(recall that
g_{1}

and
g_{2}
are compatible).
We choose
\tilde{Q}\coloneqq(Q_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}Q_{2})
.
Note that
\tilde{Q}\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
and
\tilde{Q}
has the parameters

g_{\tilde{Q}},\textup{db}_{\tilde{Q}},\textup{dr}_{\tilde{Q}}
required by the lemma.

Consider an arbitrary
k
-labeled incidence graph
L^{\prime}
satisfying
the lemma’s assumptions.
By Lemma
8.15
we have

\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{Q},L^{\prime})=\operatorname{hom}(Q_{1},L^{\prime})\cdot\operatorname{hom}(Q_{2},L^{\prime})
.
By the induction hypothesis we know for each
i\in[2]
that

\operatorname{hom}(Q_{i},L^{\prime})\in\{0,1\}
; and

\operatorname{hom}(Q_{i},L^{\prime})=1

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\chi_{i}
.
Thus,
\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{Q},L^{\prime})\in\{0,1\}
. Furthermore,

\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{Q},L^{\prime})=1

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\chi_{1}
and

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\chi_{2}

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\tilde{\chi}
.
This completes Case 5.

Case 6
:

\tilde{\psi}=\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n}(\texttt{{v}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{v}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\land\psi)
and

\tilde{g}=g-\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}
and
n,\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}

and
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
and

{X_{r}}\coloneqq\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(g)
.

If
n>(md)^{\ell}
, the
formula
\tilde{\psi}

is unfulfillable for all
k
-labeled incidence graphs
L^{\prime}
matching
the requirements imposed by the lemma (simply because each such

L^{\prime}
has at most
md
red nodes). Thus, we are done by choosing

\tilde{Q}
according to the “special case” handled above.

If
n\leqslant(md)^{\ell}
, we proceed as follows.
Let
Q^{\prime}\coloneqq Q[{X_{r}}{\to}\bullet]
and

\tilde{Q}\coloneqq Q^{\prime}[X,Y]
for
X\coloneqq\{0,\ldots,n{-}1\}
and

Y\coloneqq\{n,\ldots,(md)^{\ell}\}
.
Note that
Q^{\prime}\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
and
\tilde{Q}\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
, and

\tilde{Q}
has the parameters

g_{\tilde{Q}},\textup{db}_{\tilde{Q}},\textup{dr}_{\tilde{Q}}
required by the
lemma.
Consider an arbitrary
k
-labeled incidence graph
L^{\prime}
satisfying
the lemma’s assumptions for
\tilde{\chi}
. In particular,
L^{\prime}
has
real guards w.r.t.
\tilde{g}
and therefore satisfies

\Delta_{\tilde{g}}
. Thus,
\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\tilde{\chi}

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\tilde{\psi}
.
By Lemma
8.15
we have

\operatorname{hom}(Q^{\prime},L^{\prime})\ =\ \sum_{\overline{v}\in R(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell}}\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}])\,.

Consider an arbitrary
\overline{v}
with

\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}])>0
.
Since
Q
has real guards, the
k
-labeled incidence graph

L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}]
satisfies the lemma’s assumptions
w.r.t. the formula
\chi
(in particular, it has real guards w.r.t.
g
). Therefore, by the induction hypothesis we obtain that

\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}])=1
and

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}]}\models\chi
.

Therefore,

\operatorname{hom}(Q^{\prime},L^{\prime})\leqslant|R(I_{L^{\prime}})|^{\ell}\leqslant(md)^{\ell}
.
Moreover,
\operatorname{hom}(Q^{\prime},L^{\prime})
is exactly the number of tuples

\overline{v}\in R(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell}
such that

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}[{X_{r}}{\to}\overline{v}]}\models\chi
.

By our choice of
X,Y
and
\tilde{Q}=Q^{\prime}[X,Y]
we obtain that

\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{Q},L^{\prime})\in\{0,1\}
and, moreover,

\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{Q},L^{\prime})=1

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\tilde{\chi}
.
This completes Case 6.

Case 7
:

\tilde{\psi}=\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\geqslant$}n}(\texttt{{e}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\texttt{{e}}_{i_{\ell}}).(\Delta_{g}\land\psi)
and

\tilde{g}
, where
n,\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
and

S\coloneqq\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}\subseteq\textit{ifree}_{B}(\chi)
for

\chi\coloneqq(\Delta_{g}\wedge\psi)

with

i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
and
\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{g})=\operatorname{dom}(g)
and
all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g)
satisfy

\tilde{g}(i)=g(i)\quad\text{or}\quad\tilde{g}(i)\in S\quad\text{or}\quad\tilde{g}(i)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g).

(16)

If
n>m^{\ell}
, the formula
\tilde{\psi}
is unfulfillable for all

k
-labeled incidence graphs
L^{\prime}
matching the requirements imposed
by the lemma (simply because each such
L^{\prime}
has only
m
blue
nodes).
Thus, we are done by choosing
\tilde{Q}
according to the “special
case” handled above.

If
n\leqslant m^{\ell}
, we proceed as follows.
Since
Q
is obtained by the induction hypothesis, we know in
particular that

g_{Q}=g
,
\textup{db}_{Q}=\textit{ifree}_{B}(\chi)
, and
\textup{dr}_{Q}=\operatorname{dom}(g)=\textit{ifree}_{R}(\chi)
.
In particular, we have:
S\subseteq\textup{db}_{Q}
.

Claim 1:
Consider an arbitrary
k
-labeled incidence graph
L^{\prime}
satisfying
the lemma’s assumptions for
\tilde{\chi}
.
For

\textit{num}(L^{\prime})\ \coloneqq\ \operatorname{hom}(Q\langle S{\to}\bullet\rangle,L^{\prime})

we have:

\textit{num}(L^{\prime})=\sum_{\overline{e}\in B(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell}}\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime}\langle S{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)

and
0\leqslant\textit{num}(L^{\prime})\leqslant|B(I_{L^{\prime}})|^{\ell}

and
\textit{num}(L^{\prime})
is exactly the number of tuples

\overline{e}\in B(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell}
such that

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle}\models\chi
.

Proof of Claim 1:
Since
L^{\prime}
satisfies the lemma’s assumptions for
\tilde{\chi}
,
it has
real guards w.r.t.
\tilde{g}
and therefore satisfies

\Delta_{\tilde{g}}
.
Thus,
\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\tilde{\chi}

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\tilde{\psi}
.

Furthermore,
\textup{db}_{Q}=\textit{ifree}_{B}(\chi)\supseteq S
.
Lemma
8.15
yields:

\textit{num}(L^{\prime})\ =\ \sum_{\overline{e}\in B(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell}}\!\!\!\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime}\langle S{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)\,.

Consider an arbitrary
\overline{e}
with

\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime}\langle S{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)>0
.
Since
Q
has real guards, the
k
-labeled incidence graph

L^{\prime}\langle S{\to}\overline{e}\rangle
satisfies the lemma’s assumptions
w.r.t. the formula
\chi
(in particular, it has real guards w.r.t.
g_{Q}=g
). Therefore, by the induction hypothesis we obtain that

\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime}\langle S{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)=1
and

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}\langle S{\to}\overline{e}\rangle}\models\chi
.

Hence,

0\leqslant\textit{num}(L^{\prime})\leqslant|B(I_{L^{\prime}})|^{\ell}\leqslant m^{\ell}
.
Moreover,
\textit{num}(L^{\prime})
is exactly the number of tuples

\overline{e}\in B(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell}
such that

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}\rangle}\models\chi
.
This completes the proof of Claim 1.
∎
{}_{\textit{Claim 1}}

For the remaining parts of the proof, our aim is to

\begin{array}[]{l}\parbox{341.43306pt}{construct a $Q^{\prime}\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}$
that has the parameters
$g_{\tilde{Q}},\textup{db}_{\tilde{Q}},\textup{dr}_{\tilde{Q}}$ required by the
lemma for formula $\tilde{\chi}$,
such that $\operatorname{hom}(Q^{\prime},L^{\prime})=\textit{num}(L^{\prime})$ for all $k$-labeled incidence graphs $L^{\prime}$
satisfying the lemma's assumptions for $\tilde{\chi}$.}\end{array}

(17)

Once having achieved this, we can use
Lemma
8.16
to obtain
a
k
-labeled quantum incidence graph

\tilde{Q}\coloneqq Q^{\prime}[X,Y]\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
for
X\coloneqq\{0,\ldots,n{-}1\}
and
Y\coloneqq\{n,\ldots,m^{\ell}\}
such that

\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{Q},L^{\prime})\in\{0,1\}
and, moreover,

\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{Q},L^{\prime})=1

\iff

\textit{num}(L^{\prime})\geqslant n

\iff

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\models\tilde{\chi}
.

Hence, all that remains to be done to finish the proof is to achieve
(
17
).
We let

Z\ \coloneqq\ \{\,i\in\operatorname{dom}(g)\ :\ \tilde{g}(i)\neq g(i)\text{ \ or \ }g(i)\in S\,\}\,.

(18)

If
Z=\varnothing
, we have
\tilde{g}=g
and
\operatorname{img}(g)\cap S=\varnothing
. Hence,
S\subseteq\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus\operatorname{img}(g)
.
Using part
3
) of
Definition
8.10

we obtain that
Q^{\prime}\coloneqq Q\langle S{\to}\bullet\rangle
is in
\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
.
Note that
Q^{\prime}
has the parameters

g_{\tilde{Q}},\textup{db}_{\tilde{Q}},\textup{dr}_{\tilde{Q}}
required by the
lemma for formula
\tilde{\chi}
.
Furthermore, as stated in Claim 1, we have

\textit{num}(L^{\prime})=\operatorname{hom}(Q^{\prime},L^{\prime})
for all
k
-labeled incidence graphs

L^{\prime}
satisfying the lemma’s assumptions for
\tilde{\chi}
.
Thus, we have achieved (
17
), and therefore
the proof is finished in case that
Z=\varnothing
.

If
Z\neq\varnothing
, we cannot simply choose
Q^{\prime}
as above, because
there is no guarantee that
Q\langle S{\to}\bullet\rangle
belongs to
\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
.
Instead, we proceed as follows.

Let
f
be the partial function with
\operatorname{dom}(f)=Z
and

f(i)\coloneqq\tilde{g}(i)\quad\text{for all $i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)$.}

Claim 2:
1.)
\tilde{g}=f\cup g
.

2.)
f
is a transition for
g
.

3.) All

i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
satisfy:
f(i)\in S
or
f(i)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)
.

Proof of Claim 2:

1.) For all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
we have:
(f\cup g)(i)=f(i)=\tilde{g}(i)
.

For all
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g)\setminus\operatorname{dom}(f)
we have
(f\cup g)(i)=g(i)
. Since
i\not\in\operatorname{dom}(f)=Z
, we have
\tilde{g}(i)=g(i)
.
I.e.,
(f\cup g)=\tilde{g}
.

2.) Consider an arbitrary
i\in\operatorname{dom}(g)
with
g(i)\in\operatorname{img}(f)
. We have to show that
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
, i.e.,
i\in Z
.
Since
g(i)\in\operatorname{img}(f)
, there is a
j\in Z
such that
g(i)=f(j)
.
From (
16
) we know that
\tilde{g}(j)=g(j)
or

\tilde{g}(j)\in S
or

\tilde{g}(j)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)
.
But

\tilde{g}(j)\ =\ (f\cup g)(j)\ =\ f(j)\ =\ g(i)\,,

(19)

hence

\tilde{g}(j)\in\operatorname{img}(g)
.
This leaves us with two remaining cases:
\tilde{g}(j)=g(j)
or
\tilde{g}(j)\in S
.

In case that
\tilde{g}(j)=g(j)
, from the fact that
j\in Z
we obtain (by the definition of
Z
) that
g(j)\in S
.
Hence,
g(j)=\tilde{g}(j)=f(j)=g(i)\in S
.
Thus, by our choice of
Z
we have:
i\in Z=\operatorname{dom}(f)
.

In case that
\tilde{g}(j)\in S
, by (
19
) we have:

\tilde{g}(j)=f(j)=g(i)\in S
. Thus, by our choice of
Z
we have:
i\in Z=\operatorname{dom}(f)
.

Hence, we have shown that
f
is a transition for
g
.

3.) Consider an arbitrary
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
. By definition we have
f(i)=\tilde{g}(i)
.

In case that
\tilde{g}(i)=g(i)
, by definition of
Z
we obtain:
g(i)\in S
.
Hence,
g(i)=\tilde{g}(i)=f(i)\in S
.

In case that
\tilde{g}(i)\neq g(i)
, by (
16
) we know that

\tilde{g}(i)\in S
or

\tilde{g}(i)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)
.
Since
f(i)=\tilde{g}(i)
, we hence have:
f(i)\in S
or

f(i)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)
.

This completes the proof of Claim 2.
∎
{}_{\textit{Claim 2}}

Let
D_{1}\coloneqq(S\cap\operatorname{img}(f))\setminus\operatorname{img}(g)
,
let
D_{2}\coloneqq S\setminus\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})
, and
let
D_{3}\coloneqq\operatorname{img}(g)\cap\operatorname{img}(f)
.

Claim 3:

D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}
are pairwise disjoint, and
S=\bigcup_{i\in[3]}D_{i}
.

Proof of Claim 3:
We first show that
D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}
are pairwise disjoint:

D_{1}\cap D_{2}=\varnothing
because

D_{1}\subseteq\operatorname{img}(f)\subseteq\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})
.

D_{1}\cap D_{3}=\varnothing
because
D_{3}\subseteq\operatorname{img}(g)
.

D_{2}\cap D_{3}=\varnothing
because
D_{3}\subseteq\operatorname{img}(f)\subseteq\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})
.

We next show that
\bigcup_{i\in[3]}D_{i}\subseteq S
:
By definition,
D_{i}\subseteq S
for each
i\in[2]
.
For showing that
D_{3}\subseteq S
consider an arbitrary
j\in D_{3}=\operatorname{img}(g)\cap\operatorname{img}(f)
.
Since
j\in\operatorname{img}(f)
, there is an
i\in\operatorname{dom}(f)=Z
such that
j=f(i)
.
From Claim 2, part 3.) we obtain that
f(i)\in S
or

f(i)\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)
. Since
f(i)=j\in\operatorname{img}(g)
, we hence have

j\in S
.

Finally, we show that
S\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in[3]}D_{i}
:
Consider an arbitrary
j\in S
.
If
j\in D_{2}
, we are done.
If
j\not\in D_{2}
, then
j\in\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})
.
Hence, there is an
i\in\operatorname{dom}(\tilde{g})
such that
j=\tilde{g}(i)
.
We obtain:
i\in Z
(the reasoning is as follows: If
g(i)\neq\tilde{g}(i)
, then
i\in Z
by (
18
). Otherwise,

g(i)=\tilde{g}(i)=j\in S
, and by (
18
) we obtain that

i\in Z
).
Hence,
i\in Z=\operatorname{dom}(f)
. Thus
j=f(i)\in\operatorname{img}(f)
.
If
j\in\operatorname{img}(g)
then
j\in D_{3}
. Otherwise,
j\not\in\operatorname{img}(g)
, and
hence
j\in D_{1}
.
∎
{}_{\textit{Claim~3}}

We let
Q_{1}\coloneqq Q\langle D_{1}{\to}\bullet\rangle
and

Q_{2}\coloneqq Q_{1}[{\leadsto}f]
and

Q^{\prime}\coloneqq Q_{2}\langle D_{2}{\to}\bullet\rangle
.

Claim 4:

Q_{1},Q_{2},Q^{\prime}\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
and

g_{Q_{1}}=g
,
\textup{db}_{Q_{1}}=(\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus D_{1})
and

g_{Q^{\prime}}=\tilde{g}
,

\textup{dr}_{Q^{\prime}}=\textit{ifree}_{R}(\tilde{\chi})
,
\textup{db}_{Q^{\prime}}=\textit{ifree}_{B}(\tilde{\chi})
.

Proof of Claim 4:
Since
Q\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
with
g_{Q}=g
and
S\subseteq\textup{db}_{Q}
, we have

D_{1}\subseteq\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus\operatorname{img}(g_{Q})
. Hence
Q_{1}=Q\langle D_{1}{\to}\bullet\rangle\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
.
Note that
g_{Q_{1}}=g
and
\textup{db}_{Q_{1}}=\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus D_{1}
.

By Claim 2,
f
is a transition for
g
; hence we have

Q_{2}=Q_{1}[{\leadsto}f]\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
.
Note that
g_{Q_{2}}=f\cup g=\tilde{g}
and

\textup{db}_{Q_{2}}=\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})\cup(\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus D_{1})
.
Note that
(\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus D_{1})\supseteq(S\setminus D_{1})=D_{2}\cup D_{3}

(by Claim 3). Hence,
\textup{db}_{Q_{2}}\supseteq D_{2}
. Since

D_{2}=S\setminus\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})
we have
D_{2}\subseteq\textup{db}_{Q_{2}}\setminus\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})
.
Hence,
Q^{\prime}=Q_{2}\langle D_{2}{\to}\bullet\rangle\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
.
Note that
g_{Q^{\prime}}=\tilde{g}
and

\textup{dr}_{Q^{\prime}}=\textup{dr}_{Q}=\textit{ifree}_{R}(\chi)=\textit{ifree}_{R}(\tilde{\chi})
.

To complete the proof of Claim 4, we show that

\textup{db}_{Q^{\prime}}=\textit{ifree}_{B}(\tilde{\chi})
.
Note that
\textit{ifree}_{B}(\tilde{\chi})=\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})\cup(\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus S)
.
And
\textup{db}_{Q^{\prime}}=(\textup{db}_{Q_{2}}\setminus D_{2})=((\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})\cup(\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus D_{1}))\setminus D_{2})=(\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})\setminus D_{2})\cup(\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus(D_{1}\cup D_{2}))

(for the latter, recall from Claim 3 that
D_{1}\cap D_{2}=\varnothing
).
Since
D_{2}\cap\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})=\varnothing
we can simplify even further and get
\textup{db}_{Q^{\prime}}=\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})\cup(\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus(D_{1}\cup D_{2}))
.

We first show that
\textup{db}_{Q^{\prime}}\subseteq\textit{ifree}_{B}(\tilde{\chi})
:
Consider an arbitrary
j\in\textup{db}_{Q^{\prime}}
. Then,
j\not\in D_{2}
. Furthermore,
j\in\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})
or
j\in(\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus D_{1})
.

If
j\in\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})
, then
j\in\textit{ifree}_{B}(\tilde{\chi})
and we are done.
Otherwise,
j\in(\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus D_{1})
. Hence,
j\not\in D_{1}\cup D_{2}
.
From Claim 3 we know that
S=D_{1}\cup D_{2}\cup D_{3}
.

In case that
j\not\in D_{3}
we have:
j\not\in S
, and hence

j\in(\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus S)
. Thus,
j\in\textit{ifree}_{B}(\tilde{\chi})
.

In case that
j\in D_{3}
we have:
j\in D_{3}\subseteq\operatorname{img}(f)\subseteq\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})
, and hence

j\in\textit{ifree}_{B}(\tilde{\chi})
.

This completes the proof that
\textup{db}_{Q^{\prime}}\subseteq\textit{ifree}_{B}(\tilde{\chi})
.

Next, we show that
\textit{ifree}_{B}(\tilde{\chi})\subseteq\textup{db}_{Q^{\prime}}
:
Consider an arbitrary
j\in\textit{ifree}_{B}(\tilde{\chi})
.
Then,
j\in\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})
or
j\in(\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus S)
.
Recall from Claim 3 that
S
is the union of the pairwise disjoint
sets
D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}
.

Case 1:

j\in(\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus S)
. Then,
j\in\textup{db}_{Q}
and

j\not\in D_{1}\cup D_{2}\cup D_{3}
. In particular,
j\in(\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus(D_{1}\cup D_{2}))\subseteq\textup{db}_{Q^{\prime}}
.

Case 2:

j\not\in(\textup{db}_{Q}\setminus S)
. Then,

j\in\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})

and we are done since
\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})\subseteq\textup{db}_{Q^{\prime}}
.

This completes the proof of Claim 4.
∎
{}_{\textit{Claim 4}}

According to Claim 4,
Q^{\prime}\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
and
Q^{\prime}
has the desired
parameters
g_{Q^{\prime}},\textup{db}_{Q^{\prime}},\textup{dr}_{Q^{\prime}}
.
The next claim ensures that
Q^{\prime}
indeed satisfies what we aimed for in
(
17
).

Claim 5:

For every
k
-labeled incidence graph
L^{\prime}
that satisfies
the lemma’s assumptions for
\tilde{\chi}
we have:

\operatorname{hom}(Q^{\prime},L^{\prime})\ =\ \textit{num}(L^{\prime}).

Proof of Claim 5:
Let
L^{\prime}
be an arbitrary
k
-labeled incidence graph
L^{\prime}
that satisfies
the lemma’s assumptions for
\tilde{\chi}
(in particular,
L^{\prime}
has
real guards w.r.t.
\tilde{g}=f\cup g
).
For
i\in[3]
let
\ell_{i}\coloneqq|D_{i}|
.
From Lemma
8.15
we obtain:

\operatorname{hom}(Q^{\prime},L^{\prime})=\operatorname{hom}(Q_{2}\langle D_{2}{\to}\bullet\rangle,L^{\prime})=

\sum_{\overline{e}_{2}\in B(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell_{2}}}\operatorname{hom}(Q_{2},L^{\prime}\langle D_{2}{\to}\overline{e}_{2}\rangle)\,.

Furthermore, for every
\overline{e}_{2}\in B(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell_{2}}
and for

L^{\prime}_{\overline{e}_{2}}\coloneqq L^{\prime}\langle D_{2}{\to}\overline{e}_{2}\rangle

we have:

\operatorname{hom}(Q_{2},L^{\prime}_{\overline{e}_{2}})=\operatorname{hom}(Q_{1}[{\leadsto}f],L^{\prime}_{\overline{e}_{2}})=

\operatorname{hom}(M_{f},L^{\prime}_{\overline{e}_{2}})\ \cdot\!\!\sum_{\overline{e}_{3}\in B(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell_{3}}}\operatorname{hom}(Q_{1},L^{\prime}_{\overline{e}_{2}}\langle{X_{b}}{\to}\overline{e}_{3}\rangle)

where

{X_{b}}\coloneqq\operatorname{img}(g_{Q_{1}})\cap\operatorname{img}(f)\cap\textup{db}_{Q_{1}}=D_{3}
(the latter
follows from Claims 3 and 4).

Since
L^{\prime}
has real guards w.r.t.
\tilde{g}=f\cup g
, it also has
real guards w.r.t.
f
.
Note that
L^{\prime}_{\overline{e}_{2}}
differs from
L^{\prime}
only w.r.t. the
blue labels in
D_{2}=S\setminus\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})
; none of these labels
belongs to
\operatorname{img}(f)
(because
\operatorname{img}(f)\subseteq\operatorname{img}(\tilde{g})
).
Hence, also
L^{\prime}_{\overline{e}_{2}}
has real
guards w.r.t.
f
. This implies that

\operatorname{hom}(M_{f},L^{\prime}_{\overline{e}_{1}})=1
.

In summary, we obtain that
\operatorname{hom}(Q^{\prime},L^{\prime})=

\sum_{\overline{e}_{2}\in B(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell_{2}}}\ \sum_{\overline{e}_{3}\in B(L^{\prime})^{\ell_{3}}}\operatorname{hom}(Q_{1},L^{\prime}_{\overline{e}_{2}}\langle D_{3}{\to}\overline{e}_{3}\rangle)\,.

Since
D_{2},D_{3}
are disjoint, we obtain:

\operatorname{hom}(Q^{\prime},L^{\prime})=

\sum_{\overline{e}\in B(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}}}\ \operatorname{hom}(Q_{1},L^{\prime}\langle(D_{2}\cup D_{3}){\to}\overline{e}\rangle)\,.

Recall that
Q_{1}=Q\langle D_{1}{\to}\bullet\rangle
. Hence,
a further application of Lemma
8.15
yields:

\operatorname{hom}(Q^{\prime},L^{\prime})=

\sum_{\overline{e}\in B(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}}}\ \ \sum_{\overline{e}_{1}\in B(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell_{1}}}\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime}\langle(D_{2}\cup D_{3}){\to}\overline{e}\rangle\langle D_{1}{\to}\overline{e}_{1}\rangle)\,.

Note that
S
is the union of the disjoint sets
D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}
and

\ell=|S|=\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}
. Hence,
\operatorname{hom}(Q^{\prime},L^{\prime})=

\sum_{\overline{e}\in B(I_{L^{\prime}})^{\ell}}\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime}\langle S{\to}\overline{e}\rangle)\quad=\quad\textit{num}(L^{\prime})

(the last equality is provided by Claim 1).
Hence, the proof of Claim 5 is complete.
∎
{}_{\textit{Claim 5}}

From Claims 4 and 5 we obtain that we have achieved
(
17
).
This completes Case 7, and it completes the proof of Lemma
9.2
.∎

E.3
Detailed proof of Theorem
6.1

Proof of Theorem
6.1
:
.

Let
I
and
I^{\prime}
be arbitrary incidence graphs, and let

k\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
. Our aim is to prove the following:

I\not\equiv_{\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}}I^{\prime}\ \ \iff\ \ \textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IEHW}_{k}}(I)\neq\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IEHW}_{k}}(I^{\prime})\,.\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt

Case 1:

|B(I)|\neq|B(I^{\prime})|
.

Let
J
be the incidence graph with exactly
1

blue node, no red node, and no edge. Clearly,

J\in\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
. Furthermore,

\operatorname{hom}(J,I)=|B(I)|
and
\operatorname{hom}(J,I^{\prime})=|B(I^{\prime})|
.
As
|B(I)|\neq|B(I^{\prime})|
, we obtain that

\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IEHW}_{k}}(I)\neq\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IEHW}_{k}}(I^{\prime})
.

Let
m\coloneqq|B(I)|
and consider the sentence
\psi\coloneqq\exists^{\scalebox{0.6}{$=$}m}(\texttt{{e}}_{1}).\texttt{{e}}_{1}{=}\texttt{{e}}_{1}
in
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
. Then,
I\models\psi
and

I^{\prime}\not\models\psi
.
Hence,
I\not\equiv_{\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}}I^{\prime}
.
Thus, in Case 1 we are done.

Case 2:

|B(I)|=|B(I^{\prime})|
.

For “
\Longrightarrow
” let
\varphi
be a sentence
in
\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
such that
I\models\varphi
and
I^{\prime}\not\models\varphi
.
Let
\chi\coloneqq(\top\land\psi)\in\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}
be given according to Theorem
7.2
, i.e. it holds that
(\top\land\varphi)\equiv(\top\land\psi)=\chi
.

Let
m\coloneqq|B(I)|=|B(I^{\prime})|
, and
choose an arbitrary
d\in\mathbb{N}
such
that
d\geqslant|N_{I}(e)|
for all
e\in B(I)
and
d\geqslant|N_{I^{\prime}}(e)|
for
all
e\in B(I^{\prime})
.

Let
Q\coloneqq{Q}_{\chi,m,d}\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
be the
k
-labeled quantum
incidence graph provided by Lemma
9.2
.
Note that
Q
has guard function
g_{\varnothing}
, and

\textup{db}_{Q}=\textup{dr}_{Q}=\varnothing
.
Let
b_{\varnothing}
and
r_{\varnothing}
the labeling functions with empty
domain, and let

L\coloneqq(I,r_{\varnothing},b_{\varnothing},g_{\varnothing})
and

L^{\prime}\coloneqq(I^{\prime},r_{\varnothing},b_{\varnothing},g_{\varnothing})
.
They both have real guards w.r.t.
g_{\varnothing}
(simply because

g_{\varnothing}
has empty domain).
From Lemma
9.2
we know that

\operatorname{hom}(Q,L)=1
(because
I\models\chi
) and

\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime})=0
(because
I^{\prime}\not\models\chi
).
Thus,
\operatorname{hom}(Q,L)\neq\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime})
.
Since
Q\in\textsl{QGLI}_{k}
, there are a degree
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geqslant 1}
and
coefficients

\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}
and components

L_{1},\ldots,L_{\ell}\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
such that
Q=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\alpha_{i}L_{i}
. We have:

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\alpha_{i}\operatorname{hom}(L_{i},L)=\operatorname{hom}(Q,L)\neq\operatorname{hom}(Q,L^{\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\alpha_{i}\operatorname{hom}(L_{i},L^{\prime})
.
Hence there must exist an
i\in[\ell]
with
\alpha_{i}\neq 0
and

\operatorname{hom}(L_{i},L)\neq\operatorname{hom}(L_{i},L^{\prime})
.

We know that
L_{i}\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
,
and
L_{i}=(I_{i},r_{i},b_{i},g_{i})
has
parameters
\textup{dr}_{Q}=\textup{db}_{Q}=\varnothing
and guard function

g_{i}=g_{\varnothing}
.
I.e.,
\operatorname{dom}(r_{i})=\operatorname{dom}(b_{i})=\varnothing
.
From Theorem
8.12
1
we know that

I_{i}\in\textsl{IEHW}_{k}
. Thus,
I_{i}
witnesses that

\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IEHW}_{k}}(I)\neq\textsf{{HOM}}_{\textsl{IEHW}_{k}}(I^{\prime})
.

For “
\Longleftarrow
” let
J
be an incidence graph in
\textsl{IEHW}_{k}

such that
\operatorname{hom}(J,I)\neq\operatorname{hom}(J,I^{\prime})
.
Let
m\coloneqq\operatorname{hom}(J,I)
.
From Theorem
8.12
2

we obtain a label-free
\tilde{L}\in\textsl{GLI}_{k}
such that

I_{\tilde{L}}\cong J
.

Clearly, for the label-free
k
-labeled incidence graphs
L
and

L^{\prime}
with
I_{L}=I
and
I_{L^{\prime}}=I^{\prime}
we have

\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{L},L)=\operatorname{hom}(J,I)=m
and

\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{L},L^{\prime})=\operatorname{hom}(J,I^{\prime})\neq m
.

From Lemma
9.1
we obtain a sentence

\chi\coloneqq\varphi_{\tilde{L},m}
in
\textsf{{RGC}}^{{k}}\subseteq\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}
such that

\mathcal{I}_{L}\models\chi
(because
\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{L},L)=m
) and

\mathcal{I}_{L^{\prime}}\not\models\chi
(because

\operatorname{hom}(\tilde{L},L^{\prime})\neq m
).
Thus,
I\models\chi
and
I^{\prime}\not\models\chi
. I.e.,
\chi
witnesses
that
I\not\equiv_{\textsf{{GC}}^{{k}}}I^{\prime}
.

This completes the proof of Theorem
6.1
.
∎
